Kontorovich-Lebedev-Fourier Space for de Sitter Correlators
Nathan Belrhali K, Arthur Poisson K, Sébastien Renaux-Petel K, Denis Werth K,L,F

Abstract
In this work, we build a novel frequency-momentum space for ( d + 1 ) (d+1) -dimensional de Sitter (dS) correlators from first principles. This construction follows directly from the decomposition into unitary irreducible representations (UIRs) of the spacetime isometry group SO ⁡ ( 1 , d + 1 ) \mathrm{SO}(1,d+1) . While the spatial momentum space is given by the standard d d -dimensional Fourier transform, the frequency space arises from diagonalising the quadratic Casimir operator, leading to the ( d + 1 ) (d+1) -dimensional Kontorovich-Lebedev-Fourier (KLF) transform. We show that square-integrable functions decompose only along the principal series, whereas more general functions can receive discrete contributions from other UIRs. Applying this framework to the bulk CFT two-point function reproduces its Källén-Lehmann representation. Using the path integral formulation, we derive the Feynman rules for in-in perturbation theory in KLF space, leading to the introduction of KLF-space correlators, which are simply related to late-time correlation functions through a reduction formula. Furthermore, the KLF-space formulation sheds light on the simple mathematical structure of perturbative computations. In particular, the propagators take the form of simple rational functions, and tree-level diagrams can be written as spectral integrals over known meromorphic functions, as demonstrated in the example of the single-exchange four-point function. At the loop level, we show, through the example of the self-energy correction to the scalar propagator, that the group-theoretical nature of the construction allows the momentum integral to be recast as an orthogonality relation among SO ⁡ ( 1 , d + 1 ) \mathrm{SO}(1,d+1) Clebsch-Gordan coefficients.



1
Introduction

Theoretical physics is often about properly understanding kinematics. For instance, scattering amplitudes in Minkowski spacetime quantum field theory (QFT) are better understood when expressed in terms of energy and momentum variables rather than in terms of space and time. Since it allows one to take advantage of the spacetime translation invariance, the theoretical building blocks, such as the Feynman propagator, take a very simple form, making perturbative computations straightforward to organise. It is also the natural language to reveal non-perturbative constraints from causality and unitarity
Froissart:1961ux
;
Martin:1962rt
;
Bros:1965kbd
;
Martin:1965jj
;
Paulos:2017fhb
;
Bellazzini:2020cot
, as they often arise in the complex plane of Mandelstam variables (see
Correia:2020xtr
;
Kruczenski:2022lot
for modern reviews).
Another example is provided by the revival of the conformal bootstrap
Rattazzi:2008pe
;
Rychkov:2009ij
;
Caracciolo:2009bx
;
Rattazzi:2010gj
, in which a thorough understanding of the kinematic building blocks, such as conformal blocks and partial waves
Dolan:2000ut
;
Dolan:2003hv
;
Costa:2011dw
, is essential. The latter play a great role in the non-perturbative understanding of QFT in Anti-de Sitter (AdS) spacetime, where the boundary correlation functions satisfy the same axioms as those of a lower dimensional Lorentzian conformal field theory (CFT)
Callan:1989em
;
Paulos:2016fap
;
Carmi:2018qzm
.

The success of these two descriptions of QFT defined on a given spacetime shares a common origin, which lies in the high degree of symmetry of the background. More precisely, all the features mentioned above arise when expanding either the fields in flat spacetime, or the boundary correlators in AdS, in a harmonic basis appropriate to the spacetime symmetry group.
1
1

1

See e.g.
Mack:1974jjo
;
Mack:1974sa
;
Dobrev:1975ru
;
Dobrev:1977qv
for harmonic analysis on the conformal group and
Dolan:2003hv
;
Hogervorst:2017sfd
;
Simmons-Duffin:2017nub
;
Karateev:2018oml
for more recent work.
Unfortunately, we do not live in any of these spacetimes. The relevant background for cosmological applications is rather approached by de Sitter (dS) spacetime, which describes an expanding Universe with constant expansion rate. As such, it can describe both the early inflationary Universe and its late-time accelerated expansion.

Although dS is also a maximally symmetric space, we still lack a proper understanding of its kinematics. While the isometry group also acts on the boundary correlators as the conformal group in one lower dimension, the scaling dimensions of the operators involved in the harmonic decomposition do not always fall into its unitary irreducible representations (UIR),
which prevents a convergent operator product expansion (OPE)
Hogervorst:2021uvp
;
DiPietro:2021sjt
;
SalehiVaziri:2024joi
.
2
2

2

This statement is due to the absence of a bulk state-boundary operator correspondence, and ultimately to the space-like nature of the dS boundary. Instead, see e.g.
Paulos:2016fap
;
SalehiVaziri:2024joi
for the statement of this correspondence in AdS.
Therefore, although conformal invariance can still be used to bootstrap late-time observables at the perturbative level by mean of boundary differential equations
Maldacena:2011nz
;
Bzowski:2011ab
;
Mata:2012bx
;
Bzowski:2013sza
;
Kundu:2014gxa
;
Kundu:2015xta
;
Ghosh:2014kba
;
Shukla:2016bnu
;
Arkani-Hamed:2018kmz
;
Baumann:2019oyu
;
Baumann:2020dch
;
Pimentel:2022fsc
;
Jazayeri:2022kjy
;
Qin:2022fbv
;
Qin:2023ejc
;
Aoki:2024uyi
;
Qin:2025xct
, its constraining power at a non-perturbative level is not guaranteed (nevertheless, see
Hogervorst:2021uvp
for a recent attempt).

At a more primitive level, we even lack a proper momentum space to organise perturbative computations, as we do in flat spacetime.
The absence of time translation invariance means that energy is not conserved, rendering the usual Fourier frequency space ineffective.
The traditional method to compute late-time correlation functions is therefore to use the Schwinger-Keldysch (or in-in) formalism
Schwinger:1960qe
;
Feynman:1963fq
;
Keldysh:1964ud
;
Maldacena:2002vr
;
Weinberg:2005vy
in spatial Fourier space and conformal time. In this framework, even the simplest Feynman diagrams require the evaluation of nested time integrals, which quickly become daunting (see e.g.
Qin:2023bjk
;
Qin:2023nhv
;
Xianyu:2023ytd
;
Liu:2024xyi
;
Fan:2025scu
for recent progress). For correlators in the Bunch-Davies vacuum, a common alternative is to perform computations in the Euclidean version of spacetime, which is simply the sphere, and analytically continue the result to dS
Higuchi:1986wu
;
Bros:1990cu
;
Bros:1995js
;
Marolf:2010zp
;
Marolf:2010nz
;
Higuchi:2010xt
. In this way, fields and correlators can be expanded in the
(d+1)
-dimensional spherical harmonics basis, providing a dS momentum space. However, although this has proved useful to evaluate IR-finite loop corrections to
propagators
Marolf:2010nz
;
Marolf:2010zp
;
Chen:2016hrz
;
Chakraborty:2023qbp
;
Chakraborty:2023eoq
;
Chakraborty:2025myb
;
Loparco:2025azm
, the evaluation of higher-point correlation functions appears impossible in that context.

Due to this issue, one can instead make use of another Wick rotation that maps the Poincaré patch of dS to the one of Euclidean Anti-de Sitter (EAdS)
Sleight:2019mgd
;
Sleight:2019hfp
;
Sleight:2020obc
;
Sleight:2021plv
;
DiPietro:2021sjt
;
Loparco:2023rug
. A convenient approach is then to take maximal advantage of the scale invariance in the bulk by diagonalising the dilatation generator, resulting in the Mellin-Barnes (MB) formalism
Sleight:2019mgd
;
Sleight:2019hfp
;
Sleight:2020obc
;
Sleight:2021plv
. However, like for radial quantisation in CFT, this is incompatible with staying in spatial Fourier space, because dilatation and translations do not commute.
The corresponding “CFT-basis”
\ket{\mu,\bm{x}}
is expressed in terms of the scaling dimension
\Delta=\frac{d}{2}+i\mu
and a spatial point
\bm{x}
on the conformal boundary

Sun:2021thf
. Thus, it is especially convenient when working in the embedding-space formalism where
\bm{x}
can be identified with a point on the projective null cone
Dirac:1936fq
;
Bros:2001yw
;
Cacciatori:2007in
;
Costa:2011dw
;
Rychkov:2016iqz
;
Karateev:2017jgd
;
Baumann:2019oyu
;
Moschella:2024kvk
;
SalehiVaziri:2024joi
.

In this work, we formulate QFT in dS by using an alternative basis
\ket{\mu,\bm{k}}
where the spatial translation generators are diagonalised together with the Casimir operator, paralleling the Wigner classification of the Poincaré group
Bargmann:1948ck
. In this picture, the Mellin transform is traded for the Kontorovich-Lebedev transform, resulting in the introduction of what we call the Kontorovich-Lebedev-Fourier (KLF) space, labeled by a frequency
\mu
and a spatial momentum
\bm{k}
.

Let us highlight some features of our construction:

•

The building blocks are the harmonic functions
\Phi_{\bm{k}}^{\mu}(z,\bm{x})
(
42
), which provide the overlap between momentum states and their position-space duals, in particular between the Euclidean time
z
and the frequency
\mu
.

•

The principal series has a privileged status: only the harmonic functions with real
\mu
enter the Kontorovitch-Lebedev transform and its inverse:

\displaystyle f(z,\bm{x})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathrm{KLF}}\mathrm{d}\mu\,\mathcal{N}_{\mu}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{d}\bm{k}}{(2\pi)^{d}}\,\Phi^{\mu}_{\bm{k}}(z,\bm{x})f^{\mu}_{\bm{k}}\,,

\displaystyle f^{\mu}_{\bm{k}}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathrm{EAdS}}\frac{\mathrm{d}z\mathrm{d}^{d}\bm{x}}{(Hz)^{d+1}}\left[\Phi^{\mu}_{\bm{k}}(z,\bm{x})\right]^{*}f(z,\bm{x})\,.

They are enough to decompose square-integrable functions, from which more general results can be simply deduced by analytical continuation to complex
\mu
. In particular, the contributions from other UIRs can be read from the non-analyticities in
the complex
\mu
-plane of the KLF modes
f^{\mu}_{\bm{k}}
, see Sec.
3.4
.

•

Like in Minkowski momentum space, the KLF version of the time-ordered Feynman propagator (
121
) has a simple rational form with poles located at the on-shell mass of the propagating degree of freedom.

•

The KLF Wightman function of a local operator
\mathcal{O}
reduces to the corresponding Källén-Lehmann spectral density:
\mathcal{G}^{\mathcal{O}}_{\pm}(\mu)=\rho_{\mathcal{O}}(\mu)/\mathcal{N}_{\mu}
.

•

The computation of correlation functions can be systematically organised using KLF Feynman rules, giving rise to spectral integrals over frequencies, with key ingredients the meromorphic vertex functions
\mathcal{I}^{\mu_{1}\ldots\mu_{n}}_{k_{1}\ldots k_{n}}
(
138
).

•

A reduction formula (
151
) straightforwardly gives cosmological correlators in terms of KLF amputated diagrams. Like in Minkowski, these building blocks do not involve time, but only frequencies and momenta.

•

The introduction of dS
3\mu
symbols (
189
) together with group-theoretical arguments enables to easily compute the loop momentum integral of the self-energy correction to the scalar propagator.

Outline.

The outline of the paper is as follows: In Sec.
2
, we review de Sitter geometry and the representation theory of its isometry group. Sec.
3
is devoted to the construction of the KLF momentum space, paying attention to the role of non-principal series. Sec.
4
describes the perturbative formulation of QFT in KLF space, and Sec.
5
puts this into practice with the computations of cosmological correlators and loop corrections to propagators. We state our conclusions and discuss future directions in Sec.
6
. We collect important technical details in a collection of appendices.

2
De Sitter Space

In this section, we set up our notations and review de Sitter geometry and the representation theory of its isometry group.

2.1
De Sitter Geometry

De Sitter spacetime in
d+1
dimensions, denoted
\mathrm{dS}_{d+1}
, can be realised as a hyperboloid embedded in
(d+2)
-dimensional Minkowski spacetime
\mathbb{M}^{1,d+1}
. It is defined as the set of points at a fixed Lorentzian distance from the origin

\eta_{AB}X^{A}X^{B}=-(X^{0})^{2}+(X^{1})^{2}+\ldots+(X^{d+1})^{2}=R_{\mathrm{dS}}^{2}\,,

(1)

with
A,B=0,1,\ldots,d+1
. Here,
R_{\mathrm{dS}}\equiv H^{-1}
is the curvature radius, equal to the inverse of the constant Hubble parameter. The spacetime
\mathrm{dS}_{d+1}
satisfies Einstein’s equations in vacuum with a positive cosmological constant given by
\Lambda\equiv\tfrac{1}{2}d(d-1)H^{2}
. We now introduce several coordinate systems that can be used to parametrise this geometry.

Global coordinates.

The most natural coordinates are global coordinates. As the name suggests, they cover the entire
\mathrm{dS}_{d+1}
spacetime. These coordinates are obtained by analytically continuing the standard
(d+1)
-dimensional spherical coordinates, allowing the azimuthal angle to take imaginary values:

X^{0}=R_{\mathrm{dS}}\sinh(\rho)\,,\quad X^{a}=R_{\mathrm{dS}}\cosh(\rho)\,\omega^{a}\,,

(2)

where
\rho\in\mathbb{R}
is the global time,
a=1,\ldots,d+1
and
\omega^{a}\in S^{d}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d+1}
is a unit vector (
\omega^{a}\omega_{a}=1
) spanning a
d
-dimensional sphere. The latter can be parametrised as

\displaystyle\omega^{1}

\displaystyle=\cos\theta_{1}\,,

(3)

\displaystyle\omega^{2}

\displaystyle=\sin\theta_{1}\,\cos\theta_{2}\,,

\displaystyle\vdots

\displaystyle\omega^{d}

\displaystyle=\sin\theta_{1}\ldots\sin\theta_{d-1}\,\sin\theta_{d}\,,

\displaystyle\omega^{d+1}

\displaystyle=\sin\theta_{1}\ldots\sin\theta_{d-1}\,\sin\theta_{d}\,,

with
0\leq\theta_{i}<\pi
for
1\leq i\leq d-1
and
0\leq\theta_{d}<2\pi
, which yields the standard line element of the unit
d
-dimensional sphere
S^{d}
:

\mathrm{d}\Omega_{d}^{2}\equiv\sum_{a=1}^{d+1}(\mathrm{d}\omega^{a})^{2}=\mathrm{d}\theta_{1}^{2}+\sin^{2}\theta_{1}\mathrm{d}\theta_{2}^{2}+\ldots+\sin^{2}\theta_{1}\ldots\sin^{2}\theta_{d-1}\mathrm{d}\theta_{d}^{2}\,.

(4)

The induced
\mathrm{dS}_{d+1}
metric in global coordinates is therefore given by

\mathrm{d}s^{2}=R_{\mathrm{dS}}^{2}(-\mathrm{d}\rho^{2}+\cosh^{2}\rho\,\mathrm{d}\Omega_{d}^{2})\,.

(5)

Notice that we recover the
(d+1)
-dimensional sphere
S^{d+1}
metric by setting
\rho=i(\theta-\tfrac{\pi}{2})
with
\theta\in[0,\pi)
. At the level of embedding space, de Sitter and the sphere are related by the Wick rotation
X^{0}\to iX^{0}
. As illustrated in Fig.
1
, the metric (
5
) describes a foliation of
d
-dimensional spheres that begin with infinite radius at
\rho=-\infty
, contract to a minimal radius
R_{\mathrm{dS}}
at
\rho=0
, where it coincides with the equilateral plane of the Euclidean sphere, and expand again to infinite size at
\rho=+\infty
. From Eq. (
2
), it follows that the global coordinates correspond to a
1+(d+1)
decomposition of the embedding coordinates
X^{A}
.

Conformal coordinates.

Global time can be compactified via the change of variable
\cosh(\rho)=1/\cos(T)
so that we have
-\pi/2<T<\pi/2
. This leads to the metric

\mathrm{d}s^{2}=R_{\mathrm{dS}}^{2}\,\frac{-\mathrm{d}T^{2}+\mathrm{d}\Omega_{d}^{2}}{\cos^{2}(T)}\,,

(6)

which makes manifest that global
\mathrm{dS}_{d+1}
is conformal to a compact cylinder. The form (
6
) is particularly useful for understanding the causal structure of de Sitter spacetime, as illustrated by the corresponding Penrose diagram in Fig.
1
. In particular, no single observer can access the entire spacetime.

Figure 1
:
Left
: Illustration of
(d+1)
-dimensional de Sitter spacetime
\mathrm{dS}_{d+1}
, for
d=1
, represented as a hyperboloid in
\mathbb{M}^{1,d+1}
. The Euclidean
(d+1)
-dimensional sphere with radius
R_{\mathrm{dS}}
is also shown in
gray
for comparison. The
global coordinates
cover the entire spacetime and correspond to a foliation by
d
-dimensional spheres
S^{d}
, whereas the
Poincaré coordinates
describe an expanding patch covering only half of the spacetime.
Right
: Penrose diagram of
\mathrm{dS}_{d+1}
in conformal coordinates. The
blue region
represents the Poincaré patch as the causal future of a south pole observer sitting in the asymptotic past.

Poincaré coordinates.

An alternative splitting choice is to adopt the following
(1+d+1)
decomposition, which foliates de Sitter spacetime using flat slices:

X^{0}=R_{\mathrm{dS}}\,\frac{\tau^{2}-\bm{x}^{2}-1}{2\tau}\,,\quad X^{i}=-R_{\mathrm{dS}}\,\frac{x^{i}}{\tau}\,,\quad X^{d+1}=R_{\mathrm{dS}}\,\frac{\bm{x}^{2}-\tau^{2}-1}{2\tau}\,,

(7)

where
i=1,\ldots,d
. The Poincaré coordinates
(\tau,\bm{x})
, with
\tau<0
denoting conformal time and
x^{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
being spatial coordinates, cover only half of de Sitter space, which is therefore not geodesically complete. For concreteness, we focus on the expanding patch defined by
X^{0}+X^{d+1}\geq 0
, as illustrated in Fig.
1
. This region corresponds to the causal future of an observer located at some point in the asymptotic past. The induced metric in these coordinates is

\mathrm{d}s^{2}=\frac{-\mathrm{d}\tau^{2}+\mathrm{d}\bm{x}^{2}}{(H\tau)^{2}}\;.

(8)

This parametrisation is relevant in cosmology since the metric (
8
) corresponds to a FLRW spacetime with a constant expansion rate
H
and a scale factor
a(\tau)=-1/(H\tau)
. Constant-
\tau
spatial hypersurfaces are
d
-dimensional Euclidean planes
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, which are manifestly symmetric under the action of the Euclidean group
\mathbb{E}_{d}\equiv\mathbb{T}_{d}\rtimes\mathrm{SO}(d)
, composed of translations
\mathbb{T}_{d}
and rotations
\mathrm{SO}(d)
in
d
dimensions. Poincaré and global coordinates are related via the map

\tau=\frac{-1}{\sinh(\rho)+\cosh(\rho)\,\omega^{d+1}}\,,\quad x^{i}=\frac{\omega^{i}}{\tanh(\rho)+\omega^{d+1}}\,.

(9)

The Poincaré expanding patch in global coordinates is the region covering
\omega^{d+1}+\tanh(\rho)\geq 0
.

Two-point invariants.

When considering two-point correlation functions, an important quantity is the two-point invariant
\sigma^{\mathrm{dS}}
between the points
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
, defined as:

\sigma^{\mathrm{dS}}=H^{2}X_{1}^{A}X_{2}^{A}\eta_{AB}\,.

(10)

This is related to the so-called chordal distance
d(X_{1},X_{2})
as:

d(X_{1},X_{2})=(X_{1}^{A}-X_{2}^{A})(X_{1}^{B}-X_{2}^{B})\eta_{AB}=\frac{2}{H^{2}}(1-\sigma^{\mathrm{dS}})\;.

(11)

As a result,
\sigma^{\mathrm{dS}}=1
corresponds to two points separated by a null geodesic, while
\sigma^{\mathrm{dS}}>1\;(\textrm{resp.}<1)
corresponds to time-like (resp. space-like) separations. In Poincaré coordinates, one has

\sigma^{\mathrm{dS}}=\frac{\tau_{1}^{2}+\tau^{2}_{2}-|\bm{x}_{12}|^{2}}{2\tau_{1}\tau_{2}}\;,\,\textrm{with}\quad\bm{x}_{12}=\bm{x}_{2}-\bm{x}_{1}\,.

(12)

2.2
Representation Theory of
\mathrm{SO}(1,d+1)

We now wish to formulate quantum field theory on the de Sitter geometry introduced above. The corresponding Hilbert space decomposes as a direct sum of invariant subspaces corresponding to unitary irreducible representations (UIRs) of the de Sitter isometry group. Before turning to specific decompositions of the symmetry generators, let us first review how elementary particles in
\mathrm{dS}_{d+1}
can be organised into UIRs of the de Sitter group in a coordinate-independent manner, see
Thomas1941
;
Newton1950
;
Dixmier1961
;
Hirai1962
;
Takahashi1963
. Further details can be found in the reviews
Basile:2016aen
;
Sun:2021thf
and references therein.

Symmetries of de Sitter.

From its definition (
1
), de Sitter spacetime
\mathrm{dS}_{d+1}
is manifestly invariant under
\mathrm{SO}(1,d+1)
, which is isomorphic to the Euclidean conformal group in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. As such, it has
\tfrac{1}{2}(d+2)(d+1)
Killing vectors. A standard basis for the corresponding Lie algebra is
\{J_{AB}\}
where

J_{AB}\equiv X_{A}\frac{\partial}{\partial X^{B}}-X_{B}\frac{\partial}{\partial X^{A}}\,,

(13)

is the vector
(1/2,1/2)
representation, with
A,B=0,1,\ldots,d+1
. The generators are antisymmetric
J_{AB}=-J_{BA}
and verify the following commutation relation

[J_{AB},J_{CD}]=i\,(\eta_{BC}J_{AD}-\eta_{AC}J_{BD}+\eta_{AD}J_{BC}-\eta_{BD}J_{AC})\,,

(14)

which is the
(d+2)
-dimensional Lorentz algebra.

Unitary irreducible representations.

According to Wigner’s theorem
Wigner1948
, particles in
\mathrm{dS}_{d+1}
can be identified as UIRs of the de Sitter group. Since
\mathrm{SO}(1,d+1)
is non-compact, these representations are infinite-dimensional. To determine the Hilbert space, a standard procedure is to identify the largest set of commuting Hermitian operators among the generators and diagonalise them in a common orthogonal basis. As such, the Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
can be decomposed as a direct sum over UIRs

\mathcal{H}=\bigoplus_{\alpha}\mathcal{V}_{(\alpha)}\,,

(15)

where
\alpha
is an abstract (for now unspecified) label running over UIRs of
\mathrm{SO}(1,d+1)
, and
\mathcal{V}_{(\alpha)}
is the corresponding invariant subspace. By Schur’s lemma, any operator
\mathcal{C}
that commutes with all elements of the group must act as a multiple of the identity when restricted to
\mathcal{V}_{(\alpha)}

Tung:1985iqd
;
Georgi:2000vve
;
Isaev:2018xcg
. This operator and its action on any vector
\ket{\alpha}\in\mathcal{V}_{(\alpha)}
therefore takes the form

\mathcal{C}=\bigoplus_{\alpha}M^{2}_{(\alpha)}\mathds{1}^{(\alpha)}\,,\quad\mathcal{C}\ket{\alpha}=M_{(\alpha)}^{2}\ket{\alpha}\,.

(16)

For a Lie group, an example is given by the quadratic Casimir operator, which for
\mathrm{dS}_{d+1}
is given by

\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{SO}(1,d+1)}=-\frac{1}{2}J^{AB}J_{AB}\,.

(17)

Infinite-dimensional representations of
\mathrm{SO}(1,d+1)
are labeled by an
a priori
complex conformal dimension
\Delta
and a representation of the subgroup
\mathrm{SO}(d)
. When restricting to representations realised by scalar fields or traceless-symmetric tensor fields in spacetime, this representation is characterised by an integer spin
S
, with
S=0
corresponding to the trivial representation. The quadratic Casimir eigenvalues are parametrised by
M_{(\alpha)}^{2}=M_{(\Delta,S)}^{2}=\Delta(d-\Delta)+S(S+d-2)
. From now on, we will only consider scalar representations by setting
S=0
. It is then convenient to introduce the parameter
\mu
through
\Delta\equiv\tfrac{d}{2}+i\mu
. With this parametrisation, the associated Casimir eigenvalues become

M_{(\alpha)}^{2}=M_{\mu}^{2}=\mu^{2}+\frac{d^{2}}{4}\,.

(18)

The form (
18
) implies a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
shadow symmetry
\mu\leftrightarrow-\mu
, making manifest the isomorphism between the representations
(\mu)
and
(-\mu)
. As we shall see, the parameter
\mu
is related to the mass
m
of the corresponding bulk scalar field realisation through
(m/H)^{2}=M_{\mu}^{2}
.

To ensure unitarity of the representations of
\mathrm{SO}(1,d+1)
, the generators of the Lie algebra must be represented by Hermitian operators on the Hilbert space, i.e.
J_{AB}^{\dagger}=J_{AB}
. This requirement enforces the Casimir eigenvalues
M_{\mu}^{2}
to be real, implying that the parameter
\mu
must be either real or purely imaginary. Discarding the ever-present trivial representation, the possible scalar UIRs of
\mathrm{SO}(1,d+1)
fall into three categories:

•

The
scalar principal series
\mathcal{P}_{\mu}
:
\mu\in\mathbb{R}
. The bulk theory whose single-particle Hilbert space realises this representation corresponds to a free scalar field with mass that is heavy in Hubble units,
m>\tfrac{d}{2}H
. In the flat-space limit
R_{\mathrm{dS}}\to\infty
, these representations reduce to the familiar massive particle representations of the Poincaré group.

•

The
scalar complementary series
\mathcal{C}_{\mu}
:
\nu\equiv i\mu\in[-\tfrac{d}{2},\tfrac{d}{2}]
. The corresponding bulk fields represent light particles, with
m\leq\tfrac{d}{2}H
. These representations include the massless UIR at their endpoints,
\nu=\pm\tfrac{d}{2}
.

•

The
type-I exceptional series
\mathcal{E}_{\mu}
:
\nu\equiv i\mu=\tfrac{d}{2}+k
with
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
. These representations correspond to shift symmetric bulk scalar fields in
\mathrm{dS}_{d+1}
, see e.g.
Bonifacio:2018zex
, with
k
referred to as the level of the state. The discrete nature of these representations is reminiscent of the phenomenon of partial masslessness in (A)dS
Deser:1983tm
;
Deser:1983mm
;
Higuchi:1986py
;
Brink:2000ag
;
Zinoviev:2001dt
. These are tachyonic states with
m^{2}=-k(k+d)H^{2}
. For example, the
k=1
representation is realised by the galileon
Luty:2003vm
;
Nicolis:2008in
.

These representations are depicted in Fig.
2
.

\text{Re}(\mu)
\text{Im}(\mu)
\mathcal{P}_{\mu}
\mathcal{C}_{\mu}
{\color[rgb]{0.8398,0.1523,0.1563}+i\tfrac{d}{2}}
{\color[rgb]{0.8398,0.1523,0.1563}-i\tfrac{d}{2}}
\mathcal{E}_{\mu}

Figure 2
:
Scalar unitary irreducible representations of
\mathrm{dS}_{d+1}
in the complex
\mu
-plane, illustrating the
scalar principal series
, the scalar
complementary series
and the
type-I exceptional series
. The shadow symmetry appears as a manifest
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
invariance under
\mu\leftrightarrow-\mu
.

Invariant subspace basis.

After charting the Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
into UIRs of the de Sitter group, one must still identify an explicit basis for the invariant subspaces
\mathcal{V}_{\mu}
. A convenient strategy is to select a subgroup
G\subset\mathrm{SO}(1,d+1)
and further decompose
\mathcal{V}_{\mu}
into a direct sum of the UIRs of
G
, as

\mathcal{V}_{\mu}=\bigoplus_{\ell,m,\ldots}\mathcal{V}_{\mu}^{\ell,m,\ldots}\,,

(19)

where the indices
\ell,m,\ldots
, which label UIRs of
G
, play the role of
quantum numbers
. In what follows, we will show that choosing
G
as the isometry group of the spatial hypersurfaces corresponding to the Poincaré slicing naturally leads to a
momentum basis
, conjugate to the position-space variables. This construction provides the de Sitter counterpart of the energy-momentum Fourier space in
(d+1)
-dimensional Minkowski spacetime
\mathbb{M}^{1,d}
.

3
Kontorovich-Lebedev-Fourier Momentum Space

In this section, we describe our approach to construct a natural momentum-space representation of the Hilbert space, dual to position-space states, by exploiting the symmetries of the Poincaré slicing. This construction naturally gives rise to a
Kontorovitch-Lebedev-Fourier
(KLF) transform in
d+1
dimensions, which diagonalises the dynamical and kinematical structure of the bulk de Sitter theory.

We begin by leveraging the invariance of Cauchy slices of constant conformal time under the
d
-dimensional Euclidean group to build a complete, orthonormal basis for the Hilbert space, Sec.
3.1
. Then, using the decomposition of the de Sitter generators into the Euclidean conformal algebra, we solve the corresponding quadratic Casimir differential equation to determine the harmonic function connecting the position and momentum representations, Sec.
3.2
. We describe how (Euclidean) square-integrable functions can be decomposed into harmonic functions of the principal series through the KLF transform, Sec.
3.3
. We then show how more general functions whose spectral decomposition have support on other UIRs can be naturally described in our formalism, Sec.
3.4

3.1
Momentum Space from Symmetry Generators

The isomorphism between the
\mathrm{SO}(1,d+1)
Lie algebra (
14
) and the
d
-dimensional Euclidean conformal algebra is made manifest through the map

D=J_{0,d+1}\,,\quad M_{ij}=J_{ij}\,,\quad P_{i}=J_{d+1,i}+J_{0,i}\,,\quad K_{i}=J_{d+1,i}-J_{0,i}\,,

(20)

where the newly defined generators
D,P_{i},K_{i}
and
M_{ij}=-M_{ji}
, with
i,j=1,\ldots d
, correspond to dilatation, spatial translations, special conformal transformations (sometimes referred to as de Sitter boosts), and rotations, respectively. From Eq. (
14
) and (
20
), it follows that the generators satisfy the commutation relations of the conformal algebra:

\displaystyle=iP_{i}\,,\quad[D,K_{i}]=-iK_{i}\,,[K_{i},P_{j}]=2i\,(\delta_{ij}D-M_{ij})\,,

(21)

\displaystyle[M_{ij},P_{k}]

\displaystyle=i\,(\delta_{jk}P_{i}-\delta_{ik}P_{j})\,,\quad[M_{ij},K_{k}]=i\,(\delta_{jk}K_{i}-\delta_{ik}K_{j})\,,

\displaystyle[M_{ij},M_{k\ell}]

\displaystyle=i\,(\delta_{jk}M_{i\ell}-\delta_{ik}M_{j\ell}+\delta_{i\ell}M_{jk}-\delta_{j\ell}M_{ik})\,,

together with
[P_{i},P_{j}]=[K_{i},K_{j}]=[D,M_{ij}]=0
. Throughout, we adopt the convention that all generators are Hermitian, so that the corresponding group elements are unitary. The quadratic Casimir operator (
17
) is given by

\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{SO}(1,d+1)}=D(D-id)-P_{i}K^{i}-\frac{1}{2}M_{ij}^{2}\,,

(22)

where
-\tfrac{1}{2}M_{ij}^{2}\equiv-\tfrac{1}{2}M_{ij}M^{ij}
is the quadratic Casimir operator of
\mathrm{SO}(d)
. In the Poincaré coordinates, a local bulk scalar operator
\mathcal{O}(\tau,\bm{x})
in de Sitter transforms under the
\mathrm{SO}(1,d+1)
isometries as the following commutators

\displaystyle=\mathcal{D}\mathcal{O}(\tau,\bm{x})\equiv-i\,(\tau\partial_{\tau}+x^{i}\partial_{i})\mathcal{O}(\tau,\bm{x})\,,

(23)

\displaystyle[P_{i},\mathcal{O}(\tau,\bm{x})]

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}_{i}\mathcal{O}(\tau,\bm{x})\equiv-i\,\partial_{i}\mathcal{O}(\tau,\bm{x})\,,

\displaystyle[K_{i},\mathcal{O}(\tau,\bm{x})]

\displaystyle=\mathcal{K}_{i}\mathcal{O}(\tau,\bm{x})\equiv-i\,[2x_{i}\tau\partial_{\tau}+2x_{i}x^{j}\partial_{j}+(\tau^{2}-\bm{x}^{2})\partial_{i}]\mathcal{O}(\tau,\bm{x})\,,

\displaystyle[M_{ij},\mathcal{O}(\tau,\bm{x})]

\displaystyle=\mathcal{M}_{ij}\mathcal{O}(\tau,\bm{x})\equiv-i\,(x_{i}\partial_{j}-x_{j}\partial_{i})\mathcal{O}(\tau,\bm{x})\,,

where
\mathcal{D},\mathcal{P}_{i},\mathcal{K}_{i}
and
\mathcal{M}_{ij}
are the corresponding Killing vector differential operators.

The intrinsic
d
-dimensional nature of the algebra (
21
) is reminiscent of the conformal invariance of the late-time boundary (
\tau\to 0
) of
\mathrm{dS}_{d+1}
. However, the constant-
\tau
Cauchy slices are invariant only under spatial rotations and translations. As announced, we construct our basis by diagonalising the Casimir and the spatial translations, yielding the following momentum basis for the Poincaré-sliced Hilbert space
3
3

3

Since the Casimir operator is unbounded, its eigenstates are typically not part of the Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
. As reviewed in appendix
A
, they can be seen as linear forms acting on elements of
\mathcal{H}
. For simplicity, we ignore these subtleties in the bulk of the paper.

\mathcal{H}\equiv\mathrm{Span}\left(\ket{\mu,\bm{k}}\right)\,,

(24)

where the basis elements
\ket{\mu,\bm{k}}
satisfy the following properties

\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{SO}(1,d+1)}\ket{\mu,\bm{k}}=M_{\mu}^{2}\ket{\mu,\bm{k}}\,,\quad P_{i}\ket{\mu,\bm{k}}=k_{i}\ket{\mu,\bm{k}}\,,

(25)

with
M_{\mu}^{2}
defined in (
18
). They are identified with single-particle states that can be constructed from the Bunch-Davies vacuum
\ket{\Omega}
by acting with the appropriate creation operator:

\ket{\mu,\bm{k}}\equiv a_{\bm{k}}^{(\mu)\dagger}\ket{\Omega}\,.

(26)

Since
\ket{\Omega}
is invariant under
\mathrm{SO}(1,d+1)
, it is annihilated by every
J_{AB}
of the Lie algebra

J_{AB}\ket{\Omega}=0\;.

(27)

The basis states form a complete and orthonormal basis of the Hilbert space

\braket{\mu,\bm{k}|\mu^{\prime},\bm{k}^{\prime}}=\frac{(2\pi)^{d}|\mu|}{\mathcal{N}_{\mu}}\,\delta\left(M_{\mu}^{2}-M_{\mu^{\prime}}^{2}\right)\,\delta^{(d)}(\bm{k}-\bm{k}^{\prime})\,,

(28)

and

\mathds{1}=\int_{\mathcal{S}}\mathrm{d}\mu\mathcal{N}_{{\mu}}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{d}\bm{k}}{(2\pi)^{d}}\ket{\mu,\bm{k}}\bra{\mu,\bm{k}}\,.

(29)

The
\mu
-integration in principle runs over the full spectrum
\mathcal{S}
of the Casimir operator, i.e.
\mathcal{P}_{\mu},\mathcal{C}_{\mu}
and
\mathcal{E}_{\mu}
, the latter for which the integral turns into a discrete sum. Equivalently, one can also see (
28
) as a direct consequence of the canonical commutation relations

[a_{\bm{k}}^{\mu},a_{\bm{k}^{\prime}}^{\mu^{\prime}\dagger}]=\frac{(2\pi)^{d}|\mu|}{\mathcal{N}_{\mu}}\,\delta\left(M_{\mu}^{2}-M_{\mu^{\prime}}^{2}\right)\,\delta^{(d)}(\bm{k}-\bm{k}^{\prime})\,.

(30)

In what follows, it will be enough to compute
\mathcal{N}_{\mu}
for the principal series, see (
54
) below.

Comparison with Minkowski.

Here we comment on a simple conceptual difference between Minkowski and dS that is worth highlighting. For Minkowski, the Casimir of the Poincaré isometry group
\mathbb{R}^{d+1}\rtimes\mathrm{SO}(d+1)
is simply
\mathcal{C}=-P_{\mu}P^{\mu}=-P_{0}P^{0}+P^{i}P^{i}
, where
P^{\mu}
correspond to spacetime translations, which form an abelian subalgebra of the full isometry group. As a result, one can diagonalise at the same time the Casimir, the spatial translations
P_{i}
, and the time-translation operator
P_{0}
, with eigenvalues that are related through
M^{2}=\omega^{2}-\bm{k}^{2}
. There is no such equivalent for the dS Casimir (
22
), whose building blocks do not all commute with one another. Hence, one can diagonalise the Casimir and spatial translations, as we do in our construction, but there is no dispersion relation between the frequency
\mu
and the spatial momentum
\bm{k}
. Saying it otherwise, the de-Sitter mass-shell condition simply states that the Casimir eigenvalue
M_{\mu}
coincides with the mass of a particle of the spectrum.

\ket{\mu,\bm{x}}\equiv e^{-i\bm{P}\cdot\bm{x}}\ket{\mu,\bm{x}=0}

\ket{\mu,\bm{k}}

Casimir Operator
\mathcal{C}

\frac{d^{2}}{4}+\mu^{2}

\frac{d^{2}}{4}+\mu^{2}

Spatial Translation Operator
P_{i}

-i\partial_{i}

k_{i}

Dilatation Operator
D

\frac{d}{2}+i\mu-ix^{j}\partial_{j}

i\left(k_{i}\partial_{k^{i}}+\tfrac{d}{2}\right)

Table 1
:
Action of the Casimir, translations and dilatation operators on the CFT-basis
\ket{\mu,\bm{x}}
and the basis
\ket{\mu,\bm{k}}
used in this paper.

Comparison with the CFT basis.

In Ref.
Hogervorst:2021uvp
, the basis
\ket{\mu,\bm{k}}
was introduced but quickly abandoned for the CFT basis mentioned in the introduction. In the latter, one diagonalises the dilatation, with eigenvalue
\Delta=\frac{d}{2}+i\mu
, and then acts with the translation operator to obtain the CFT position basis
\ket{\mu,\bm{x}}

Sun:2021thf
. This is related to the basis
\ket{\mu,\bm{k}}
by the following tilted Fourier transform
Hogervorst:2021uvp
:

\ket{\mu,\bm{x}}=\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{d}\bm{k}}{(2\pi)^{d}}e^{i\bm{k}\cdot\bm{x}}k^{i\mu}\ket{\mu,\bm{k}}\;,

(31)

and we compare in Table
1
how the Casimir, translations and dilatation operators act on each basis. Note that
\ket{\mu,\bm{x}}
is not a dilatation eigenstate for
\bm{x}\neq 0
.

3.2
Harmonic Functions and Position Space

In order to obtain the transformation properties of the states
\ket{\mu,\bm{k}}
, and to construct the integral transform that connects the KLF momentum space to position space, we define the following wavefuntions:

\Phi^{\mu}_{\bm{k},\mathcal{O}}(\tau,\bm{x})\equiv\braket{\Omega|\mathcal{O}(\tau,\bm{x})|\mu,\bm{k}}\,,

(32)

where
\mathcal{O}(\tau,\bm{x})
is any local operator in the theory. Physically, this object represents the transition amplitude from the Bunch-Davies vacuum of the theory to a specific field configuration at some time
\tau
, given by
\mathcal{O}(\tau,\bm{x})
, projected onto a single-particle state.

Before deriving the explicit expression for
\Phi_{\bm{k},\mathcal{O}}^{\mu}(\tau,\bm{x})
, let us first perform a Wick rotation to Euclidean signature. In order to define the Bunch-Davies vacuum state, we need an appropriate
i\epsilon
-prescription in the infinite past.
4
4

4

The
i\epsilon
-prescription prepares the Hartle-Hawking state of the theory in the far past, as shown in
Higuchi:2010xt
.
Because of this, the time variable we use acquires a non-zero imaginary value and the theory must be studied on the tilted conformal time axes
\tau_{c}=\tau e^{-i{\sf{a}}\epsilon}
, with
{\sf{a}}=\pm 1
, on which the Casimir operator is not self-adjoint. This can be solved by analytically continuing the conformal time to the imaginary axis. To this aim, it is convenient to introduce the variable
z\in\mathbb{C}
as a phase shift of the complexified conformal time
\tau_{c}
:

\tau_{c}=z\,e^{\frac{i{\sf{a}}\pi}{2}}\;.

(33)

The Euclidean signature is defined when
z\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
and the tilted axis is recovered when:

z=-\tau e^{i\,{\sf{a}}\left(\frac{\pi}{2}-\epsilon\right)}\;.

(34)

This analytic continuation maps the geometry of
\mathrm{dS}_{d+1}
to the one of
\mathrm{EAdS}_{d+1}
, with
z
acting as the radial variable with conformal boundary located at
z=0
. The conformal time integrals are deformed to

\int_{-\infty_{\sf{a}}}^{0}\mathrm{d}\tau f(\tau)=e^{-\frac{i{\sf{a}}\pi}{2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}zf(e^{\frac{i{\sf{a}}\pi}{2}}z)\,,

(35)

where
-\infty_{\pm}\equiv-\infty(1\mp i\epsilon)
. As shown in (
257
) and (
259
), this analytic continuation procedure can be repeated at the level of the two-point invariant (
10
). We stress that we do not need to perform the Wick rotation for the Hubble radius. This is because we are interested in dS correlation functions, and the analytic continuation to EAdS should be seen as a technical step.

Using (
23
), the Killing vector differential expression for the Casimir operator is given by the Laplace-Beltrami operator in
\mathrm{dS}_{d+1}

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}:H^{-2}\Box_{\mathrm{dS}}

\displaystyle=-\left(\tau^{2}\partial_{\tau}^{2}-(d-1)\tau\partial_{\tau}-\tau^{2}\partial_{i}^{2}\right)

(36)

\displaystyle=-\left(z^{2}\partial_{z}^{2}-(d-1)z\partial_{z}+z^{2}\partial_{i}^{2}\right)\,.

Then, the definition (
32
) together with the hermiticity of the generators provides us with a set of differential equations for the wavefunction
\Phi^{\mu}_{\bm{k},\mathcal{O}}
:

\displaystyle\left(z^{2}\partial_{z}^{2}-(d-1)z\partial_{z}+z^{2}\partial_{i}^{2}\right)\Phi_{\bm{k},\mathcal{O}}^{\mu}(z,\bm{x})

\displaystyle=-M_{\mu}^{2}\Phi_{\bm{k},\mathcal{O}}^{\mu}(z,\bm{x})\,,

(37)

\displaystyle\partial_{i}\Phi_{\bm{k},\mathcal{O}}^{\mu}(z,\bm{x})

\displaystyle=-ik_{i}\Phi_{\bm{k},\mathcal{O}}^{\mu}(z,\bm{x})\,.

This can be seen in the following way for the momentum, and similarly for the Casimir:

\displaystyle-ik_{i}\Phi^{\mu}_{\bm{k},\mathcal{O}}(z,\bm{x})

\displaystyle=-i\bra{\Omega}\mathcal{O}(z,\bm{x})P_{i}\ket{\mu,\bm{k}}

(38)

\displaystyle=-i\bra{\Omega}[\mathcal{O}(z,\bm{x}),P_{i}]\ket{\mu,\bm{k}}

\displaystyle=\partial_{i}\Phi^{\mu}_{\bm{k},\mathcal{O}}(z,\bm{x})\;.

Since these equations are independent of the nature of
\mathcal{O}
, we can write the wavefuntion (
32
) as:

\Phi^{\mu}_{\bm{k},\mathcal{O}}(z,\bm{x})=c_{\mathcal{O}}(\mu)\Phi^{\mu}_{\bm{k}}(z,\bm{x})\;,

(39)

where the harmonic function
\Phi^{\mu}_{\bm{k}}(z,\bm{x})
satisfies the system (
37
) with Bunch-Davies initial conditions and
c_{\mathcal{O}}(\mu)
is a theory-dependent quantity encoding the physics of the operator
\mathcal{O}(z,\bm{x})
. Equation (
32
) together with the resolution of the identity (
29
) can be used to derive the Euclidean Källén-Lehmann decomposition of the two-point function of the operator
\mathcal{O}(z,\bm{x})

Loparco:2023rug
:

\bra{\Omega}\mathcal{O}(z_{1},\bm{x}_{1})\mathcal{O}(z_{2},\bm{x}_{2})\ket{\Omega}=\int_{\mathcal{S}}\mathrm{d}\mu\;\frac{\rho_{\mathcal{O}}(\mu)}{H^{2}}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{d}\bm{k}}{(2\pi)^{d}}\Phi^{\mu}_{\bm{k}}(z_{1},\bm{x}_{1})\left[\Phi^{\mu}(z_{2},\bm{x}_{2})\right]^{*}\;,

(40)

where
\rho_{\mathcal{O}}(\mu)=H^{2}\mathcal{N}_{\mu}|c_{\mathcal{O}}(\mu)|^{2}
is called the spectral density of
\mathcal{O}
.
5
5

5

If
\mathcal{O}
has mass dimension
\delta_{\mathcal{O}}
, the dimension of the spectral density is
\delta_{\rho}=2\delta_{\mathcal{O}}-d+1
.
For reasons that will become clear later, this only makes sense when the two operators
\mathcal{O}(z_{1,2},\bm{x}_{1,2})
do not live on the same copy of EAdS, which corresponds to the Wightman two-point function. The Lorentzian version can be obtained by consistently applying the Wick rotation as explained in appendix
B
.

Notice that the differential operators in (
37
) are self-adjoint, i.e.
\braket{\mathcal{D}f,g}=\braket{f,\mathcal{D}g}
, with respect to the natural
L^{2}(\mathrm{EAdS}_{d+1})
-inner product

\braket{f,g}\equiv\int_{\mathrm{dS}_{d+1}}f^{*}(X^{E})g(X^{E})\,\mathrm{vol}_{g}=\int_{\mathrm{EAdS}}\frac{\mathrm{d}z\mathrm{d}^{d}\bm{x}}{(Hz)^{d+1}}\,f^{*}(z,\bm{x})g(z,\bm{x})\,,

(41)

with
\mathrm{vol}_{g}\equiv\mathrm{d}^{d+2}X^{E}\,\delta(X^{E}\cdot X^{E}+R_{\mathrm{EAdS}}^{2})
, where we have specified to Poincaré coordinates. This can be checked explicitly after successive integrations by parts.

Keeping only the solution that decays at infinity
z\to\infty
, which is equivalent to requiring the absence of states with negative energy, the set of equations (
37
) fixes the functional dependence of
\Phi
:

\Phi_{\bm{k}}^{\mu}(z,\bm{x})=\frac{H^{\frac{d+1}{2}}}{\sqrt{\pi}}\,e^{-i\bm{k}\cdot\bm{x}}z^{\frac{d}{2}}K_{i\mu}(kz)\,,

(42)

where
k\equiv|\bm{k}|
,
K_{i\mu}
is the modified Bessel function of the second kind (Macdonald function), and the pre-factor has been chosen for later convenience. For real values of
\mu
, the functions
K_{i\mu}
are the kernels of the Kontorovich-Lebedev integral transformation reviewed in App.
A
. This will be central in what follows.
The harmonic functions (
42
) verify the following property under complex conjugation

\left[\Phi_{\bm{k}}^{\mu}(z,\bm{x})\right]^{*}=\Phi_{-\bm{k}}^{\mu}(z,\bm{x})\,,

(43)

which can be interpreted as a CPT symmetry,
6
6

6

See e.g.
Goodhew:2024eup
;
Thavanesan:2025kyc
;
Thavanesan:2025ibm
for constraints from CPT on cosmological correlators.
and the shadow symmetry yields

\Phi_{\bm{k}}^{\mu}(z,\bm{x})=\Phi_{\bm{k}}^{-\mu}(z,\bm{x})\,,

(44)

for
\mu\in\mathbb{C}
and
z\in\mathbb{C}\backslash(-\infty,0]
. Analytic continuation to the Lorentzian signature is recovered when one evaluates
z
on the tilted axis (
34
):

\Phi^{\mu}_{\bm{k}}\left(-\tau e^{i{\sf{a}}\left(\frac{\pi}{2}-\epsilon\right)},\bm{x}\right)=H^{\frac{d+1}{2}}e^{-i\bm{k}\cdot\bm{x}}u^{-{\sf{a}}}_{k}\left(\tau(1-i{\sf{a}}\epsilon),\mu\right)\;,

(45)

where the
u^{\sf{a}}_{k}(\tau,\mu)
are the familiar mode functions of a massive field of mass
m=HM_{\mu}
in momentum space:

\displaystyle u^{+}_{k}(\tau,\mu)

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{\pi}}{2}e^{-\frac{\pi\mu}{2}-\frac{i\pi(d-2)}{4}}(-\tau)^{\frac{d}{2}}H_{i\mu}^{(1)}(-k\tau)

(46)

\displaystyle u^{-}_{k}(\tau,\mu)

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{\pi}}{2}e^{+\frac{\pi\mu}{2}+\frac{i\pi(d-2)}{4}}(-\tau)^{\frac{d}{2}}H_{i\mu}^{(2)}(-k\tau)\;,

and
H_{i\mu}^{(1,2)}
are the Hankel functions. Notice that the
i\epsilon
prescription is not needed in order to evaluate expression (
45
) as the mode functions are analytic for
\tau\in\mathbb{R}_{-}
. However, we will see that the Lorentzian correlation functions have branch cuts when two operators are within each other’s light cones, and keeping the
\epsilon
’s explicit will be useful. The Wick rotation (
45
) and the relation (
225
) can be combined to show the completenesses relation among Hankel functions used in
Grafe:2026qsm
;
Lee:2025kgs
.

Action of generators.

The definition (
32
) of the wavefunctions, together with the functional form (
42
) of their Euclidean versions, and the commutation relations (
23
), dictate how the
\mathrm{SO}(1,d+1)
generators (
20
) act on the (scalar) single-particle states
\ket{\mu,\bm{k}}
. Explicitly, these actions are given by
7
7

7

Our minus sign and factor
i
convention differ from
Hogervorst:2021uvp
because we take the generators to be Hermitian.

\displaystyle D\ket{\mu,\bm{k}}

\displaystyle=i\left(k_{i}\partial_{k^{i}}+\tfrac{d}{2}\right)\ket{\mu,\bm{k}}\,,

(47)

\displaystyle P_{i}\ket{\mu,\bm{k}}

\displaystyle=k_{i}\ket{\mu,\bm{k}}\,,

\displaystyle K_{i}\ket{\mu,\bm{k}}

\displaystyle=\left[k_{i}\delta_{j\ell}\partial_{k^{j}}\partial_{k^{\ell}}-2\left(2k_{j}\partial_{k^{j}}+d\right)\partial_{k^{i}}-\tfrac{\mu^{2}}{k^{2}}k_{i}\right]\ket{\mu,\bm{k}}\,,

\displaystyle M_{ij}\ket{\mu,\bm{k}}

\displaystyle=-i\left(k_{i}\partial_{k^{j}}-k_{j}\partial_{k^{i}}\right)\ket{\mu,\bm{k}}\,.

For instance, using (
23
), one obtains

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}\Phi_{\bm{k},\mathcal{O}}^{\mu}(z,\bm{x})

\displaystyle=-i\left(z\partial_{z}+x^{i}\partial_{x^{i}}\right)\Phi_{\bm{k},\mathcal{O}}^{\mu}(z,\bm{x})

(48)

\displaystyle=-i\left(\tfrac{d}{2}-i\bm{k}\cdot\bm{x}\right)\Phi_{\bm{k},\mathcal{O}}^{\mu}(z,\bm{x})-ic_{\mathcal{O}}(\mu)e^{-i\bm{k}\cdot\bm{x}}z^{d/2}k^{i}\partial_{k^{i}}K_{i\mu}(kz)

\displaystyle=-i\left(k^{i}\partial_{k^{i}}+\tfrac{d}{2}\right)\Phi_{\bm{k},\mathcal{O}}^{\mu}(z,\bm{x})

\displaystyle=-i\left(k^{i}\partial_{k^{i}}+\tfrac{d}{2}\right)\braket{\Omega|\mathcal{O}(z,\bm{x})|\mu,\bm{k}}\,.

The symmetry of
K_{i\mu}(kz)
under
k\leftrightarrow z
was used to write
z\partial_{z}K_{i\mu}(kz)=k^{i}\partial_{k^{i}}K_{i\mu}(kz)
. Additionally, the dilatation acts on a bulk operator as the commutator

\mathcal{D}\Phi_{\bm{k},\mathcal{O}}^{\mu}(z,\bm{x})=\braket{\Omega|[D,\mathcal{O}(z,\bm{x})]|\mu,\bm{k}}=-\braket{\Omega|\mathcal{O}(z,\bm{x})D|\mu,\bm{k}}\,,

(49)

where we used the invariance of the Bunch-Davies vacuum under de Sitter isometries, i.e.
D\ket{\Omega}=0
. Since the generators are Hermitian, it follows that
\bra{\Omega}D=0
. Identifying both expressions, we recover the first relation in (
47
). Deriving the remaining identities is similar and straightforward. In the same way, one can derive the transformation properties of the position-space states
\ket{z,\bm{x}}
that we now introduce.

Back to position space.

The harmonic functions allow to define the position-space states to which the momentum-space basis elements
\ket{\mu,\bm{k}}
are dual as:

\displaystyle\ket{z,\bm{x}}_{\mathcal{S}}

\displaystyle\equiv\int_{\mathcal{S}}\mathrm{d}\mu\mathcal{N}_{\mu}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{d}\bm{k}}{(2\pi)^{d}}\left[\Phi_{\bm{k}}^{\mu}(z,\bm{x})\right]^{*}\ket{\mu,\bm{k}}\;,

(50)

A consequence of (
50
) is that the harmonic functions provide the overlap between momentum states and their position-space duals

\;{}_{\mathcal{S}}\braket{z,\bm{x}|\mu,\bm{k}}=\braket{\mu,\bm{k}|z,\bm{x}}^{*}_{\mathcal{S}}=\Phi_{\bm{k}}^{\mu}(z,\bm{x})\,.

(51)

As the states
\ket{\mu,\bm{k}}
represent the propagation of some unlocalised particle of mass
M_{\mu}
and momentum
\bm{k}
, the dual state
\ket{z,\bm{x}}_{\mathcal{S}}
stands for a localised quanta in a superposition of Casimir and momentum eigenstates. In the next sub-section, we will show that these position states form a complete basis of the space of square-integrable functions when the
\mu
integral is restricted from the full spectrum
\mathcal{S}
to the principal series
\mathcal{P}
.

3.3
Kontorovich-Lebedev-Fourier Space

In the above section, we showed that the differential operators in the eigenvalue equations (
37
) for the harmonic functions are self-adjoint with respect to the natural
L^{2}(\mathrm{EAdS}_{d+1})
-inner product (
41
). When dealing with self-ajoint bounded operators on a compact domain, the Sturm-Liouville theory guarantees that the corresponding discrete set of eigenfunctions form an orthogonal basis of the associated Hilbert space. For non-compact operators, as relevant here, the spectrum acquires a continuous part, but, more importantly,
the proper mathematical structure becomes the rigged Hilbert space (also known as the Gelfand triple), and only a subset of the eigenfunctions enter into the corresponding expansion.
This rather technical procedure is carried out in App.
A
(see also
Belrhali:2026ktb
for a summary), where we show that any square integrable function on
\mathrm{EAdS}_{d+1}
,
f(z,\bm{x})\in L^{2}(\mathrm{EAdS}_{d+1})
can be decomposed along the principal series harmonic functions:

f(z,\bm{x})=\int_{\mathrm{KLF}}\mathrm{d}\mu\,\mathcal{N}_{\mu}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{d}\bm{k}}{(2\pi)^{d}}\,\Phi^{\mu}_{\bm{k}}(z,\bm{x})f^{\mu}_{\bm{k}}\,,

(52)

where, importantly, the
\mu
-integration range spans the real axis only:

\int_{\mathrm{KLF}}\mathrm{d}\mu\,\mathcal{N}_{\mu}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{d}\bm{k}}{(2\pi)^{d}}\equiv\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{d}\mu\,\mathcal{N}_{\mu}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{d}\bm{k}}{(2\pi)^{d}}\,,

(53)

and

\mathcal{N}_{\mu}=\frac{\mu}{\pi}\sinh(\pi\mu)

(54)

is often called the de Sitter density of (principal series) states. The temporal component of the transformation (
52
) can be recognised as the the Kontorovich-Lebedev (KL) transform, introduced in App.
A
. Since the spatial part is the usual
d
-dimensional Fourier transform, we name this (restricted) momentum space the
Kontorovich-Lebedev-Fourier
(KLF) space.

Equivalently to the decomposition (
52
), the function
f(z,\bm{x})
can be seen as a state living on the principal series subspace

f(z,\bm{x})=\braket{z,\bm{x}|f}\,,\quad\text{where}\quad\ket{f}\in\bigcup_{\mu\in\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{P}_{\mu}\,,

(55)

and we defined
\ket{z,\bm{x}}
as the restriction of the position state (
50
) to a superposition of principal series states only:

\ket{z,\bm{x}}=\int_{\mathrm{KLF}}\mathrm{d}\mu\mathcal{N}_{\mu}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{d}\bm{k}}{(2\pi)^{d}}\left[\Phi^{\mu}_{\bm{k}}(z,\bm{x})\right]^{*}\ket{\mu,\bm{k}}\;.

(56)

As advertised before, the restricted position states (
56
) constitute an orthonormal and complete basis of the principal series subspace. Indeed, the completeness and orthogonality relations of the principal series harmonic functions (
225
) implies

\braket{z,\bm{x}|z^{\prime},\bm{x}^{\prime}}=(Hz)^{d+1}\delta(z-z^{\prime})\delta^{(d)}(\bm{x}-\bm{x}^{\prime})\;,

(57)

and

\displaystyle\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{P}}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathrm{EAdS}}\frac{\mathrm{d}z\mathrm{d}^{d}\bm{x}}{(Hz)^{d+1}}\ket{z,\bm{x}}\bra{z,\bm{x}}

(58)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathrm{KLF}}\mathrm{d}\mu\mathcal{N}_{\mu}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{d}\bm{k}}{(2\pi)^{d}}\ket{\mu,k}\bra{\mu,\bm{k}}\;.

Consequently, the general KLF decomposition (
52
) can be easily recovered by inserting a resolution of identity (
58
) in (
55
). Similarly, the momentum-space representation of the
f^{\mu}_{\bm{k}}=\braket{\mu,\bm{k}|f}
in the integral (
52
) is given by the following inverse formula

f^{\mu}_{\bm{k}}=\int_{\mathrm{EAdS}}\frac{\mathrm{d}z\mathrm{d}^{d}\bm{x}}{(Hz)^{d+1}}\left[\Phi^{\mu}_{\bm{k}}(z,\bm{x})\right]^{*}f(z,\bm{x})\,.

(59)

A crucial consequence is that the principal series provides a complete representation only within the
subspace
of the full Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
consisting of square-integrable
L^{2}(\mathrm{EAdS}_{d+1})
functions. For completeness, let us mention the existence of the Parseval identity, i.e. for any pair of square integrable functions
f(z,\bm{x}),g(z,\bm{x})\in L^{2}(\mathrm{EAdS}_{d+1})
, the scalar product is preserved by the KLF transformation:

\int_{\mathrm{KLF}}\mathrm{d}\mu\,\mathcal{N}_{\mu}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{d}\bm{k}}{(2\pi)^{d}}\left[f^{\mu}_{\bm{k}}\right]^{*}g^{\mu}_{\bm{k}}=\int_{\mathrm{EAdS}}\frac{\mathrm{d}z\,\mathrm{d}^{d}\bm{x}}{(Hz)^{d+1}}f(z,\bm{x})^{*}g(z,\bm{x})\,.

(60)

This identity also holds for more general functions provided that the momentum-space integral also includes contributions from the complementary and exceptional series, as we now discuss.

3.4
Role of the Non-Principal Series

Motivated by the path-integral formalism, we paid special attention to the space
L^{2}\left(\mathrm{EAdS}_{d+1}\right)
. However, the objects we encounter in physical situations can be more general functions and other UIRs can appear in their spectral decomposition. As we show in this section, these contributions can be read from the non-analyticities in the
\mu
complex plane of the KLF modes. Moreover, since the latter are meromorphic functions in every physical example, the non-principal UIRs only contribute as isolated points in the spectrum and can be taken into account in the decomposition (
52
) by promoting
f^{\mu}_{\bm{k}}
to a distribution. In order to achieve this, we generalise the orthogonality
relation among the harmonic functions:

\int_{\mathrm{EAdS}}\frac{\mathrm{d}z\mathrm{d}^{d}\bm{x}}{(Hz)^{d+1}}\left[\Phi^{\mu}_{\bm{k}}(z,\bm{x})\right]^{*}\Phi^{\alpha}_{\bm{k}^{\prime}}(z,\bm{x})=\frac{(2\pi)^{d}}{\mathcal{N}_{\mu}}\delta^{(d)}(\bm{k}-\bm{k}^{\prime})\hat{\delta}_{\alpha}(\mu)\;,

(61)

where for any
\alpha\in\mathbb{C}
,
\hat{\delta}_{\alpha}(\mu)
acts on KLF space as:

\hat{\delta}_{\alpha}[f]\equiv\int\displaylimits^{\infty}_{-\infty}\mathrm{d}\mu\;\hat{\delta}_{\alpha}(\mu)f^{\mu}_{\bm{k}}=\frac{1}{2}\left(f^{(\alpha)}_{\bm{k}}+f^{(-\alpha)}_{\bm{k}}\right)\;.

(62)

Notice that for real values of
\alpha
, (
62
) reduces to the definition of the usual symmetrised delta function. For convenience, we introduce the following notation for the KLF generalised delta function:

\delta\left({}^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}_{\bm{k}_{1}\bm{k}_{2}}\right)\equiv\frac{(2\pi)^{d}}{\mathcal{N}_{\mu_{1}}}\hat{\delta}_{\mu_{2}}(\mu_{1})\delta^{(d)}(\bm{k}_{1}-\bm{k}_{2})\;.

(63)

Generic case.

Let us now explain how non-principal contributions are naturally taken into account in our formalism. A generic smooth function
f(z,\bm{x})
defined on EAdS can fail to be square-integrable either at
z\to 0
or
z\to\infty
. Since the large
z
behavior of the harmonic function is independent of the frequency, it cannot impact the structure of the KLF mode and does not lead to the appearance of new UIRs in the spectrum. We thus consider functions that are UV square-integrable in what follows. However, if the function fails to be square-integrable in the IR, this necessarily yields contributions from the complementary series. To see this, let us consider the following series expansion:
8
8

8

IR logarithmic behaviours are also taken into account as they can be obtained by differentiation:
z^{\Delta}\log^{n}(z)=\frac{\mathrm{d}^{n}}{\mathrm{d}\Delta^{n}}z^{\Delta}
. If the KLF modes of the monomials are meromorphic, the differentiation cannot induce a branch cut.

f(z,\bm{x})=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}z^{\Delta+n}f_{n}(\bm{x})\;,

(64)

where
\Delta
is the leading power near
z=0
and we assume the spatial coefficients
f_{n}(\bm{x})
to be square-integrable.
The function
f(z,\bm{x})
is square-integrable only
if
\text{Re}(\Delta)>\frac{d}{2}
. In this case, the KLF transform (
59
) is well defined and can be evaluated exactly:
9
9

9

The evaluation of the time integral can be performed by using eq.
6.561
,
16
of
gradshteyn2007
.

f^{(\mu),\mathcal{P}}_{\bm{k}}=\frac{H^{-\frac{d+1}{2}}}{4\sqrt{\pi}}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\hat{f}_{n}(\bm{k})\left(\frac{2}{k}\right)^{\Delta-\frac{d}{2}+n}\Gamma\left(\frac{\Delta-\frac{d}{2}+n\pm i\mu}{2}\right)\;,

(65)

where
\hat{f}(\bm{k})
is the usual Fourier transform and we add the subscript
{\cal P}
to recall that
\mu
is real. Now, in the
\mu
complex plane, this is a meromorphic function with two series of poles in the lower and upper half-planes:

\mu_{\pm}^{\ell}=\pm i\left(\ell+\Delta-\frac{d}{2}\right)\;,\quad\ell\in\mathbb{N}\;,

(66)

where the residues are expressed as the following sums of
\lfloor\frac{\ell}{2}+1\rfloor
terms:

\displaystyle\textrm{Res}\left(f^{(\mu),\mathcal{P}}_{\bm{k}},\mu^{\ell}_{\pm}\right)=\mp\frac{iH^{-\frac{d+1}{2}}}{2\sqrt{\pi}}\mathcal{S}^{\ell}_{\Delta}\;,

(67)

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{\ell}_{\Delta}=\sum_{j\textrm{ s.t. }\ell-j\in 2\mathbb{N}}\frac{(-1)^{\frac{\ell-j}{2}}\hat{f}_{j}(\bm{k})}{\left(\frac{\ell-j}{2}\right)!}

\displaystyle\left(\frac{2}{k}\right)^{\Delta-\frac{d}{2}+j}\Gamma\left(\Delta-\frac{d}{2}+\frac{\ell-j}{2}\right)\;.

The direct consequence of the integrability condition is that the KLF modes are analytic in the strip
|\mathrm{Im}(\mu)|\leq\text{Re}(\Delta)-\frac{d}{2}
. Since the function (
64
) is quite general, we expect this statement to hold for a very large class of functions. In particular, we do not know any example of a function whose KLF mode features a cut in the
\mu
plane. The corresponding analytic structure is pictured in the left panel of figure
5
. In the regime of square-integrability,
f(z,\bm{x})
can be written as:

\displaystyle f(z,\bm{x})=i\frac{H^{\frac{d+1}{2}}z^{\frac{d}{2}}}{\sqrt{\pi}}\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{d}\bm{k}}{(2\pi)^{d}}e^{-i\bm{k}\cdot\bm{x}}\int\displaylimits^{\infty}_{-\infty}\mathrm{d}\mu\;\mu f^{(\mu),\mathcal{P}}_{\bm{k}}I_{i\mu}(kz)\;,

(68)

where we split
\mathcal{N}_{\mu}K_{i\mu}(z)=\frac{i}{2}\mu(I_{i\mu}(z)-I_{-i\mu}(z))
to use the decay of
I_{i\mu}
in the lower
\mu
half-plane, the two contributions being equal due to the shadow-symmetry of
f^{(\mu),\mathcal{P}}_{\bm{k}}
.
The spectral integral can then be performed by collecting the residues
\mu_{-}^{\ell}
:

\displaystyle f(z,\bm{x})

\displaystyle=2\sqrt{\pi}H^{\frac{d+1}{2}}z^{\frac{d}{2}}\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{d}\bm{k}}{(2\pi)^{d}}e^{-i\bm{k}\cdot\bm{x}}\mu^{\ell}_{-}\text{Res}\left(f^{(\mu),\mathcal{P}}_{\bm{k}},\mu_{-}^{\ell}\right)I_{i\mu_{-}^{\ell}}(kz)\;.

(69)

It is not obvious that this complicated sum reproduces a simple power-law dependence, but this can be checked using the following Neumann-type expansion (see
10.44.4
of
NIST:DLMF
):
10
10

10

This kind of resummation formulas can be systematically obtained as the residues expansion of the inverse KL transform of known functions.

\left(\frac{z}{2}\right)^{\alpha}=\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{\ell}}{\ell!}(\alpha+2\ell)\Gamma(\alpha+\ell)I_{\alpha+2\ell}(z)\;,\quad\alpha\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0,-1,-2,\ldots\}\;.

(70)

When
\Delta-\frac{d}{2}\notin-\mathbb{N}
, it is clear that the expansion (
69
) remains valid in the regime
\textrm{Re}(\Delta)<\frac{d}{2}
, where the function
f(z,\bm{x})
fails to be square-integrable. In that case, the integral over the principal series (
68
) does not coincide with the residues expansion (
69
) and extra terms are needed in the spectral decomposition. To see that, one can look at the analytical structure of the KLF modes (
65
) in this regime, which is pictured in the right panel of figure
5
. We can see that the two series of poles cross the real axis, and the previous region of analyticity
|\mathrm{Im}(\mu)|\leq|\text{Re}(\Delta)-\frac{d}{2}|
is now populated by poles coming from both Gamma factors. Consequently, the integral (
68
) misses some
\mu_{-}^{\ell}
residues and gets extra contributions from some
\mu_{+}^{\ell}
residues. If
\frac{d}{2}-\textrm{Re}(\Delta)\notin\mathbb{N}
, we have:

\displaystyle\int\displaylimits^{\infty}_{-\infty}\mathrm{d}\mu\;\mu f^{(\mu),\mathcal{P}}_{\bm{k}}I_{i\mu}(kz)=

\displaystyle-2\pi i\sum_{\ell=\lceil\frac{d}{2}-\textrm{Re}(\Delta)\rceil}^{\infty}\mu^{0,\ell}_{-}\text{Res}\left(f^{(\mu),\mathcal{P}}_{\bm{k}},\mu_{-}^{\ell}\right)I_{i\mu_{-}^{\ell}}(kz)

(71)

\displaystyle-2\pi i\sum_{\ell=0}^{\lfloor\frac{d}{2}-\textrm{Re}(\Delta)\rfloor}\mu^{\ell}_{+}\text{Res}\left(f^{(\mu),\mathcal{P}}_{\bm{k}},\mu_{+}^{\ell}\right)I_{i\mu_{+}^{\ell}}(kz)\;.

In the integer case, some
\mu_{\pm}^{\ell}
poles collide, resulting in a second-order singularity. Moreover, the pole for
\ell=\frac{d}{2}-\Delta
, being located on the real axis, requires the integral to be regularised by taking its principal value. In order to avoid dealing with these complications, we consider the non-integer case in what follows.

\mathrm{Re}(\Delta)-\frac{d}{2}
\mathrm{Re}(\Delta)-\frac{d}{2}
\text{Re}(\mu)
\text{Im}(\mu)
\boxed{\mathrm{Re}(\Delta)>\frac{d}{2}}
\mu^{\ell}_{+}
\mu^{\ell}_{-}

\frac{d}{2}-\mathrm{Re}(\Delta)
\frac{d}{2}-\mathrm{Re}(\Delta)
\text{Re}(\mu)
\text{Im}(\mu)
\boxed{\mathrm{Re}(\Delta)<\frac{d}{2}}
\mu^{\ell}_{+}
\mu^{\ell}_{-}

Figure 3
:
Analytic structure in the
\mu
complex plane of (
65
), in the generic situation where
\mathrm{Im}(\Delta)\neq 0
.
Left:
case where the function (
64
) is square-integrable as its leading fall-off is greater than
\frac{d}{2}
. The function has two distinct series of poles separated by a region of analyticity.
Right:
case where the function (
64
) is not square-integrable as its leading fall-off is smaller than
\frac{d}{2}
. The two series of poles are now intertwined in the former analyticity region.

In order to recover the right expansion, one should add the missing contributions from the
\mu_{-}^{\ell}
poles and subtract those coming from the
\mu_{+}^{\ell}
ones, yielding the following non-principal contribution to the spectral decomposition:

\displaystyle 2\pi i\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{d}\bm{k}}{(2\pi)^{d}}\frac{H^{\frac{d+1}{2}}}{\sqrt{\pi}}z^{\frac{d}{2}}e^{-i\bm{k}\cdot\bm{x}}\sum_{a=\pm}\sum_{\ell=0}^{\lfloor\frac{d}{2}-\mathrm{Re}(\Delta)\rfloor}a\,i\mu_{a}^{\ell}\text{Res}\left(f^{(\mu),\mathcal{P}}_{\bm{k}},\mu_{a}^{\ell}\right)I_{i\mu_{a}^{\ell}}(kz)

(72)

\displaystyle=4\pi i\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{d}\bm{k}}{(2\pi)^{d}}\sum_{\ell=0}^{\lfloor\frac{d}{2}-\mathrm{Re}(\Delta)\rfloor}\mathcal{N}_{\mu_{+}^{\ell}}\text{Res}\left(f^{(\mu),\mathcal{P}}_{\bm{k}},\mu_{+}^{\ell}\right)\Phi^{\mu_{+}^{\ell}}_{\bm{k}}(z,\bm{x})

\displaystyle=4\pi i\int_{\mathrm{KLF}}\sum_{\ell=0}^{\lfloor\frac{d}{2}-\mathrm{Re}(\Delta)\rfloor}\hat{\delta}_{\mu_{+}^{\ell}}(\mu)\text{Res}\left(f^{(\mu),\mathcal{P}}_{\bm{k}},\mu_{+}^{\ell}\right)\Phi^{\mu}_{\bm{k}}(z,\bm{x})\;,

where we made use of the shadow anti-symmetry of the residue and again of
\mathcal{N}_{\mu}K_{i\mu}(z)=\frac{i}{2}\mu(I_{i\mu}(z)-I_{-i\mu}(z))
.

A few comments are in order. In physical applications coming from a unitary theory, one expects these contributions to fall in
\mathrm{SO}(1,d+1)
UIRs. This corresponds to the following behaviours for the possible fall-offs:

\displaystyle\textrm{Unitarity:}

\displaystyle\bullet\mathrm{Re}(\Delta)>\frac{d}{2},\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\mathrm{Im}(\Delta)\,\textrm{arbitrary},

\displaystyle\textrm{principal}

\displaystyle\bullet 0\leq\mathrm{Re}(\Delta)<\frac{d}{2},\,\,\mathrm{Im}(\Delta)=0,

\displaystyle\textrm{principal}+\textrm{complementary}

\displaystyle\bullet\mathrm{Re}(\Delta)\in\mathbb{N}^{-},\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\mathrm{Im}(\Delta)=0,\quad

\displaystyle\textrm{principal}+\textrm{(complementary)}+\textrm{exceptional}

where we listed the ever-present principal series contribution, and the possible non-principal ones according to each case (in the last situation, complementary series contributions arise or not depending on the values of
d
). As advertised before, the non-principal contributions are fully determined by the non analyticities of the principal series contribution. Since for our class of functions, the latter is meromorphic in the
\mu
plane, it can only constitute a discrete set.

To summarise, any generic function of the form (
64
) can be expanded into KLF modes, simply by promoting them to the sum of a meromorphic function and a set of distributional terms encoding the contributions outside the principal series:

f^{\mu}_{\bm{k},0}=f^{(\mu),\mathcal{P}}_{\bm{k},0}+4\pi i\Theta\left(\frac{d}{2}-\mathrm{Re}(\Delta)\right)\sum_{\ell=0}^{\lfloor\frac{d}{2}-\mathrm{Re}(\Delta)\rfloor}\hat{\delta}_{\mu_{+}^{\ell}}(\mu)\text{Res}\left(f^{(\mu),\mathcal{P}}_{\bm{k}},\mu_{+}^{\ell}\right)\;.

(73)

An example: scalar conformal two-point function.

For concreteness, let us look at the example of a conformal two point function
Hogervorst:2021uvp
;
Loparco:2023rug
. In a dS CFT, the two-point function of a local scalar operator
\mathcal{O}
with scaling dimension
\Delta\geq\frac{d-1}{2}
is completely fixed by conformal invariance to be:

\braket{\mathcal{O}(X_{1})\mathcal{O}(X_{2})}\equiv\left(\frac{R_{\mathrm{dS}}^{2}}{d(X_{1},X_{2})}\right)^{\Delta}=\frac{1}{2^{\Delta}(1-\sigma^{\mathrm{dS}})^{\Delta}}\;,

(74)

where
X_{1,2}\equiv(\tau_{1,2},\bm{x}_{1,2})
denotes two points in
\mathrm{dS}
, and the chordal distance
d(X_{1},X_{2})
and two-point invariant
\sigma^{\mathrm{dS}}
are defined in Eqs. (
10
)-(
11
). As explained in appendix
B
, one should regulate the branch cut at time-like separations by means of the appropriate
i\epsilon
prescription. Our interest here lies in the Wightman two-point function, whose Euclidean counterpart simply follows from (
74
) and the Wick rotation (
257
) as
11
11

11

There is no need to keep track of the
i\epsilon
here as (
75
)
1-\sigma^{E}
is always positive.

\braket{\mathcal{O}(X_{1}^{E})\mathcal{O}(X_{2}^{E})}=\frac{1}{2^{\Delta}(1-\sigma^{E})^{\Delta}}\;.

(75)

In the regime where this is a square-integrable function, its KLF transform with respect to one of the two variables converges. Since the leading fall-off of this function is given by the scaling dimension of the operator
\Delta
, we know from the above analysis that this is the case when
\Delta>\frac{d}{2}
. As explained in appendix
C
, the KLF transform in
X_{1}^{E}
can be evaluated by direct computation and it takes the following form:

\displaystyle\int_{\mathrm{EAdS}}\frac{\mathrm{d}z_{1}\mathrm{d}^{d}\bm{x}_{1}}{(Hz)^{d+1}}\left[\Phi^{\mu}_{\bm{k}}(z_{1},\bm{x}_{1})\right]^{*}\braket{\mathcal{O}(X_{1}^{E})\mathcal{O}(X_{2}^{E})}=\frac{\rho^{\mathcal{P}}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mu)}{H^{2}\mathcal{N}_{\mu}}\left[\Phi^{\mu}_{\bm{k}}(z_{2},\bm{x}_{2})\right]^{*}\;,

(76)

where the spectral density
\rho^{\mathcal{P}}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mu)
is given by:

\rho^{\mathcal{P}}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mu)=\frac{\mathcal{N}_{\mu}}{H^{d-1}}\underbrace{\frac{2^{-2\Delta+d+1}\pi^{\frac{d+1}{2}}}{\Gamma(\Delta)\Gamma\left(\Delta-\frac{d}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\right)}}_{c_{\Delta}}\Gamma\left(\Delta-\frac{d}{2}\pm i\mu\right)\;.

(77)

As above, this has two distinct series of poles separated by an analyticity region (see figure
5
). Because of the simple form of the function we do not need the late-time expansion and the poles are labeled by a single integer
n
:

\mu^{n}_{\pm}=\pm i\left(\Delta-\frac{d}{2}+n\right)\;.

(78)

Now, let us insert this in the KLF decomposition (
52
):

\braket{\mathcal{O}(X_{1}^{E})\mathcal{O}(X_{2}^{E})}_{\Delta>\frac{d}{2}}=\int\displaylimits^{\infty}_{-\infty}\mathrm{d}\mu\rho^{\mathcal{P}}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mu)W_{\mu}(\sigma^{E})\;,

(79)

where the function
W_{\mu}(\sigma^{E})
coming from the evaluation of the momentum integral is defined in (
232
). For the function (
64
), it was convenient, in order to evaluate the integral, to split
K_{i\mu}
into the two modified Bessel functions
I_{\pm i\mu}
. Here, since we already performed the spatial momentum integration, the same procedure amounts to split the function
W_{\mu}(\sigma^{E})
in two EAdS bulk-to-bulk propagators
\Pi^{\mathrm{EAdS}}_{\mu}(\sigma^{E})
according to the connection formula (
267
). This function, defined in (
268
), both decays and is analytic in the
\mu
lower half-plane. One can thus evaluate the integral (
79
) by closing the contour and collecting the residues at the poles
\mu_{n}^{-}
:

\displaystyle\braket{\mathcal{O}(X_{1}^{E})\mathcal{O}(X_{2}^{E})}=-2\pi\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mu_{-}^{n}\textrm{Res}\left(\frac{\rho^{\mathcal{P}}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mu)}{\mathcal{N}_{\mu}},\mu^{n}_{-}\right)\Pi^{\mathrm{EAdS}}_{\mu_{-}^{n}}(\sigma^{E})\;.

(80)

An explicit expression is provided in equation (
270
).

This is the analogue for the CFT of Eq. (
69
) for the test function (
64
).
Like for this case, Eq. (
80
) remains valid when
\Delta<\frac{d}{2}
for
\frac{d}{2}-\Delta\notin\mathbb{N}
, and the principal series integral should be corrected by adding and subtracting the appropriate pole contributions. The integer case has the same pathology as above. This is the dS counterpart of the singularities one encounters by taking the Fourier transform of a conformal two-point function in flat spacetime, resulting in the appearance of a scale anomaly
Bzowski:2013sza
.

Finally, the extension of the KLF expansion takes the same form as in the generic case:

\displaystyle\braket{\mathcal{O}(X_{1}^{E})\mathcal{O}(X_{2}^{E})}_{\Delta<\frac{d}{2}}

\displaystyle=\int\displaylimits^{\infty}_{-\infty}\mathrm{d}\mu\rho^{\mathcal{P}}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mu)W_{\mu}(\sigma^{E})

(81)

\displaystyle+4i\pi\sum_{n=0}^{\lfloor\frac{d}{2}-\Delta\rfloor}\text{Res}\left(\rho^{P}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mu)W_{\mu}(\sigma^{E}),\mu=\mu^{n}_{+}\right)\;.

An explicit expression can be found in equation (
272
).

In the case of a unitary CFT, i.e.
\Delta>\frac{d-1}{2}
, there is only a single contribution from the complementary series. Using our generalised delta function (
62
), this can be rewritten as a KLF decomposition:

\braket{\mathcal{O}(X_{1}^{E})\mathcal{O}(X_{2}^{E})}=\int_{\mathrm{KLF}}\frac{\rho_{\mathcal{O}}(\mu)}{H^{2}\mathcal{N}_{\mu}}\Phi^{\mu}_{\bm{k}}(X_{1}^{E})\left[\Phi^{\mu}_{\bm{k}}(X_{2}^{E})\right]^{*}\;,

(82)

where the KLF spectral density is given by the sum of a smooth function and a distributional part

\rho_{\mathcal{O}}(\mu)=\rho^{\mathcal{P}}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mu)+4i\pi\,\Theta\left(\frac{d}{2}-\Delta\right)\hat{\delta}_{\mu^{0}_{+}}(\mu)\text{Res}\left(\rho^{\mathcal{P}}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mu),\mu=\mu^{0}_{+}\right)\;.

(83)

This reproduces the result of
Loparco:2023rug
for the Källén-Lehmann decomposition of the CFT Wightman function.

4
Cosmological Correlators in Kontorovich-Lebedev-Fourier Space

As it is now clear that KLF space is the dS counterpart of the
(d+1)
-dimensional energy-momentum space in Minkowski, let us follow this example and turn the kinematical statements we obtained into handy prescriptions to formulate perturbation theory in any QFT. To take a maximal advantage of our functional approach, we make use of the path-integral formalism.

4.1
KLF Space Correlators

In inflationary quantum field theory, the observable quantities are given by equal-time correlation functions in the Bunch-Davies vacuum state
\ket{\Omega}
, evaluated on the conformal future boundary
\tau\to 0
:

\mathcal{C}_{\cal{B}}(\bm{x}_{1},\ldots,\bm{x}_{n})=\bra{\Omega}\hat{\mathcal{O}_{1}}(\bm{x}_{1})\ldots\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{n}(\bm{x}_{n})\ket{\Omega}\;,

(84)

where the operators
\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{i}(x_{i})
are defined by the late-time leading behaviour of bulk local operators
\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{i}(\tau_{i},\bm{x}_{i})
.
12
12

12

See e.g.
SalehiVaziri:2024joi
for a detailed discussion of the subtleties raised by this definition.
These correlation functions are naturally expressed via the so-called Schwinger-Keldysh (SK) formalism
Maldacena:2002vr
;
Weinberg:2005vy
. In the latter, we define the bulk correlation function as an inner product between two states resulting from two path integrals with different local operator insertions:

\mathcal{C}_{{\sf{a}}_{1}\ldots{\sf{a}}_{n}}(\tau_{1},\bm{x}_{1};\ldots;\tau_{n},\bm{x}_{n})=\int_{\textrm{B.D.}}^{\varphi_{+}=\varphi_{-}}\mathcal{D}\varphi_{+}\mathcal{D}\varphi_{-}\mathcal{O}_{1}^{{\sf{a}}_{1}}\ldots\mathcal{O}_{n}^{{\sf{a}}_{n}}e^{iS_{+}[\varphi_{+}]-iS_{-}[\varphi_{-}]}\;,

(85)

where
\mathcal{O}^{{\sf{a}}_{j}}_{j}\equiv\mathcal{O}^{{\sf{a}}_{j}}_{j}(\tau_{j},\bm{x}_{j})
are functions of the physical field degrees of freedom
\varphi_{{\sf{a}}_{j}}(\tau_{j},\bm{x}_{j})
standing for the insertion of a local operator
\hat{\mathcal{O}}^{{\sf{a}}_{j}}_{j}(\tau_{j},\bm{x}_{j})
on the branch
{\sf{a}}_{j}=\pm
of the path integral.
13
13

13

We consider that the Bunch-Davies vacuum state is normalised, hence there is no contribution from disconnected bubble diagrams.
In a unitary theory, these two copies of the evolution are perfectly independent and we only impose the two fields to coincide at final time in order to define the inner product.
14
14

14

Strictly speaking, one can impose the future boundary condition at any time
\tau_{f}
in the future of the latest time involved in the correlation function
Weinberg:2005vy
.
Therefore, the ordering subtleties only matter for operators inserted on the same branch. As a result, the operators living on the
(-)+
branches are, respectively, (anti-)time ordered. For instance, as shown in the left panel of Fig.
4
, in the case of a mixed four-point function, one has:

\mathcal{C}_{+-+-}(\tau_{1},\bm{x}_{1};\ldots;\tau_{4},\bm{x}_{4})=\bra{\Omega}\bar{\mathcal{T}}\left[\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{2}^{-}\hat{\mathcal{O}}^{-}_{4}\right]\mathcal{T}\left[\hat{\mathcal{O}}_{1}^{+}\hat{\mathcal{O}}^{+}_{3}\right]\ket{\Omega}\;.

(86)

Since the two branches are sewed at the final stage of the evolution, one can define the boundary correlators (
84
) as the late time limit of a bulk correlator (
85
) with arbitrary values of the indices
{\sf{a}}_{j}
:

\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{B}}(\bm{x}_{1},\ldots,\bm{x}_{n})=\lim_{\tau\to 0}\mathcal{C}_{{\sf{a}}_{1}\ldots{\sf{a}}_{n}}(\tau,\bm{x}_{1};\ldots;\tau,\bm{x}_{n})\;.

(87)

In practice, we are interested in the momentum space version of the boundary correlators defined as:

\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{B}}(\bm{k}_{1},\ldots,\bm{k}_{n})=\int\prod^{n}_{j=1}\mathrm{d}^{d}\bm{x}_{j}e^{i\sum_{j=1}^{n}\bm{x}_{j}\cdot\bm{k}_{j}}\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{B}}(\bm{x}_{1},\ldots,\bm{x}_{n})\;.

(88)

Most of the correlators we will consider actually vanish like power laws in this limit. Therefore, we will introduce a late-time regulator
-1\ll\tau_{0}<0
to factorise this leading behaviour and the physical result of interest.

Both path integrals encode the time evolution from the Bunch-Davies vacuum state via the action
S[\varphi]
of the theory. In order to enforce this initial condition, the time integration contour should be slightly tilted in the
\tau
complex plane. For the two branches of (
85
), this is achieved with the standard
i\epsilon
prescription:

S_{\pm}[\varphi_{\pm}]=\int^{0}_{-\infty_{\pm}}\frac{\mathrm{d}\tau\mathrm{d}^{d}\bm{x}}{(-H\tau)^{d+1}}\mathcal{L}(\varphi_{\pm},\varphi^{\prime}_{\pm},\partial_{j}\varphi_{\pm};\tau)\;.

(89)

Performing the Wick rotation to EAdS (
35
), one obtains:

\pm iS_{\pm}[\varphi_{\pm}]=e^{\pm\frac{i\pi(d+1)}{2}}S_{E}[\varphi^{E}_{\pm}]\;,

(90)

where
S_{E}[\varphi^{E}_{\pm}]
is the Euclidean action:

S_{E}[\varphi^{E}_{\pm}]=\int^{\infty}_{0}\frac{\mathrm{d}z\mathrm{d}^{d}\bm{x}}{(Hz)^{d+1}}\mathcal{L}(\varphi_{\pm}^{E},\partial_{z}\varphi_{\pm}^{E},i\partial_{j}\varphi^{E}_{\pm};z)\;,

(91)

and the
\pm
versions of the fields now live, respectively, on the positive and negative real axis in the complex
\tau
-plane. One can always perform these Wick rotations if the Lagrangian is an analytic function of conformal time. If not, the presence of poles would induce additional terms coming from the residues of the Lagrangian. However, such peculiar features are usually motivated by dS breaking setups, such that emergent non-locality (see, e.g.
Jazayeri:2023kji
), which are beyond the scope of the present work.

The resulting Euclidean path integral allows us to compute Euclidean correlation functions
\mathcal{C}^{E}_{{\sf{a}}_{1}\ldots{\sf{a}}_{n}}\left({}^{z_{1}\ldots z_{n}}_{\bm{x}_{1}\ldots\bm{x}_{n}}\right)
related to their Lorentzian counterparts by making use of the Wick rotation (
34
):

\mathcal{C}_{{\sf{a}}_{1}\ldots{\sf{a}}_{n}}\left({}^{\tau_{1}\ldots\tau_{n}}_{\bm{x}_{1}\ldots\bm{x}_{n}}\right)=\mathcal{C}_{{\sf{a}}_{1}\ldots{\sf{a}}_{n}}^{E}\left(-\tau_{1}e^{i{\sf{a}}_{1}\left(\frac{\pi}{2}-\epsilon\right)},\bm{x}_{1};\ldots;-\tau_{n}e^{i{\sf{a}}_{n}\left(\frac{\pi}{2}-\epsilon\right)},\bm{x}_{n}\right)\;.

(92)

Notice that it does not make sense to add correlators with different
{\sf{a}}_{j}
indices, since they do not live on the same copies of EAdS.
15
15

15

For instance, in the right panel of Fig.
4
,
\mathcal{O}_{1},\mathcal{O}_{3}
and
\mathcal{O}_{2},\mathcal{O}_{4}
are inserted on two distinct copies of EAdS.
However, it makes sense at the final Lorentzian time slice due to the boundary condition
\varphi_{+}(\tau_{0})=\varphi_{-}(\tau_{0})
.

\text{Re}(\tau)
\text{Im}(\tau)
+i\epsilon
-i\epsilon
\mathcal{O}_{1}^{+}
\mathcal{O}_{2}^{-}
\mathcal{O}_{3}^{+}
\mathcal{O}_{4}^{-}

\text{Re}(\tau)
\text{Im}(\tau)
+i\epsilon
-i\epsilon
\mathcal{O}_{1}^{+}
\mathcal{O}_{2}^{-}
\mathcal{O}_{3}^{+}
\mathcal{O}_{4}^{-}
\mathcal{O}_{1}^{+}
\mathcal{O}_{2}^{-}
\mathcal{O}_{3}^{+}
\mathcal{O}_{4}^{-}

Figure 4
:
Integration contour for the Schwinger-Keldysh path integral in Lorentzian (
left
panel) and Euclidean (
right
panel) regimes.

The path integral, being unaffected by the functional basis we use in order to express the fields, can be expressed in KLF space. This motivates the definition of the Euclidean correlators directly in terms of frequency and momentum variables:

\mathcal{G}_{{\sf{a}}_{1}\ldots{\sf{a}}_{n}}\left({}^{\mu_{1}\ldots\mu_{n}}_{\bm{k}_{1}\ldots\bm{k}_{n}}\right)=\left.\frac{(2\pi)^{d}}{\mathcal{N}_{\mu_{1}}}\frac{\delta}{\delta J_{{\sf{a}}_{1},\bm{k}_{1}}^{\mu_{1}}}\ldots\frac{(2\pi)^{d}}{\mathcal{N}_{\mu_{n}}}\frac{\delta}{\delta J_{{\sf{a}}_{n},\bm{k}_{n}}^{\mu_{n}}}Z[J_{+},J_{-}]\right|_{J_{\pm}=0}\;,

(93)

where the functional generator is defined in the usual way:

Z[J_{+},J_{-}]=\int^{\varphi_{+}=\varphi_{-}}_{\textrm{B.D.}}\mathcal{D}\varphi_{+}\mathcal{D}\varphi_{-}e^{iS_{+}[\varphi_{+}]-iS_{-}[\varphi_{-}]+\int_{\mathrm{KLF}}(J_{+}\varphi_{+}+J_{-}\varphi_{-})}\;.

(94)

These correlators are simply related to their spacetime version
\mathcal{C}^{E}_{{\sf{a}}_{1}\ldots{\sf{a}}_{n}}\left({}^{z_{1}\ldots z_{n}}_{\bm{x}_{1}\ldots\bm{x}_{n}}\right)
by an inverse KLF transform in each variable:

\mathcal{C}^{E}_{{\sf{a}}_{1}\ldots{\sf{a}}_{n}}\left({}^{z_{1}\ldots z_{n}}_{\bm{x}_{1}\ldots\bm{x}_{n}}\right)=\int_{\mathrm{KLF}_{n}}\prod^{n}_{j=1}\Phi^{\mu_{j}}_{\bm{k}_{j}}(z_{j},\bm{x}_{j})\mathcal{G}_{{\sf{a}}_{1}\ldots{\sf{a}}_{n}}\left({}^{\mu_{1}\ldots\mu_{n}}_{\bm{k}_{1}\ldots\bm{k}_{n}}\right)\;.

(95)

Since the
\mathcal{G}_{{\sf{a}}_{1}\ldots{\sf{a}}_{n}}\left({}^{\mu_{1}\ldots\mu_{n}}_{\bm{k}_{1}\ldots\bm{k}_{n}}\right)
are defined by functional differentiation, they do not have to be square-integrable functions in their KLF variables. We have already seen an example of this in section
3.4
: the two-point Whightman function is proportional to a KLF delta

\mathcal{G}^{\mathcal{O}}_{+-}\left({}^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}_{\bm{k}_{1}\bm{k}_{2}}\right)=\delta\left({}^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}_{\bm{k}_{1}-\bm{k}_{2}}\right)\frac{\rho_{\mathcal{O}}(\mu_{1})}{H^{2}\mathcal{N}_{\mu_{1}}}\;.

(96)

Furthermore, for the CFT, the spectral density itself is a sum of smooth and a distributional part, see Eq. (
83
).
In the next sections, we explicitly work out the example of the free theory where the functional generator can be explicitly evaluated, and that of simple interacting theories with polynomial interactions involving test fields in
\mathrm{dS}
.

4.2
Free Theories

Let us consider the free theory of a massive scalar field in dS:

S_{0}=\frac{1}{2}\int\frac{\mathrm{d}\tau\mathrm{d}^{d}\bm{x}}{(-H\tau)^{d-1}}\left[\left(\partial_{\tau}\varphi\right)^{2}-\left(\partial_{i}\varphi\right)^{2}-\frac{m_{\varphi}^{2}}{H^{2}\tau^{2}}\varphi^{2}\right]\;,

(97)

where the mass is related to the Casimir eigenvalue (
18
) as
m_{\varphi}=HM_{\mu_{\varphi}}
.
16
16

16

Since dS invariance forbids non diagonalisable quadratic mixings among fields, the construction also holds for multiple fields.
We first perform the Wick rotation (
90
) to EAdS, and then use the KLF decomposition (
52
) to obtain the action in KLF space:

\pm iS_{0,\pm}[\varphi]=-\frac{H^{2}e^{\pm\frac{i(d-1)\pi}{2}}}{2}\int_{\mathrm{KLF}}\varphi^{\mu}_{\bm{k}}(\mu^{2}-\mu_{\varphi}^{2})\varphi^{\mu}_{-\bm{k}}\;.

(98)

Since both actions are now expressed as integrals over the same set of variables, one can write their difference in a compact way using a vector notation:

iS_{+}[\varphi_{+}]-iS_{-}[\varphi_{-}]=-\frac{H^{2}}{2}\int_{\mathrm{KLF}_{2}}\bm{\varphi}^{T}\left({}^{\mu_{1}}_{\bm{k}_{1}}\right)\cdot\bm{\mathcal{D}}^{0}\left({}^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}_{\bm{k}_{1}\bm{k}_{2}}\right)\cdot\bm{\varphi}\left({}^{\mu_{2}}_{\bm{k}_{2}}\right)\;,

(99)

where we defined the vectors
\bm{\varphi}_{j}
and
\bm{\mathcal{D}}^{0}_{ij}
as

\varphi_{{\sf{a}}}\left({}^{\mu}_{\bm{k}}\right)=\varphi^{\mu}_{{\sf{a}},\bm{k}}\;,\quad\mathcal{D}^{0}_{{\sf{a}}_{1}{\sf{a}}_{2}}\left({}^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}_{\bm{k}_{1}\bm{k}_{2}}\right)=\delta_{{\sf{a}}_{1}{\sf{a}}_{2}}\delta\left({}^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}_{\bm{k}_{1}-\bm{k}_{2}}\right)(\mu_{1}^{2}-\mu_{\varphi}^{2})e^{\frac{i{\sf{a}}_{1}(d-1)\pi}{2}}\;.

(100)

The matrix
\bm{{\cal D}}^{0}_{ij}
