does not take the full quadratic sector into account, as there is a non trivial contribution from the future boundary condition that imposes that the two branches of the SK path integral are sewed at late time. This condition can be expressed as a field space
\delta-
function
Weinberg:2005vy
:

\delta\left[\varphi_{+}(0,\bm{x})-\varphi_{-}(0,\bm{x})\right]\propto\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{\epsilon}\int_{\Sigma_{0}}\mathrm{d}^{d}\bm{x}\left(\varphi_{+}(0,\bm{x})-\varphi_{-}(0,\bm{x})\right)^{2}\right)\;,

(101)

where
\Sigma_{0}
denotes the final spatial slice of the Poincaré patch. Since the harmonic functions vanish in the asymptotic future, we use the late-time regulator
0<z_{0}\ll 1
described above, and evaluate the harmonic functions on this time slice:

\int_{\Sigma_{z_{0}}}\mathrm{d}^{d}\bm{x}\left(\varphi_{+}(z_{0},\bm{x})-\varphi_{-}(z_{0},\bm{x})\right)^{2}=H^{2}\int_{\mathrm{KLF}_{2}}\bm{\varphi}^{T}\left({}^{\mu_{1}}_{\bm{k}_{1}}\right)\cdot\bm{C}\left({}^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}_{\bm{k}_{1}\bm{k}_{2}}\right)\cdot\bm{\varphi}\left({}^{\mu_{2}}_{\bm{k}_{2}}\right)\;,

(102)

where the matrix
\bm{C}
is defined as:

\bm{C}_{{\sf{a}}_{1}{\sf{a}}_{2}}\left({}^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}_{\bm{k}_{1}\bm{k}_{2}}\right)=\frac{{\sf{a}}_{1}{\sf{a}}_{2}}{H^{2}}(2\pi)^{d}\delta^{(d)}(\bm{k}_{1}+\bm{k}_{2})\Phi^{\mu_{1}}_{\bm{k}_{1}}(z_{0},\bm{0})\Phi^{\mu_{2}}_{\bm{k}_{2}}(z_{0},\bm{0})\;.

(103)

The functional generator of the free theory can now be completely rewritten in KLF space:

\displaystyle Z_{0}[J_{+},J_{-}]=\int_{\textrm{B.D.}}\mathcal{D}\varphi_{+}\mathcal{D}\varphi_{-}\exp\Bigg(-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathrm{KLF}_{2}}\bm{\varphi}^{T}\left({}^{\mu_{1}}_{\bm{k}_{2}}\right)\cdot\bm{\mathcal{D}}\left({}^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}_{\bm{k}_{1}\bm{k}_{2}}\right)\cdot\bm{\varphi}\left({}^{\mu_{2}}_{\bm{k}_{2}}\right)

(104)

\displaystyle+\int_{\mathrm{KLF}}\bm{J}^{T}\left({}^{\mu}_{\bm{k}}\right)\cdot\bm{\varphi}\left({}^{\mu}_{\bm{k}}\right)\Bigg)\;,

where the symbol
\bm{J}
uses the same vector notation as before and the matrix
\bm{\mathcal{D}}
is defined as the sum:

\bm{\mathcal{D}}\left({}^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}_{\bm{k}_{1}\bm{k}_{2}}\right)=H^{2}\left(\bm{\mathcal{D}}^{0}\left({}^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}_{\bm{k}_{1}\bm{k}_{2}}\right)+\frac{2}{\epsilon}\bm{C}\left({}^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}_{\bm{k}_{1}\bm{k}_{2}}\right)\right)\;.

(105)

The usual trick to evaluate this integral is to complete the square by shifting the field with a current-dependent quantity:

\bm{\varphi}\left({}^{\mu_{1}}_{\bm{k}_{1}}\right)=\bm{\psi}\left({}^{\mu_{1}}_{\bm{k}_{1}}\right)+\int_{\mathrm{KLF}}\bm{\mathcal{G}}_{f}\left({}^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}_{\bm{k}_{1}\bm{k}_{2}}\right)\cdot\bm{J}\left({}^{\mu_{2}}_{\bm{k}_{2}}\right)\;,

(106)

where the matrix
\bm{\mathcal{G}}^{f}
satisfies

\int_{\mathrm{KLF}}\bm{\mathcal{D}}\left({}^{\mu_{1}\;\mu}_{\bm{k}_{1}\;\bm{k}}\right)\cdot\bm{\mathcal{G}}_{f}\left({}^{\mu\;\mu_{2}}_{\bm{k}\;\bm{k}_{2}}\right)=\delta\left({}^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}_{\bm{k}_{1}\bm{k}_{2}}\right)\left(\mathds{1}_{2}+(\mu_{1}^{2}-\mu_{\varphi}^{2})\bm{f}(\mu_{1})\hat{\delta}_{\mu_{\varphi}}(\mu_{1})\right)\;,

(107)

and
f_{{\sf{a}}{\sf{b}}}(\mu)=\delta_{ab}f_{a}(\mu)
is an arbitrary diagonal matrix with
f_{\sf{a}}(\mu)
shadow-symmetric functions. Such an additional term is omitted in Minkowski as it turns out to be degenerate with contour prescriptions when going back to position space. As we will see, this also holds in our construction for fields in the principal series. However, we need to keep it in general.
Using (
106
), the path-integral in (
104
) can be computed to yield the functional generator:

Z_{0}[J_{+},J_{-}]=\underbrace{Z_{0}[0,0]}_{\braket{\Omega|\Omega}\equiv 1}\exp\left(\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathrm{KLF}_{2}}\bm{J}^{T}\cdot\bm{\mathcal{G}}_{f}\cdot\bm{J}\right)\;.

(108)

The extra factor is the current-less functional integral over the field variables
\psi_{\pm}
without any operator insertion. By definition, this is the norm of the Bunch-Davies vacuum state, which we took to be one. From Eq. (
108
) and using the general definition (
93
),
\mathcal{G}_{f}
coincides with the 2-point KLF correlator:

\mathcal{G}^{\mu_{\varphi}}_{f,{\sf{a}}_{1}{\sf{a}}_{2}}\left({}^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}_{\bm{k}_{1}\bm{k}_{2}}\right)=\left.\frac{(2\pi)^{d}}{\mathcal{N}_{\mu_{1}}}\frac{\delta}{\delta J^{\mu_{1}}_{{\sf{a}}_{1},k_{1}}}\frac{(2\pi)^{d}}{\mathcal{N}_{\mu_{2}}}\frac{\delta}{\delta J^{\mu_{2}}_{{\sf{a}}_{2},k_{2}}}Z[J_{+},J_{-}]\right|_{J_{\pm}=0}\;.

(109)

The components of the matrix
\bm{\mathcal{G}}_{f}
can be found by solving equation (
107
). In the limit where
\epsilon
goes to zero, this gives two sets of equations:

\int_{\mathrm{KLF}}\bm{\mathcal{D}}_{0}\left({}^{\mu_{1}\mu}_{\bm{k}_{1}\bm{k}}\right)\cdot\bm{\mathcal{G}}_{f}\left({}^{\mu\;\mu_{2}}_{\bm{k}\;\bm{k}_{2}}\right)=\frac{1}{H^{2}}\delta\left({}^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}_{\bm{k}_{1}\bm{k}_{2}}\right)\left(\mathds{1}_{2}+(\mu_{1}^{2}-\mu_{\varphi}^{2})\bm{f}(\mu_{1})\hat{\delta}_{\mu_{\varphi}}(\mu_{1})\right)

(110)

and

\int_{\mathrm{KLF}}\bm{C}\left({}^{\mu_{1}\mu}_{\bm{k}_{1}\bm{k}}\right)\cdot\bm{\mathcal{G}}_{f}\left({}^{\mu\;\mu_{2}}_{\bm{k}\;\bm{k}_{2}}\right)=0\;.

(111)

Diagonal propagators.

Let us start with the diagonal part of (
110
):

(\mu_{1}^{2}-\mu_{\varphi}^{2})\mathcal{G}_{f,{\sf{a}}{\sf{a}}}^{\mu_{\varphi}}\left({}^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}_{\bm{k}_{1}\bm{k}_{2}}\right)=\frac{e^{-{\sf{a}}\frac{i\pi(d-1)}{2}}}{H^{2}}\delta\left({}^{\mu_{1}\;\mu_{2}}_{\bm{k}_{1}-\bm{k}_{2}}\right)\left(1+(\mu_{1}^{2}-\mu_{\varphi}^{2})\hat{\delta}_{\mu_{\varphi}}(\mu_{1})f_{{\sf{a}}}(\mu_{1})\right)\;.

(112)

The solution is given by:

\mathcal{G}^{\mu_{\varphi}}_{f,{\sf{a}}{\sf{a}}}\left({}^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}_{\bm{k}_{1}\bm{k}_{2}}\right)=\frac{1}{H^{2}}\delta\left({}^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}_{\bm{k}_{1}-\bm{k}_{2}}\right)\mathcal{G}^{\mu_{\varphi}}_{f,{\sf{a}}{\sf{a}}}(\mu_{1})\;,

(113)

where we defined the reduced propagator as:

\mathcal{G}^{\mu_{\varphi}}_{f,{\sf{a}}{\sf{a}}}(\mu)\equiv e^{-\frac{i{\sf{a}}\pi(d-1)}{2}}\left(\frac{1}{\mu^{2}-\mu_{\varphi}^{2}}+\hat{\delta}_{\mu_{\varphi}}(\mu)f_{{\sf{a}}}(\mu)\right)\;.

(114)

The corresponding position-space objects
\mathcal{C}_{{\sf{a}}{\sf{a}}}^{E}(X_{1}^{E},X_{2}^{E})
we find by applying the inverse transformation (
95
) are nothing but the (anti-)time-ordered Green functions such that:

\left(H^{2}\Box_{\mathrm{EAdS}}-m_{\varphi}^{2}\right)\mathcal{C}_{{\sf{a}}{\sf{a}}}^{E}(X_{1}^{E},X_{2}^{E})=(Hz_{1})^{d+1}\delta(z_{1}-z_{2})\delta^{(d)}(\bm{x}_{1}-\bm{x}_{2})\;.

(115)

We stress that this holds for any choice of functions
f_{\sf{a}}(\mu)
, and that the latter only affect boundary conditions.

Like in flat spacetime, the reduced two-point function (
114
) has poles when the propagating quanta goes on-shell, i.e. at
\mu=\pm\mu_{\varphi}
. Therefore, for principal series fields, the inverse KLF transform must be regulated with an appropriate
i\epsilon
prescription. As explained in appendix
B
, the usual Feynman prescription breaks the shadow symmetry. Thus, the only way to proceed is to add up two pole prescriptions as:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\mu^{2}-\mu_{\varphi}^{2}}\to

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(\mu^{2}-\mu_{\varphi}^{2})^{g}_{i{\sf{a}}\epsilon}}\equiv\frac{g(\mu_{\varphi})}{\mu^{2}-\mu_{\varphi}^{2}+i{\sf{a}}\epsilon}+\frac{g(-\mu_{\varphi})}{\mu^{2}-\mu_{\varphi}^{2}-i{\sf{a}}\epsilon}\;,

(116)

in terms of a function
g(\mu)
. Like for the functions
f_{\sf{a}}(\mu)
above, it should be determined by imposing appropriate boundary conditions. In order to produce the (anti-)time ordered propagators, it is shown in appendix
B
that there is a large freedom in the choice of these functions. The simplest choice turns out to be:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{crc}f_{{\sf{a}}}(\mu)&=&0\\
g(\mu)&=&\frac{e^{\pi\mu}}{2\sinh(\pi\mu)}\end{array}\right.\;.

(117)

This makes manifest the possibility of absorbing the effect of the functions
f_{{\sf{a}}}(\mu)
in the contour prescription for heavy fields. As a result, we recover the same convention as
Melville:2023kgd
;
Werth:2024mjg
:

\frac{1}{(\mu^{2}-\mu_{\varphi}^{2})_{i{\sf{a}}\epsilon}}\equiv\frac{i}{2\sinh(\pi\mu_{\varphi})}\left(\frac{e^{+\pi\mu_{\varphi}}}{\mu^{2}-\mu_{\varphi}^{2}+i{\sf{a}}\epsilon}-\frac{e^{-\pi\mu_{\varphi}}}{\mu^{2}-\mu_{\varphi}^{2}-i{\sf{a}}\epsilon}\right)\;.

(118)

In the case of non-principal fields where
\mu_{\varphi}=i\nu_{\varphi}
with real
\nu_{\varphi}
, the poles are located on the imaginary axis and there is no need for a particular contour prescription. Because of this, the boundary condition is fully encoded in the functions
f_{\sf{a}}
. It is shown in appendix
B
that the (anti-)time ordered propagators are defined by the following choice:

f_{{\sf{a}}}(i\nu_{\varphi})=-i{\sf{a}}\frac{e^{i{\sf{a}}\pi|\nu_{\varphi}|}}{\mathcal{N}_{i\nu_{\varphi}}}\;.

(119)

Physically, we need this additional contribution compared to principal series fields because the (anti-)time ordered SK propagators do not define a Green function in
L^{2}
. As a result, the reduced propagator should include non-principal contributions, as we have seen in section
3.4
.

Now that the functions
f_{{\sf{a}}}
and
g
are known, we will remove their explicit mentions in the propagators. Our conventions for the KLF (anti-)time ordered propagators are thus expressed as:

\mathcal{G}^{\mu_{\varphi}}_{{\sf{a}}{\sf{a}}}\left({}^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}_{\bm{k}_{1}\bm{k}_{2}}\right)=\frac{1}{H^{2}}\delta\left({}^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}_{\bm{k}_{1}\bm{k}_{2}}\right)\mathcal{G}^{\mu_{\varphi}}_{{\sf{a}}{\sf{a}}}(\mu_{1})\;,

(120)

where the reduced propagators
\mathcal{G}^{\mu_{\varphi}}_{{\sf{a}}{\sf{a}}}(\mu)
are of the form:

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{{\sf{a}}{\sf{a}}}^{\mu_{\varphi}}(\mu)

\displaystyle=e^{-\frac{i{\sf{a}}\pi(d-1)}{2}}\frac{1}{(\mu^{2}-\mu_{\varphi}^{2})_{i{\sf{a}}\epsilon}}\quad\textrm{Principal Series}

(121)

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{{\sf{a}}{\sf{a}}}^{i\nu_{\varphi}}(\mu)

\displaystyle=e^{-\frac{i{\sf{a}}\pi(d-1)}{2}}\left(\frac{1}{\mu^{2}+\nu_{\varphi}^{2}}-i{\sf{a}}\frac{e^{i{\sf{a}}\pi|\nu_{\varphi}|}}{\mathcal{N}_{i\nu_{\varphi}}}\hat{\delta}_{i\nu_{\varphi}}(\mu)\right)\quad\textrm{Other UIRs}\;.

We show in appendix
B
that this is consistent with the usual expression of the (anti-) time ordered propagators in Lorentzian position space (see e.g.
DiPietro:2021sjt
):

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{{\sf{a}}{\sf{a}}}(\tau_{1},\bm{x}_{1};\tau_{2},\bm{x}_{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{H^{2}}\int_{\mathrm{KLF}}\Phi^{\mu}_{\bm{k}}\left(-\tau_{1}e^{i{\sf{a}}\left(\frac{\pi}{2}-\epsilon\right)},\bm{x}_{1}\right)\Phi^{\mu}_{-\bm{k}}\left(-\tau_{2}e^{i{\sf{a}}\left(\frac{\pi}{2}-\epsilon\right)},\bm{x}_{2}\right)\mathcal{G}_{{\sf{a}}{\sf{a}}}^{\mu_{\varphi}}(\mu)

(122)

\displaystyle=W_{\mu_{\varphi}}(\sigma^{\textrm{dS}}-i{\sf{a}}\epsilon)\;,

where
W_{\mu_{\varphi}}(\sigma)
is defined in equation (
232
),
\sigma^{\textrm{dS}}
is defined in (
10
), and the
i\epsilon
prescription is here to regulate the propagator for time-like separations, where
W_{\mu_{\varphi}}(\sigma)
has a branch-cut.

Off-diagonal propagators.

The off-diagonal part of (
110
) gives the following constraint

(\mu_{1}^{2}-\mu_{\varphi}^{2})\mathcal{G}^{f}_{-{\sf{a}}{\sf{a}}}\left({}^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}_{\bm{k}_{1}\bm{k}_{2}}\right)=(\mu_{2}^{2}-\mu_{\varphi}^{2})\mathcal{G}^{f}_{-{\sf{a}}{\sf{a}}}\left({}^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}_{\bm{k}_{1}\bm{k}_{2}}\right)=0\;,

(123)

which is the KLF-space counterpart of the Casimir equation. On the other hand, Eq. (
111
) gives

\int\displaylimits^{\infty}_{-\infty}\mathrm{d}\mu\mathcal{N}_{\mu}\Phi^{\mu_{1}}_{\bm{k}_{1}}(z_{0},\bm{0})\Phi^{\mu}_{-\bm{k}_{1}}(z_{0},\bm{0})\left(\mathcal{G}_{-{\sf{a}}{\sf{a}}}\left({}^{\;\;\mu\;\;\mu_{2}}_{-\bm{k}_{1}\bm{k}_{2}}\right)-\mathcal{G}_{{\sf{a}}{\sf{a}}}\left({}^{\;\;\mu\;\;\mu_{2}}_{-\bm{k}_{1}\bm{k}_{2}}\right)\right)=0\;.

(124)

We deduce from (
123
) that
\mathcal{G}_{-{\sf{a}}{\sf{a}}}
is proportional to the distribution
\hat{\delta}_{\mu_{\varphi}}(\mu_{1})
. Besides, Eq. (
124
) implies that the propagator has the same dependence as in (
113
). Therefore, the off-diagonal two-point function can be expressed as

\mathcal{G}^{\mu_{\varphi}}_{-{\sf{a}}{\sf{a}}}\left({}^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}_{\bm{k}_{1}\bm{k}_{2}}\right)=\frac{1}{H^{2}}\delta\left({}^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}_{\bm{k}_{1}-\bm{k}_{2}}\right)\frac{\hat{\delta}_{\mu_{\varphi}}(\mu_{1})}{\mathcal{N}_{\mu_{1}}}\mathcal{F}_{-{\sf{a}}{\sf{a}}}^{\mu_{\varphi}}.

(125)

In the insert below, we show that boundary conditions impose
\mathcal{F}_{-{\sf{a}}{\sf{a}}}^{\mu_{\varphi}}=1
. To summarise, the KLF space Wightman function takes the same reduced form as the (anti-)time ordered propagators:

\mathcal{G}^{\mu_{\varphi}}_{-{\sf{a}}{\sf{a}}}\left({}^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}_{\bm{k}_{1}\bm{k}_{2}}\right)=\frac{1}{H^{2}}\delta\left({}^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}_{\bm{k}_{1}\bm{k}_{2}}\right)\mathcal{G}^{\mu_{\varphi}}_{-{\sf{a}}{\sf{a}}}(\mu_{1})\;,

(126)

where
\mathcal{G}^{\mu_{\varphi}}_{-{\sf{a}}{\sf{a}}}(\mu)
is identified as:

\mathcal{G}_{\pm\mp}^{\mu_{\varphi}}(\mu)=\frac{\hat{\delta}_{\mu_{\varphi}}(\mu)}{\mathcal{N}_{\mu}}\;.

(127)

The position-space version of this propagator can be found in appendix
B
to be:

\mathcal{C}_{-{\sf{a}}{\sf{a}}}(\tau_{1},\bm{x}_{1};\tau_{2},\bm{x}_{2})=W_{\mu_{\varphi}}(\sigma^{\textrm{dS}}-i{\sf{a}}\epsilon\;\textrm{sgn}(\tau_{1}-\tau_{2}))\;.

(128)

In this insert, we use boundary conditions to determine
\mathcal{F}_{-{\sf{a}}{\sf{a}}}^{\mu_{\varphi}}
in (
125
). For this, we plug this equation into (
124
) and perform the
\mu_{1}
integral, yielding:

\Phi^{\mu_{1}}_{\bm{k}_{1}}(z_{0},\bm{0})\Phi^{\mu_{2}}_{-\bm{k}_{1}}(z_{0},\bm{0})\hat{\delta}_{\mu_{\varphi}}(\mu_{2})\mathcal{F}_{-{\sf{a}}{\sf{a}}}^{\mu_{\varphi}}=\mathcal{N}_{\mu_{2}}\Phi^{\mu_{1}}_{\bm{k}_{1}}(z_{0},\bm{0})\Phi^{\mu_{2}}_{-\bm{k}_{1}}(z_{0},\bm{0})\mathcal{G}^{\mu_{\varphi}}_{{\sf{a}}{\sf{a}}}(\mu_{2})\;.

(129)

Since this equation is true for any values of the KLF variables, it can be evaluated at
\mu_{1}=\mu_{2}\equiv\mu
. Then, we integrate over the frequency and get:

\Phi^{\mu_{\varphi}}_{\bm{k}_{1}}(z_{0},\bm{0})\Phi^{\mu_{\varphi}}_{-\bm{k}_{1}}(z_{0},\bm{0})\mathcal{F}^{\mu_{\varphi}}_{-{\sf{a}}{\sf{a}}}=\int\displaylimits^{\infty}_{-\infty}\mathrm{d}\mu\mathcal{N}_{\mu}\Phi^{\mu}_{\bm{k}_{1}}(z_{0},\bm{0})\Phi^{\mu}_{-\bm{k}_{1}}(z_{0},\bm{0})\mathcal{G}_{{\sf{a}}{\sf{a}}}^{\mu_{\varphi}}(\mu)\;.

(130)

With the time-ordered boundary conditions encoded in the choice of the functions
f_{{\sf{a}}}
and
g
discussed above, it is shown in appendix
B
that the right-hand side is:

\int\displaylimits^{\infty}_{-\infty}\mathrm{d}\mu\mathcal{N}_{\mu}\Phi^{\mu}_{\bm{k}_{1}}(z_{0},\bm{0})\Phi^{\mu}_{-\bm{k}_{1}}(z_{0},\bm{0})\mathcal{G}_{{\sf{a}}{\sf{a}}}^{\mu_{\varphi}}(\mu)=\Phi^{\mu_{\varphi}}_{\bm{k}_{1}}(z_{0},\bm{0})\Phi^{\mu_{\varphi}}_{-\bm{k}_{1}}(e^{-i{\sf{a}}\pi}z_{0},\bm{0})\;.

(131)

Therefore, equation (
130
) becomes:

\Phi^{\mu_{\varphi}}_{\bm{k}_{1}}(z_{0},\bm{0})\Phi^{\mu_{\varphi}}_{-\bm{k}_{1}}(z_{0},\bm{0})\mathcal{F}^{\mu_{\varphi}}_{-{\sf{a}}{\sf{a}}}=\Phi^{\mu_{\varphi}}_{\bm{k}_{1}}(z_{0},\bm{0})\Phi^{\mu_{\varphi}}_{-\bm{k}_{1}}(e^{-i{\sf{a}}\pi}z_{0},\bm{0})\;.

(132)

At first sight, equation (
132
) has no solution. This is because we try to compare two propagators living on different copies of EAdS. Since the late-time boundary condition is imposed at
\tau_{0}
, Eq. (
132
) is valid either in the exact limit where
\tau_{0},z_{0}\equiv 0
, or after performing the inverse rotation to Lorentzian signature. As the harmonic functions are singular in the late time limit, we pick the second option and apply the rotations (
45
), consistently with the signs of the indices in (
92
). Since the left-hand side lives on the branch
-{\sf{a}}{\sf{a}}
, the rotation gives:

\displaystyle\Phi^{\mu_{\varphi}}_{\bm{k}_{1}}(z_{0},\bm{0})\Phi^{\mu_{\varphi}}_{-\bm{k}_{1}}(z_{0},\bm{0})

\displaystyle\equiv\Phi^{\mu_{\varphi}}_{\bm{k}_{1}}\left(-\tau_{0}e^{-\frac{i{\sf{a}}\pi}{2}},\bm{0}\right)\Phi^{\mu_{\varphi}}_{-\bm{k}_{1}}\left(-\tau_{0}e^{\frac{i{\sf{a}}\pi}{2}},\bm{0}\right)

(133)

\displaystyle=H^{d+1}u^{\sf{a}}_{k_{1}}(\tau_{0},\mu)u^{-{\sf{a}}}_{k_{1}}(\tau_{0},\mu)\;.

On the other hand, the right-hand side lives on the
{\sf{a}}{\sf{a}}
branch, so the rotation reads:

\displaystyle\Phi^{\mu_{\varphi}}_{\bm{k}_{1}}(z_{0},\bm{0})\Phi^{\mu_{\varphi}}_{-\bm{k}_{1}}\left(e^{-ia\pi}z_{0},\bm{0}\right)

\displaystyle\equiv\Phi^{\mu_{\varphi}}_{\bm{k}_{1}}\left(-\tau_{0}e^{\frac{i{\sf{a}}\pi}{2}},\bm{0}\right)\Phi^{\mu_{\varphi}}_{-\bm{k}_{1}}\left(-\tau_{0}e^{-\frac{i{\sf{a}}\pi}{2}},\bm{0}\right)

(134)

\displaystyle=H^{d+1}u^{-{\sf{a}}}_{k_{1}}(\tau_{0},\mu)u^{{\sf{a}}}_{k_{1}}(\tau_{0},\mu)\;.

One thus deduces that the consistent normalisation is:

\mathcal{F}_{-{\sf{a}}{\sf{a}}}^{\mu_{\varphi}}=1\;.

(135)

4.3
Polynomial Interactions

Let us now turn to interactions. First, let us consider an
n
-point polynomial interaction in a single-field theory:

S_{I}[\varphi]=-\lambda\int\frac{\mathrm{d}\tau\mathrm{d}^{d}\bm{x}}{(-H\tau)^{d+1}}\varphi^{n}\;.

(136)

As in the free-theory case, we perform the Wick rotation to
\mathrm{EAdS}
and we expand the fields in KLF modes. The actions on the two branches are therefore expressed as:

\displaystyle\pm iS_{I}[\varphi]=-\frac{\lambda H^{\frac{(n-2)(d+1)}{2}}e^{\pm\frac{i(d+1)\pi}{2}}}{\pi^{\frac{n}{2}}}\int_{\mathrm{KLF}_{n}}(2\pi)^{d}\delta^{(d)}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{n}\bm{k}_{j}\right)\mathcal{I}^{\mu_{1}\ldots\mu_{n}}_{k_{1}\ldots k_{n}}\prod_{j=1}^{n}\varphi^{\mu_{j}}_{\bm{k}_{j}}\;,

(137)

where we defined the
n
-point vertex function as an integral over
n
functions
K_{i\mu}
:

\mathcal{I}^{\mu_{1}\ldots\mu_{n}}_{k_{1}\ldots k_{n}}=\int^{\infty}_{0}\mathrm{d}z\;z^{\frac{d(n-2)}{2}-1}K_{i\mu_{1}}(k_{1}z)\ldots K_{i\mu_{n}}(k_{n}z)\;.

(138)

Similar-looking quantities arise in in-out computations in flat space, with harmonic functions replaced by
4
-momentum plane waves, yielding an energy-conserving delta function, in addition to the
3
-momentum conservation.
However, in flat space, the true conceptual analogues of the frequencies
\mu_{j}
, labelling UIRs, are the masses
m_{j}
of the particles rather than their energies
\omega_{j}
. The nonconservation of mass at vertices in flat space has a counterpart here: the nonconservation of
\mu
’s at vertices, which is precisely encoded in the vertex functions (
138
). The latter are purely kinematic quantities, independent of the nature of the interacting fields, and are central in our formalism.

The integrals (
138
) naturally
arise when evaluating the late-time
n
-point function generated by a contact interaction between
n
general massive scalars
Sleight:2019hfp
.
They are known in terms of Lauricella hypergeometric series
Lauricella1893SulleFI
. However, their evaluation usually requires analytic continuation, as the physical domain does not always fall within the convergence domain of the series.
Besides, as we show in section
4.4
and as we will see with examples in section
5
, computing cosmological correlators also requires to analytically continue the vertex functions (
138
) for complex
\mu
, i.e. outside the principal series. This is not problematic: from their writing as Lauricella series, we know that
\mathcal{I}^{\mu_{1}\ldots\mu_{n}}_{k_{1}\ldots k_{n}}
is meromorphic in the
\mu_{j}
planes, which also allows us to deform the KLF contour integrations and compute correlators by collecting residues, see section
5.2
.

In the simplest case,
n=3
, the vertex function reads:

\mathcal{I}^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\mu_{3}}_{k_{1}k_{2}k_{3}}=\int^{\infty}_{0}\mathrm{d}z\;z^{\frac{d}{2}-1}K_{i\mu_{1}}(k_{1}z)K_{i\mu_{2}}(k_{2}z)K_{i\mu_{3}}(k_{3}z)\;.

(139)

These integrals, known as triple-
K
integrals, are known to arise in the evaluation of the conformal three-point function in momentum space
Bzowski:2012ih
;
Bzowski:2013sza
;
Bzowski:2015pba
;
Bzowski:2015yxv
;
Bzowski:2017poo
. They can be expressed in terms of the function Appell
F_{4}

Bzowski:2013sza
, but whose series representation only converges when the momenta violate the triangle inequality, see appendix
E
. Therefore, the evaluation of these integrals requires a suitable analytical continuation of the function
F_{4}
to the physical domain. For generic complex
\mu
’s, the (late-time) convergence of (
139
) requires that
|\text{Im}(\mu_{1})|+|\text{Im}(\mu_{2})|+|\text{Im}(\mu_{3})|<\frac{d}{2}
.
In the opposite regime, the vertex function admits a unique analytical continuation.
Furthermore, by expanding the integrand around
z\sim 0
, one sees that analyticity is violated at the points such that:

\frac{d}{2}\pm i\mu_{1}\pm i\mu_{2}\pm i\mu_{3}\in-2\;\mathbb{N}\;.

(140)

This manifests as simple poles in the analytically continued vertex function
Bzowski:2015yxv
.

Eventually, notice that
the above construction can be trivially extended to multifield theories. For instance, for a cubic interaction of the type
\mathcal{L}_{I}=-\frac{1}{2}g\varphi^{2}\chi
the KLF-space version of the interaction action reads:

\pm iS_{I}=-\frac{gH^{\frac{d+1}{2}}e^{\pm\frac{i(d+1)\pi}{2}}}{2\pi^{\frac{3}{2}}}\int_{\mathrm{KLF}_{3}}(2\pi)^{d}\delta^{(d)}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{3}\bm{k}_{j}\right)\mathcal{I}^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\mu_{3}}_{\bm{k}_{1},\bm{k}_{2},\bm{k}_{3}}\,\varphi^{\mu_{1}}_{\bm{k}_{1}}\varphi^{\mu_{2}}_{\bm{k}_{2}}\chi^{\mu_{3}}_{\bm{k}_{3}}\;.

(141)

4.4
KLF Feynman Rules

Now that we have simple expressions for the KLF space propagators and vertices, let us explain how to use this in order to write every possible perturbative Feynman diagram. In the path integral formalism, the standard way is to evaluate the functional generator as an asymptotic series in the interacting action. In this picture, the vacuum and the dynamical evolution are those of the free theory and the interactions are viewed as operator insertions in the Gaussian path integral
Chen:2017ryl
:

\displaystyle Z[J_{+},J_{-}]

\displaystyle=\braket{0|e^{iS_{I}^{+}[\varphi_{+}]-iS_{I}^{-}[\varphi_{-}]+\int_{\mathrm{KLF}}J_{+}\varphi_{+}+J_{-}\varphi_{-}}|0}

(142)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n!}\left(iS_{I}^{+}\left[\frac{\delta}{\delta J_{+}}\right]-iS_{I}^{-}\left[\frac{\delta}{\delta J_{-}}\right]\right)^{n}\,Z_{0}[J_{+},J_{-}]\;,

where
\ket{0}
is the Bunch-Davies vacuum associated with the free theory
S_{0}
, and
S_{I}=S-S_{0}
is the interacting action. This is fully equivalent to the operator formalism where the fields variables are in interaction picture
Weinberg:2005vy
;
Chen:2017ryl
.

The KLF correlators can then be obtained by applying the definition (
93
), leading to the following set of Feynman rules:

•

A line in a diagram denotes the propagation of a free particle. Therefore, it corresponds to a free propagator:

\displaystyle\hskip-22.76228pt=\frac{1}{H^{2}}\delta\left({}^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}_{\bm{k}_{1}-\bm{k}_{2}}\right)\mathcal{G}_{{\sf{a}}{\sf{b}}}^{\mu_{\varphi}}(\mu_{1})\;.

(143)

•

An
n
-point vertex living on the branch
{\sf{a}}=\pm
is expressed by a momentum-conserving delta function and a vertex function
\mathcal{I}^{\mu_{1}\ldots\mu_{n}}_{\bm{k}_{1}\ldots\bm{k}_{n}}
:

\displaystyle\hskip-22.76228pt=-i\,{\sf{a}}\,\mathcal{C}\,\lambda(2\pi)^{d}\delta^{(d)}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{n}\bm{k}_{j}\right)\frac{H^{\frac{(n-2)(d+1)}{2}}e^{{\sf{a}}\frac{i\pi d}{2}}}{\pi^{\frac{n}{2}}}\mathcal{I}^{\mu_{1}\ldots\mu_{n}}_{k_{1}\ldots k_{n}}\,,

(144)

where
\mathcal{C}
is a combinatorial factor fixed by the form of the interaction term.

•

A KLF space correlator
\mathcal{G}_{{\sf{a}}_{1}\ldots{\sf{a}}_{n}}\left({}^{\mu_{1}\ldots\mu_{n}}_{\bm{k}_{1}\ldots\bm{k}_{n}}\right)
is obtained by setting the external point to the corresponding branch and summing over the possible branch
{\sf{a}}^{I}_{j}
of the internal vertices.

•

All the frequency and momenta attached to the internal lines should be integrated over with the KLF measure. At tree level, the internal momentum integrals are straightforward because of momentum conservation at vertices. However, due to the lack of frequency conservation, even tree-level diagrams feature one frequency integral per internal line.

According to these rules, a diagram can be represented as:

\mathcal{G}_{{\sf{a}}_{1}\ldots{\sf{a}}_{n}}\left({}^{\mu_{1}\ldots\mu_{n}}_{\bm{k}_{1}\ldots\bm{k}_{n}}\right)=\underbrace{(2\pi)^{d}\delta^{(d)}\left(\sum_{m=1}^{n}\bm{k}_{m}\right)}_{\textrm{Momentum Conservation}}\sum_{\{a^{V}_{E}\}}\mathcal{G}_{\{a_{E}^{V}\}}\left({}^{\mu_{1}\ldots\mu_{n}}_{\bm{k}_{1}\ldots\bm{k}_{n}}\right)\;,

(145)

where

\mathcal{G}_{\{a_{E}^{V}\}}\left({}^{\mu_{1}\ldots\mu_{n}}_{\bm{k}_{1}\ldots\bm{k}_{n}}\right)=\underbrace{\left(\prod_{j=1}^{n_{E}}\prod_{\ell=1}^{n_{j}}\frac{\mathcal{G}^{\mu_{\varphi_{\ell}}}_{{\sf{a}}_{\ell}{\sf{a}}_{j}}(\mu_{\ell})}{H^{2}}\right)}_{\textrm{External Legs}}\underbrace{\mathcal{G}^{A}_{\{{\sf{a}}^{V}_{E}\}}\left({}^{\mu_{1}\ldots\mu_{n}}_{\bm{k}_{1}\ldots\bm{k}_{n}}\right)}_{\textrm{Amputated Diagram}}\;.

(146)

The amputated diagram
\mathcal{G}^{A}_{\{{\sf{a}}^{V}_{E}\}}\left({}^{\mu_{1}\ldots\mu_{n}}_{\bm{k}_{1}\ldots\bm{k}_{n}}\right)
is labeled by a configuration of
n_{E}
external vertices
{\sf{a}}_{j}\in\{{\sf{a}}^{V}_{E}\}
to which
n_{j}
external legs are attached. Consistently, we have
\sum_{j=1}^{n_{E}}n_{j}=n
and the
{\sf{a}}_{\ell}
refer to the external legs of
\mathcal{G}_{{\sf{a}}_{1}\ldots{\sf{a}}_{n}}\left({}^{\mu_{1}\ldots\mu_{n}}_{\bm{k}_{1}\ldots\bm{k}_{n}}\right)
.

This object represents the non trivial part of the KLF correlator. It implicitly contains a sum over all possible internal vertices
\{{\sf{a}}^{V}_{I}\}
and integrals over every internal frequency and momentum
\{\mu_{I},\bm{k}_{I}\}
. For instance, in the case of a four-point function with three external vertices
\{{\sf{a}}_{E}^{V}\}=\{{\sf{a}}_{1},{\sf{a}}_{2},{\sf{a}}_{4}\}
, the amputated diagram can be represented as:

(147)

Finally, the corresponding bulk correlator is obtained by successively applying the KLF inverse transformation (
95
) and the Wick rotation (
92
). See appendix
B
and section
5.3
for applications of this procedure, respectively, to the free propagator and its one-loop correction.

Boundary Correlators.

Let us now explain how to extract the boundary correlator
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{B}}\left(\bm{k}_{1},\ldots,\bm{k}_{n}\right)
from the KLF space correlator
\mathcal{G}_{{\sf{a}}_{1}\ldots{\sf{a}}_{n}}(^{\mu_{1}\ldots\mu_{n}}_{\bm{k}_{1}\ldots\bm{k}_{n}})
. As explained above, they are defined as the late time limit of bulk correlators with any value of the external SK indices. In order to simplify things, we make use of this freedom to turn all the external legs in (
146
) into off-diagonal propagators. Following (
145
), the contribution of this diagram thus reads:

\mathcal{G}_{\{a_{E}^{V}\}}\left({}^{\mu_{1}\ldots\mu_{n}}_{\bm{k}_{1}\ldots\bm{k}_{n}}\right)\equiv\underbrace{\left(\prod_{j=1}^{n_{E}}\prod_{\ell=1}^{n_{j}}\frac{\hat{\delta}_{\mu_{\varphi_{\ell}}}(\mu_{\ell})}{H^{2}\mathcal{N}_{\mu_{\ell}}}\right)}_{\textrm{External Legs}}\underbrace{\mathcal{G}^{A}_{\{{\sf{a}}_{E}^{V}\}}\left({}^{\mu_{1}\ldots\mu_{n}}_{\bm{k}_{1}\ldots\bm{k}_{n}}\right)}_{\textrm{Amputated Correlator}}\;.

(148)

Then, as stated before, we apply the inverse KLF transform (
95
) and the Wick rotation (
92
) in the limit where
\tau_{0}\to 0
. Since the external legs are fully expressed in terms of
\hat{\delta}_{\mu_{\varphi_{j}}}
, the frequency integrals are straightforward to perform. Their effect is to send the KLF frequency of the amputated diagram
\mathcal{G}^{A}_{\{{\sf{a}}^{V}_{E}\}}\left({}^{\mu_{1}\ldots\mu_{n}}_{\bm{k}_{1}\ldots\bm{k}_{n}}\right)
on-shell and to add an extra factor
\mathcal{E}_{\{{\sf{a}}_{E}^{V}\}}(\tau_{0})
coming from the late time limit of the harmonic function and the Wick rotation. Diagrammatically, we will denote the contribution coming from a single external line attached to an external vertex
{\sf{a}}\in\{{\sf{a}}^{V}_{E}\}
as:

\displaystyle\hbox to51.46pt{\vbox to14.51pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 22.96228pt\lower-11.31044pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
\par{}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -31.5 0 L 31.5 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope \par{}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 31.5 0 M 35.65 0 C 35.65 2.29 33.79 4.15 31.5 4.15 C 29.2 4.15 27.35 2.29 27.35 0 C 27.35 -2.29 29.2 -4.15 31.5 -4.15 C 33.79 -4.15 35.65 -2.29 35.65 0 Z M 31.5 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}{{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 31.5 0 M 35.65 0 C 35.65 2.29 33.79 4.15 31.5 4.15 C 29.2 4.15 27.35 2.29 27.35 0 C 27.35 -2.29 29.2 -4.15 31.5 -4.15 C 33.79 -4.15 35.65 -2.29 35.65 0 Z M 31.5 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{20.3595pt}{-7.97743pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -31.5 -2.77 M -31.5 -2.77 L -31.5 2.77 L -25.96 2.77 L -25.96 -2.77 Z M -25.96 2.77}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

\displaystyle=\frac{H^{\frac{d-3}{2}}}{\sqrt{\pi}}e^{-\frac{i{\sf{a}}\pi d}{4}}(-\tau_{0})^{\frac{d}{2}}K_{i\mu_{\varphi}}(-k\tau_{0}e^{-\frac{i{\sf{a}}\pi}{2}})\;,

(149)

where, as customary, a
denotes an evaluation at the boundary time
\tau_{0}
.
The full factor
\mathcal{E}_{\{{\sf{a}}_{E}^{V}\}}(\tau_{0})
is then given by the product of all the external line contributions:

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\{{\sf{a}}_{E}^{V}\}}(\tau_{0})

\displaystyle=\prod_{j=1}^{n_{E}}\prod_{\ell=1}^{n_{j}}\left(\hbox to53.57pt{\vbox to30.94pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 22.96228pt\lower-14.17155pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
\par{}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope \par{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {{}{}{{

{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-14.54193pt}{6.49423pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\par{}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 31.5 0 M 35.65 0 C 35.65 2.29 33.79 4.15 31.5 4.15 C 29.2 4.15 27.35 2.29 27.35 0 C 27.35 -2.29 29.2 -4.15 31.5 -4.15 C 33.79 -4.15 35.65 -2.29 35.65 0 Z M 31.5 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}{{{}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}}{{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 31.5 0 M 35.65 0 C 35.65 2.29 33.79 4.15 31.5 4.15 C 29.2 4.15 27.35 2.29 27.35 0 C 27.35 -2.29 29.2 -4.15 31.5 -4.15 C 33.79 -4.15 35.65 -2.29 35.65 0 Z M 31.5 0}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{18.25272pt}{-7.97745pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -31.5 -2.77 M -31.5 -2.77 L -31.5 2.77 L -25.96 2.77 L -25.96 -2.77 Z M -25.96 2.77}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\right)

(150)

\displaystyle=\frac{H^{\frac{n(d-3)}{2}}}{\pi^{\frac{n}{2}}}(-\tau_{0})^{\frac{nd}{2}}\prod_{j=1}^{n_{E}}e^{-\frac{in_{j}{\sf{a}}_{j}\pi d}{4}}\prod_{\ell=1}^{n_{j}}K_{i\mu_{\varphi_{\ell}}}(-k_{\ell}\tau_{0}e^{-\frac{i{\sf{a}}_{j}\pi}{2}})\;,

where
{\sf{a}}_{j}\in\{{\sf{a}}^{V}_{E}\}
,
n_{j}
is the number of external legs attached to it, and we have
\sum_{j=1}^{n_{E}}n_{j}=n
.
Thus, the full momentum-space contribution, labeled by the set
\{{\sf{a}}^{V}_{E}\}
, to a late-time dS correlator involving fields with masses
\{\mu_{\varphi_{j}}\}_{j=1}^{n}
, is:

F^{\varphi_{1}\ldots\varphi_{n}}_{\{{\sf{a}}^{V}_{E}\}}\left(\bm{k}_{1}\ldots\bm{k}_{n}\right)=(2\pi)^{d}\delta^{(d)}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{n}\bm{k}_{j}\right)\mathcal{E}_{\{{\sf{a}}_{E}^{V}\}}(\tau_{0})\mathcal{G}^{A}_{\{{\sf{a}}^{V}_{E}\}}\left({}^{\mu_{\varphi_{1}}\ldots\mu_{\varphi_{n}}}_{\bm{k}_{1}\;\ldots\;\bm{k}_{n}}\right)\;.

(151)

The complete boundary correlator is then given by the sum over all values of the set
\{{\sf{a}}_{E}^{V}\}
:

\mathcal{C}^{\varphi_{1}\ldots\varphi_{n}}_{\mathcal{B}}(\bm{k}_{1},\ldots,\bm{k}_{n})\equiv\sum_{\{{\sf{a}}^{V}_{E}\}}F^{\varphi_{1}\ldots\varphi_{n}}_{\{{\sf{a}}_{E}^{V}\}}\left(\bm{k}_{1},\ldots,\bm{k}_{n}\right)\;.

(152)

In most applications, we will consider the correlators of
n
identical conformally coupled fields with
\mu_{\varphi}=i/2
, for which the Bessel function reads:

K_{\frac{1}{2}}(z)=\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{2z}}e^{-z}\;.

(153)

In this case, the contribution from the external legs can be written as:

\lim_{\tau_{0}\to 0}\mathcal{E}_{\{{\sf{a}}_{E}^{V}\}}(\tau_{0})=\frac{H^{\frac{n(d-3)}{2}}}{2^{\frac{n}{2}}}\frac{(-\tau_{0})^{\frac{n(d-1)}{2}}}{(k_{1}\ldots k_{n})^{\frac{1}{2}}}e^{-\frac{i\pi(d-1)}{4}\sum_{j=1}^{n_{E}}n_{j}{\sf{a}}_{j}}\;,\quad\textrm{c.c. external legs}

(154)

5
Applications with Cubic Interactions

Let us see some applications of these Feynman rules in three situations.

5.1
Boundary Three-Point Function

Let us start with the boundary three-point function of conformally coupled scalars. In a
\frac{1}{3!}\lambda\varphi^{3}
self-interacting theory, the set
\{a^{V}_{E}\}
only contains a single vertex
{\sf{a}}
and the different indices are
n=3
,
n_{E}=1
and
n_{j=1}=3
. The only relevant diagrams are:

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{7.56598pt}{-33.33987pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}=-\frac{i{\sf{a}}H^{\frac{d+1}{2}}\lambda e^{\frac{i{\sf{a}}\pi d}{2}}}{\pi^{\frac{3}{2}}}\mathcal{I}^{\mu_{\varphi}\mu_{\varphi}\mu_{\varphi}}_{k_{1}k_{2}k_{3}}\;.

(157)

One can now use the very simple form of the Bessel function (
153
) to evaluate the vertex function as:

\mathcal{I}^{\mu_{\varphi}\mu_{\varphi}\mu_{\varphi}}_{k_{1}k_{2}k_{3}}=\frac{\pi^{\frac{3}{2}}}{2\sqrt{2}}\frac{k_{t}^{\frac{3-d}{2}}}{\sqrt{k_{1}k_{2}k_{3}}}\Gamma\left(\frac{d-3}{2}\right)\;,

(158)

where we defined the total energy as
k_{t}=k_{1}+k_{2}+k_{3}
. Notice that the vertex function is singular at
d=3
, highlighting the need of using dimensional regularisation. As we now show, this divergence is canceled by destructive interferences among the two channels, so that the full correlator remains finite.

Omitting the momentum-conserving delta function, the contribution from the channel
{\sf{a}}
is:

F^{\mu_{\varphi}}_{\sf{a}}(\bm{k}_{1},\bm{k}_{2},\bm{k}_{3})=-\frac{i{\sf{a}}\lambda H^{2(d-2)}k_{t}^{\frac{3-d}{2}}}{8k_{1}k_{2}k_{3}}e^{\frac{i{\sf{a}}\pi(3-d)}{4}}(-\tau_{0})^{\frac{3}{2}(d-1)}\Gamma\left(\frac{d-3}{2}\right)\;.

(159)

The full boundary three-point function is now given by the sum of the two channels:

\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{B}}(\bm{k}_{1},\bm{k}_{2},\bm{k}_{3})=-\frac{\lambda}{4}H^{2(d-2)}(-\tau_{0})^{\frac{3}{2}(d-1)}\frac{k_{t}^{\frac{3-d}{2}}}{k_{1}k_{2}k_{3}}\sin\left(\frac{\pi(d-3)}{4}\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{d-3}{2}\right)\;.

(160)

One can now take the limit
d\to 3
to obtain a finite result in agreement with the standard in-in computation
Creminelli:2011mw
:

\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{B}}(\bm{k}_{1},\bm{k}_{2},\bm{k}_{3})=\frac{\lambda H^{2}\pi}{8}\frac{\tau_{0}^{3}}{k_{1}k_{2}k_{3}}\;.

(161)

5.2
Boundary Four-Point Function

Let us now consider a two-field theory with the cubic interaction:

\mathcal{L}_{I}=-\frac{g}{2}\varphi^{2}\chi\;,

(162)

where the
1/2
factor implies that there are no combinatorial factors in the vertex. As before, we take external fields
\varphi
to be conformally coupled. We consider the second field
\chi
to lie in the principal series, with other UIRs simply following by analytical continuation. The tree-level diagrams contributing to the boundary four-point function are labeled by a set of two external vertices
\{{\sf{a}}^{V}_{E}\}=\{{\sf{a}},{\sf{b}}\}
and are of the form:

\displaystyle F_{{\sf{a}}{\sf{b}}}^{\mu_{\varphi}}(\left\{\bm{k}_{j}\right\})=

\displaystyle\hbox to96.81pt{\vbox to73.38pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 48.4048pt\lower-36.68854pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -39.37 23.62 L -19.69 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}

{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{19.80046pt}{26.41109pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{}{}{{

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{19.80046pt}{-30.49443pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0.1211,0.4648,0.707}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0.1211,0.4648,0.707}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-12.5551pt}{12.18471pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{E}_{{\sf{a}}{\sf{b}}}(\tau_{0})\mathcal{G}^{A}_{{\sf{a}}{\sf{b}}}\left({}^{\mu_{\varphi}\mu_{\varphi}\mu_{\varphi}\mu_{\varphi}}_{\bm{k}_{1}\,\bm{k}_{2}\,\bm{k}_{3}\,\bm{k}_{4}}\right)\;,

(163)

where we omitted the momentum-conserving delta function for simplicity. The contribution from the external legs is simply given by applying (
154
) with
n_{E}=2
,
n_{1}=n_{2}=2
and
n=4
:

\lim_{\tau_{0}\to 0}\mathcal{E}_{{\sf{a}}{\sf{b}}}(\tau_{0})=\frac{H^{2(d-3)}}{4}\frac{(-\tau_{0})^{2(d-1)}}{\sqrt{k_{1}k_{2}k_{3}k_{4}}}e^{-\frac{i\pi(d-1)}{2}({\sf{a}}+{\sf{b}})}\;.

(164)

On the other hand, the amputated diagram is given by the spectral integration :

\mathcal{G}^{A}_{{\sf{a}}{\sf{b}}}\left({}^{\mu_{\varphi}\mu_{\varphi}\mu_{\varphi}\mu_{\varphi}}_{\bm{k}_{1}\,\bm{k}_{2}\,\bm{k}_{3}\,\bm{k}_{4}}\right)=-\frac{{\sf{a}}{\sf{b}}g^{2}H^{d-1}}{\pi^{3}}e^{\frac{i({\sf{a}}+{\sf{b}})\pi d}{2}}\int\displaylimits^{\infty}_{-\infty}\mathrm{d}\mu\,\mathcal{N}_{\mu}\,\mathcal{I}^{\mu_{\varphi}\mu_{\varphi}\mu}_{k_{1}\;k_{2}\;k_{I}}\,\mathcal{I}^{\mu\;\mu_{\varphi}\mu_{\varphi}}_{k_{I}\;k_{3}\;k_{4}}\,\mathcal{G}^{\mu_{\chi}}_{{\sf{a}}{\sf{b}}}(\mu)\;,

(165)

where
\mathcal{G}^{\mu_{\chi}}_{{\sf{a}}{\sf{b}}}
is the KLF propagator of the field
\chi
and
\bm{k}_{I}=(\bm{k}_{1}+\bm{k}_{2})
is the exchanged momentum. Like in the previous example, the simple form of the Bessel function (
153
) for a conformally coupled field allows us to evaluate the vertex function as:

\mathcal{I}^{\mu_{\varphi}\mu_{\varphi}\mu}_{k_{1}\;k_{2}\;k_{I}}=\frac{\pi^{2}}{8}\frac{2^{\frac{d}{2}}\Gamma\left(\frac{d-2}{2}\right)}{\sqrt{k_{1}k_{2}}k_{I}^{\frac{d-2}{2}}}\frac{C_{i\mu-\frac{d-2}{2}}^{\left(\frac{d-2}{2}\right)}(u^{-1})}{i\sinh\left(\pi\mu+\frac{i\pi d}{2}\right)}\;,

(166)

where
C_{\nu}^{(\alpha)}(z)
is the Gegenbauer function (see (
282
)) and we defined the two cross-ratios
u
and
v
as:

u=\frac{k_{I}}{k_{1}+k_{2}}\;,\quad v=\frac{k_{I}}{k_{1}+k_{2}}\;.

(167)

In the physical region, one has
0\leq u,v\leq 1
.

Factorised contributions.

When the two SK indices
{\sf{a}}
and
{\sf{b}}
are opposite, the KLF propagator is given by (
127
). Consequently, the corresponding amputated diagram is factorised:

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}^{A}_{-{\sf{a}}{\sf{a}}}\left({}^{\mu_{\varphi}\mu_{\varphi}\mu_{\varphi}\mu_{\varphi}}_{\bm{k}_{1}\;\bm{k}_{2}\;\bm{k}_{3}\;\bm{k}_{4}}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{g^{2}H^{d-1}}{\pi^{3}}\mathcal{I}^{\mu_{\varphi}\mu_{\varphi}\mu_{\chi}}_{k_{1}\;k_{2}\;k_{I}}\mathcal{I}^{\mu_{\chi}\mu_{\varphi}\mu_{\varphi}}_{k_{I}\;k_{3}\;k_{4}}

(168)

\displaystyle=-\frac{g^{2}H^{d-1}\pi}{2^{6-d}}\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{d-2}{2}\right)^{2}}{\sqrt{k_{1}k_{2}k_{3}k_{4}}k_{I}^{d-2}}\frac{C_{i\mu_{\chi}-\frac{d-2}{2}}^{\left(\frac{d-2}{2}\right)}(u^{-1})C_{i\mu_{\chi}-\frac{d-2}{2}}^{\left(\frac{d-2}{2}\right)}(v^{-1})}{\sinh^{2}\left(\pi\mu_{\chi}+\frac{i\pi d}{2}\right)}\;.

This expression is analytic in the dimension
d
. The spurious second-order pole at
d=2
coming from the Gamma factor is cancelled by some zeros in the Gegenbauer function. Since this expression does not depend on
{\sf{a}}
, one can simply sum up the
+-
and
-+
contributions as:

F_{+-}^{\mu_{\varphi}}+F_{-+}^{\mu_{\varphi}}=-\frac{g^{2}H^{3d-7}\pi}{2^{7-d}}\frac{(-\tau_{0})^{2(d-1)}\Gamma\left(\frac{d-2}{2}\right)^{2}}{k_{1}k_{2}k_{3}k_{4}k_{I}^{d-2}}\frac{C_{i\mu_{\chi}-\frac{d-2}{2}}^{\left(\frac{d-2}{2}\right)}(u^{-1})C_{i\mu_{\chi}-\frac{d-2}{2}}^{\left(\frac{d-2}{2}\right)}(v^{-1})}{\sinh^{2}\left(\pi\mu_{\chi}+\frac{i\pi d}{2}\right)}\;.

(169)

Nested
++
component: KLF integral.

The case where
{\sf{a}}={\sf{b}}
is more complicated since one has to carry out a non-trivial spectral integration. However, because the function
\mu\mapsto\mathcal{I}^{\mu_{\varphi}\mu_{\varphi}\mu}_{k_{1}\;k_{2}\;k_{I}}
given in (
166
) is meromorphic, the spectral integral can be computed by collecting its poles. Analytical properties of
\mathcal{I}^{\mu_{\varphi}\mu_{\varphi}\mu}_{k_{1}\;k_{2}\;k_{I}}
and other useful relations are detailed in Appendices
D
and
F.1
. We focus on the
\mathcal{G}^{A}_{++}
component, since
\mathcal{G}^{A}_{--}
can be obtained by complex conjugation.

From the reduced propagator (
121
) and the vertex functions (
166
), one finds:

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}^{A}_{++}\left({}^{\mu_{\varphi}\mu_{\varphi}\mu_{\varphi}\mu_{\varphi}}_{\bm{k}_{1}\;\bm{k}_{2}\;\bm{k}_{3}\;\bm{k}_{4}}\right)=

\displaystyle\frac{g^{2}H^{d-1}e^{\frac{i\pi(d+1)}{2}}}{2^{6-d}}\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{d-2}{2}\right)^{2}}{\sqrt{k_{1}k_{2}k_{3}k_{4}}k_{I}^{d-2}}

(170)

\displaystyle\times\int\displaylimits^{\infty}_{-\infty}\mathrm{d}\mu\,\frac{\mu\,\sinh\left(\pi\mu\right)}{\sinh\left(\pi\mu+\frac{i\pi d}{2}\right)^{2}}\frac{C_{i\mu-\frac{d-2}{2}}^{\left(\frac{d-2}{2}\right)}(u^{-1})C_{i\mu-\frac{d-2}{2}}^{\left(\frac{d-2}{2}\right)}(v^{-1})}{(\mu^{2}-\mu_{\chi}^{2})_{i\epsilon}}\,.

As Gegenbauer functions have an exponentially growing large
\mu
asymptotic behaviour outside the real axis (see Appendix
F.1
), the writing (
170
) is not convenient if one wants to perform a contour integration to use the residue theorem. Hence, we rewrite the integrand in terms of Legendre functions of the second kind (defined in (
283
), with the asymptotic behaviour given in (
285
)), using the connection formula (
287
). The correlator (
170
) can thus be expressed in terms of the functions
Q_{\pm i\mu-\frac{1}{2}}^{-\frac{d-3}{2}}
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}^{A}_{++}\left({}^{\mu_{\varphi}\mu_{\varphi}\mu_{\varphi}\mu_{\varphi}}_{\bm{k}_{1}\;\bm{k}_{2}\;\bm{k}_{3}\;\bm{k}_{4}}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{g^{2}H^{d-1}e^{-\frac{i\pi(d+1)}{2}}}{4\sqrt{k_{1}k_{2}k_{3}k_{4}}k_{I}^{d-2}}\left(u^{-2}-1\right)^{\frac{3-d}{4}}\left(v^{-2}-1\right)^{\frac{3-d}{4}}

(171)

\displaystyle\times\left(\hat{F}_{++}^{0}+\hat{F}_{++}^{1}\right)\,,

where

\hat{F}_{++}^{0}\equiv\frac{1}{\pi}\int\displaylimits^{\infty}_{-\infty}\mathrm{d}\mu\,\frac{\mu}{\sinh\left(\pi\mu\right)}\frac{Q_{i\mu-\frac{1}{2}}^{\frac{d-3}{2}}\left(u^{-1}\right)Q_{i\mu-\frac{1}{2}}^{\frac{d-3}{2}}\left(v^{-1}\right)}{(\mu^{2}-\mu_{\chi}^{2})_{i\epsilon}}\,,

(172)

\hat{F}_{++}^{1}\equiv-\frac{1}{\pi}\int\displaylimits^{\infty}_{-\infty}\mathrm{d}\mu\,\frac{\mu}{\sinh\left(\pi\mu\right)}\frac{Q_{i\mu-\frac{1}{2}}^{\frac{d-3}{2}}\left(u^{-1}\right)Q_{-i\mu-\frac{1}{2}}^{\frac{d-3}{2}}\left(v^{-1}\right)}{(\mu^{2}-\mu_{\chi}^{2})_{i\epsilon}}\,.

(173)

and we used that
\hat{F}_{++}^{0}
and
\hat{F}_{++}^{1}
are symmetric under
u\leftrightarrow v
(for the latter, this can be checked by changing variables
\mu\leftrightarrow-\mu
in the integrand).

We now focus on the computation of
\hat{F}_{++}^{0}
and
\hat{F}_{++}^{1}
. Different terms in the above integrands have poles that contribute to
\mathcal{G}^{A}_{++}
. The analytic structures of the integrands are shown in Fig.
5
and are detailed in Appendix
F.1
. In the following, we call signal residues the ones that come from
\mu\mapsto\left(\mu^{2}-\mu_{\chi}^{2}\right)_{i\epsilon}^{-1}
in
\hat{F}_{++}^{0}
and
\hat{F}_{++}^{1}
, and we will denote
\hat{F}_{++}^{S}
their two contributions.
All other residues in
\hat{F}_{++}^{0}
and
\hat{F}_{++}^{1}
are called background residues, and we gather them in the term
\hat{F}_{++}^{B}
such that

\hat{F}_{++}\equiv\hat{F}_{++}^{0}+\hat{F}_{++}^{1}=\hat{F}_{++}^{B}+\hat{F}_{++}^{S}\,.

(174)

\text{Re}(\mu)
\text{Im}(\mu)
Q_{i\mu-\frac{1}{2}}^{\frac{d-3}{2}}\left(u^{-1}\right)
Q_{i\mu-\frac{1}{2}}^{\frac{d-3}{2}}\left(v^{-1}\right)
\frac{\mu}{\sinh\left(\pi\mu\right)}
\left(\mu^{2}-\mu_{\chi}^{2}\right)_{i\varepsilon}^{-1}

\text{Re}(\mu)
\text{Im}(\mu)
Q_{i\mu-\frac{1}{2}}^{\frac{d-3}{2}}\left(u^{-1}\right)
Q_{-i\mu-\frac{1}{2}}^{\frac{d-3}{2}}\left(v^{-1}\right)
\frac{\mu}{\sinh\left(\pi\mu\right)}
\left(\mu^{2}-\mu_{\chi}^{2}\right)_{i\varepsilon}^{-1}

Figure 5
:
Analytic structure in the
\mu
complex plane of the integrands of
\hat{F}_{++}^{0}
, Eq. (
172
) (left) and
\hat{F}_{++}^{1}
, Eq. (
173
) (right). To perform the integrals, we always close the contour with a half-circle in the lower half-plane for
\hat{F}_{++}^{0}
. For
\hat{F}_{++}^{1}
, the contour is closed in the lower (resp. upper) half-plane for
u<v
(resp.
u>v
).

Integration contour.

In the
\mu
-complex plane, we complete the integration contour with a half-circle, with a radius that is sent to infinity. To choose whether we close the contour in the upper or lower half-plane, let us examine the asymptotic behaviours of the integrands at
\mu\rightarrow+\infty
.
From the knowledge of the asymptotic expansion of
Q_{\pm i\mu-\frac{d-2}{2}}^{\frac{d-3}{2}}(x)
(
285
), we have in the integrand of
\hat{F}_{++}^{0}
(
172
):

Q_{i\mu-\frac{1}{2}}^{\frac{d-3}{2}}(u^{-1})\,Q_{i\mu-\frac{1}{2}}^{\frac{d-3}{2}}(v^{-1})\underset{\mu\rightarrow+\infty}{\propto}e^{-i\mu(\xi_{u}+\xi_{v})}\,,

(175)

where we defined
\xi_{u}\,,\,\xi_{v}>0
such that
u^{-1}\equiv\cosh(\xi_{u})
and
v^{-1}\equiv\cosh(\xi_{v})
. As for the factor
\mu/\sinh(\pi\mu)
, it decays exponentially fast outside the imaginary axis, and simply oscillates there. As a result, for
\hat{F}_{++}^{0}
, we close the contour in the lower half-plane.
For the Legendre functions entering
\hat{F}_{++}^{1}
(
173
), we have

Q_{i\mu-\frac{1}{2}}^{\frac{d-3}{2}}(u^{-1})\,Q_{-i\mu-\frac{1}{2}}^{\frac{d-3}{2}}(v^{-1})\underset{\mu\rightarrow+\infty}{\propto}e^{-i\mu(\xi_{u}-\xi_{v})}\,.

(176)

Here, the contour prescription thus depends on the ordering of
u
and
v
. In the following we choose
u<v
, and then also close the contour in the lower half-plane of
\mu
.

Equipped with this contour prescription for
\hat{F}_{++}
, which is summarised in Fig.
5
, we can collect the residues coming from
\hat{F}_{++}^{0}
and
\hat{F}_{++}^{1}
.
Let us start by the contribution coming from background residues, i.e.
\hat{F}_{++}^{B}
.
Here, it appears that we have to make a distinction according to
d
.

Background residues for
d\in\mathbb{C}\setminus(2\mathbb{Z})
.

When
d
is a generic complex quantity that is not an even integer, the background residues in the integrals (
172
) and (
173
) come from poles of
{\color[rgb]{0.1211,0.4648,0.707}\mu/\sinh(\pi\mu)}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.8398,0.1523,0.1563}Q_{\pm i\mu-\frac{1}{2}}^{\frac{d-3}{2}}\left(x\right)}

(with
x=u^{-1},v^{-1}
) along the imaginary axis (see Fig.
5
). The different contributions are the following:

•

Poles from
\frac{\mu}{\sinh(\pi\mu)}
in
\hat{F}_{++}^{0}
.

Given the contour prescription, the only background poles that contribute to
\hat{F}_{++}^{0}
are the poles from
\mu\mapsto\frac{\mu}{\sinh(\pi\mu)}
(see (
298
)) that are located in the lower half-plane:

\hat{F}_{++}^{0}\supset-\frac{2}{\pi}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{n}\frac{n+1}{(n+1)^{2}+\mu_{\chi}^{2}}Q_{n+\frac{1}{2}}^{\frac{d-3}{2}}(u^{-1})Q_{n+\frac{1}{2}}^{\frac{d-3}{2}}(v^{-1})\,.

(177)

•

Poles from
\frac{\mu}{\sinh(\pi\mu)}
in
\hat{F}_{++}^{1}
.

Similarly, given the contour for
\hat{F}_{++}^{1}
, poles from
\frac{\mu}{\sinh(\pi\mu)}
in the lower half-plane contribute as

\hat{F}_{++}^{1}\supset+\frac{2}{\pi}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{n}\frac{n+1}{(n+1)^{2}+\mu_{\chi}^{2}}Q_{n+\frac{1}{2}}^{\frac{d-3}{2}}(u^{-1})Q_{-n-\frac{3}{2}}^{\frac{d-3}{2}}(v^{-1})\,.

(178)

Now, we observe that the sum of these first two contributions (
177
), (
178
) vanishes. Indeed, the expression obtained by the sum is a series that involves the difference
Q_{n+\frac{1}{2}}^{\frac{d-3}{2}}(v^{-1})-Q_{-n-\frac{3}{2}}^{\frac{d-3}{2}}(v^{-1})
, which vanishes according to the connection formula between associated Legendre functions for
d
not an even integer (
286
)-(
293
). Therefore, the background term
\hat{F}_{++}^{B}
will only be given by its last contribution.

•

Poles from
Q_{-i\mu-\frac{1}{2}}^{\frac{d-3}{2}}(v^{-1})
in
\hat{F}_{++}^{1}
.

The last and only contribution to background poles is then from the residues (
300
) of
\mu\mapsto Q_{-i\mu-\frac{1}{2}}^{\frac{d-3}{2}}(v^{-1})
in
\hat{F}_{++}^{1}
(
\mu\mapsto Q_{i\mu-\frac{1}{2}}^{\frac{d-3}{2}}(u^{-1})
does not contribute since its poles are located in the upper half-plane).

Finally, the background term is

\displaystyle\hat{F}_{++}^{B}

\displaystyle=e^{i\pi(d+1)}u^{d-2}\left[\left(u^{-2}-1\right)\left(v^{-2}-1\right)\right]^{\frac{d-3}{4}}

(179)

\displaystyle\times\sum_{n=0}^{+\infty}\frac{(-1)^{n}}{\left(n+\frac{d-2}{2}\right)^{2}+\mu_{\chi}^{2}}\left(\frac{u}{v}\right)^{n}\frac{\Gamma(n+d-2)}{\Gamma(n+1)}

\displaystyle\times\,_{2}F_{1}\left(\begin{matrix}\frac{1-n}{2}\,,\,-\frac{n}{2}\\
-n-\frac{d-4}{2}\end{matrix}\,,v^{2}\right)\,_{2}F_{1}\left(\begin{matrix}\frac{n+d-1}{2}\,,\,\frac{n+d-2}{2}\\
n+\frac{d}{2}\end{matrix}\,,u^{2}\right)\,.

Background residues for
d
even integer.

When the the spatial dimension
d
is even, the function
\mu\mapsto Q_{\pm i\mu-\frac{1}{2}}^{\frac{d-3}{2}}(x)
,
x>1
, is analytic in the complex
\mu
-plane, see Appendix (
F.1
). Consequently, the background term
\hat{F}_{++}^{B}
is given by the sum of the two contributions (
177
) and (
178
), which is now non-vanishing (except for a finite number of terms), due to the Legendre functions connection formula (
297
) that holds when
d
is an even integer. We then obtain
17
17

17

For numerical evaluation, it is convenient to use the form (
283
) for the function
Q_{n+\frac{1}{2}}^{\frac{d-3}{2}}
.

\hat{F}_{++}^{B}=+2i\sum_{n=K-1}^{+\infty}(-1)^{n}\frac{\Gamma(n+1+K)}{\Gamma(n+2-K)}\frac{n+1}{(n+1)^{2}+\mu_{\chi}^{2}}P_{n+\frac{1}{2}}^{-\frac{d-3}{2}}\left(u^{-1}\right)\,Q_{n+\frac{1}{2}}^{\frac{d-3}{2}}\left(v^{-1}\right)\,,

(180)

where
K\equiv\frac{d-2}{2}
,
K\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
when
d
is even. Remarkably, it turns out that Eq. (
179
) has a smooth limit when
d
approaches an even integer, and that this limit coincides with (
180
), which was not guaranteed a priori.

Signal residues.

We now collect the signal residues, a reasoning that does not depend on
d
.
Using the
i\epsilon
-prescription (
118
), which has poles and residues given in (
303
), we obtain
\hat{F}_{++}^{S}
from the following two contributions:

•

Poles from
\frac{1}{\left(\mu^{2}-\mu_{\chi}^{2}\right)_{i\epsilon}}
in
\hat{F}_{++}^{0}
.

From the contour prescription, the two signal poles that are located in the lower half-plane contribute to
\hat{F}_{++}^{0}
:

\displaystyle\hat{F}_{++}^{0}\supset-\frac{i}{2\sinh(\pi\mu_{\chi})^{2}}

\displaystyle\biggl(e^{\pi\mu_{\chi}}\,Q_{i\mu_{\chi}-\frac{1}{2}}^{\frac{d-3}{2}}(u^{-1})Q_{i\mu_{\chi}-\frac{1}{2}}^{\frac{d-3}{2}}(v^{-1})

(181)

\displaystyle+e^{-\pi\mu_{\chi}}\,Q_{-i\mu_{\chi}-\frac{1}{2}}^{\frac{d-3}{2}}(u^{-1})Q_{-i\mu_{\chi}-\frac{1}{2}}^{\frac{d-3}{2}}(v^{-1})\biggr)\,.

•

Poles from
\frac{1}{\left(\mu^{2}-\mu_{\chi}^{2}\right)_{i\epsilon}}
in
\hat{F}_{++}^{1}
.

The signal poles coming from
\hat{F}_{++}^{1}
are those located in the lower half-plane:

\displaystyle\hat{F}_{++}^{1}\supset\frac{i}{2\sinh(\pi\mu_{\chi})^{2}}

\displaystyle\biggl(e^{\pi\mu_{\chi}}\,Q_{-i\mu_{\chi}-\frac{1}{2}}^{\frac{d-3}{2}}(v^{-1})Q_{i\mu_{\chi}-\frac{1}{2}}^{\frac{d-3}{2}}(u^{-1})

(182)

\displaystyle+e^{-\pi\mu_{\chi}}\,Q_{i\mu_{\chi}-\frac{1}{2}}^{\frac{d-3}{2}}(v^{-1})Q_{-i\mu_{\chi}-\frac{1}{2}}^{\frac{d-3}{2}}(u^{-1})\biggr)\,.

The sum of these two terms can be simplified using the Legendre functions connection formula (
286
) and the analytic continuation formula for the Legendre function of the first kind (
288
). Finally, the signal term
\hat{F}_{++}^{S}
is

\hat{F}_{++}^{S}=-\frac{e^{i\pi(d-3)}}{2}\left(\Gamma\left(\frac{d-2}{2}\pm i\mu_{\chi}\right)\right)^{2}P_{i\mu_{\chi}-\frac{1}{2}}^{-\frac{d-3}{2}}(e^{+i\pi}u^{-1})\,P_{i\mu_{\chi}-\frac{1}{2}}^{-\frac{d-3}{2}}(v^{-1})\,.

(183)

Final result.

Gathering the expressions of the prefactor
\mathcal{E}_{++}(\tau_{0})
(
164
) and of the amputated diagram
\mathcal{G}^{A}_{++}
(
171
), the final result for
F_{++}
(
163
) is given by

\displaystyle F_{++}^{\mu_{\varphi}}(\left\{\bm{k}_{j}\right\})

\displaystyle=\frac{\bar{g}^{2}H^{2d-2}(-\tau_{0})^{2(d-1)}e^{-\frac{3i\pi}{2}(d-1)}}{16k_{1}k_{2}k_{3}k_{4}k_{I}^{d-2}}\left(u^{-2}-1\right)^{\frac{3-d}{4}}\left(v^{-2}-1\right)^{\frac{3-d}{4}}

(184)

\displaystyle\times\left(\hat{F}_{++}^{B}+\hat{F}_{++}^{S}\right)\;,

where
\hat{F}_{++}^{B}
is given by (
179
) (or (
180
) for
d
an even integer),
\bar{g}=H^{-\frac{5-d}{2}}g
is the dimensionless coupling, and
\hat{F}_{++}^{S}
is given in (
183
).
The result found in
Werth:2024mjg
is recovered for
d=3
.

5.3
Bulk Two-Point Function at One Loop

g\varphi\chi_{1}\chi_{2}
and define the dimensionless coupling
\bar{g}=H^{-\frac{5-d}{2}}g
. The one-loop correction to the
\{{\sf{a}}_{1},{\sf{a}}_{2}\}

\varphi
is given by the sum over vertices
\{{\sf{a}}^{V}_{E}\}=\{{\sf{b}}_{1},{\sf{b}}_{2}\}
of the bubble diagram:

(185)

\displaystyle=(2\pi)^{d}\delta^{(d)}(\bm{k}_{1}+\bm{k}_{2})\sum_{{\sf{b}}_{1},{\sf{b}}_{2}}\frac{\mathcal{G}^{\mu_{\varphi}}_{{\sf{a}}_{1}{\sf{b}}_{1}}(\mu_{1})}{H^{2}}\mathcal{G}^{A}_{{\sf{b}}_{1}{\sf{b}}_{2}}\left({}^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}_{k_{1}k_{2}}\right)\frac{\mathcal{G}^{\mu_{\varphi}}_{{\sf{b}}_{2}{\sf{a}}_{2}}(\mu_{2})}{H^{2}}\;.

First, let us look at the amputated diagram:

\mathcal{G}^{A}_{{\sf{a}}{\sf{b}}}\left({}^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}_{k_{1}k_{2}}\right)=-\frac{{\sf{a}}{\sf{b}}g^{2}H^{d-3}}{\pi^{3}}e^{\frac{i\pi({\sf{a}}+{\sf{b}})d}{2}}\int\displaylimits^{\infty}_{-\infty}\mathrm{d}\mu\mathcal{N}_{\mu}\mathrm{d}\mu^{\prime}\mathcal{N}_{\mu^{\prime}}\mathcal{Q}_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}(\mu,\mu^{\prime})\mathcal{G}_{{\sf{a}}{\sf{b}}}^{\chi_{1}}(\mu)\mathcal{G}_{{\sf{a}}{\sf{b}}}^{\chi_{2}}(\mu^{\prime})\;,

(186)

where the loop momentum integral is:

\mathcal{Q}_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}(\mu,\mu^{\prime})=\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{d}\bm{p}}{(2\pi)^{d}}\mathcal{I}^{\mu_{1}\mu\mu^{\prime}}_{k_{1}\;p|\bm{k}_{1}-\bm{p}|}\mathcal{I}^{\mu_{2}\mu\,\mu^{\prime}}_{k_{1}\,p|\bm{k}_{1}-\bm{p}|}\;.

(187)

Since the only scale of the integral is
k_{1}=k_{2}
and the vertex functions scale as
\mathcal{I}\sim k_{1}^{-\frac{d}{2}}
, dimensional analysis implies that the integral (
187
) is scale-invariant. The amputated diagram is therefore independent of
k_{1}
. As we show in the following insert, the integral (
187
) can be evaluated thanks to the orthogonality relation among the Clebsch-Gordan coefficients of
\mathrm{SO}(1,d+1)
.

Loop Momentum Integral and tensor product of UIRs.

Despite being a
d
-dimensional integral over a product of Appell
F_{4}
functions, the loop-integral (
187
) can be fixed by symmetry. The argument is to see the vertex function
\mathcal{I}^{\mu\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}_{p\;k_{1}\;k_{2}}
with three frequencies within the principal series as related to the decomposition of the tensor product space
\mathcal{V}_{\mu_{1}}\otimes\mathcal{V}_{\mu_{2}}
along UIRs of
\mathrm{SO}(1,d+1)
using that
Dobrev:1975ru
;
Dobrev:1977qv
:

\mathcal{P}_{\mu_{1}}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{\mu_{2}}\sim\int_{\mu}\mathcal{P}_{\mu}\;.

(188)

For this, one defines the
3\mu
symbol as:

\left(\begin{array}[]{crc}\mu_{1}&\mu_{2}&\mu\\
\bm{k}_{1}&\bm{k}_{2}&\bm{k}\end{array}\right)\equiv\braket{\mu_{1},\bm{k}_{1};\mu_{2},\bm{k}_{2}|\mu,\bm{k}}\;.

(189)

By definition, the tensor product states are orthogonal to each other:

\braket{\mu_{1},\mu_{2},\bm{k}_{1},\bm{k}_{2}|\mu_{1},\mu_{2},\bm{k}_{1}^{\prime},\bm{k}_{2}^{\prime}}=\frac{\delta(0)^{2}}{\mathcal{N}_{\mu_{1}}\mathcal{N}_{\mu_{2}}}(2\pi)^{d}\delta^{(d)}(\bm{k}_{1}-\bm{k}_{1}^{\prime})(2\pi)^{d}\delta^{(d)}(\bm{k}_{2}-\bm{k}_{2}^{\prime})\;.

(190)

The completeness relation in the tensor product state is therefore given by:

\mathds{1}_{\mu_{1}\otimes\mu_{2}}\equiv\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{d}\bm{k}_{1}}{(2\pi)^{d}}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{d}\bm{k}_{2}}{(2\pi)^{d}}\frac{\mathcal{N}_{\mu_{1}}\mathcal{N}_{\mu_{2}}}{\delta(0)^{2}}\ket{\mu_{1},\bm{k}_{1};\mu_{2},\bm{k}_{2}}\bra{\mu_{1},\bm{k}_{1};\mu_{2},\bm{k}_{2}}\;.

(191)

Since the tensor product state is isomorphic to the full KLF space, one can sandwich this expression with two single particle states, yielding an orthogonality relation for the
3\mu
symbols:

\delta\left({}^{\mu\,\mu^{\prime}}_{\bm{k}\,\bm{k}^{\prime}}\right)=\frac{\mathcal{N}_{\mu_{1}}\mathcal{N}_{\mu_{2}}}{\left[\delta(0)\right]^{2}}\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{d}\bm{k}_{1}}{(2\pi)^{d}}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{d}\bm{k}_{2}}{(2\pi)^{d}}\left(\begin{array}[]{crc}\mu_{1}&\mu_{2}&\mu\\
\bm{k}_{1}&\bm{k}_{2}&\bm{k}\end{array}\right)^{*}\left(\begin{array}[]{crc}\mu_{1}&\mu_{2}&\mu^{\prime}\\
\bm{k}_{1}&\bm{k}_{2}&\bm{k}^{\prime}\end{array}\right)\;.

(192)

Let us use this to evaluate the momentum integral (
187
). To this aim, we will relate the
3\mu
symbol (
189
) to the cubic vertex function. The overlap between the state produced by a product of two free principal series fields
\chi_{1}
and
\chi_{2}
and the vacuum can be written:

\bra{\Omega}\chi_{1}(z,\bm{x})\chi_{2}(z,\bm{x})\ket{\mu_{1},\bm{k}_{1},\mu_{2},\bm{k}_{2}}=c_{\chi_{1}}(\mu_{1})c_{\chi_{2}}(\mu_{2})\Phi^{\mu_{1}}_{\bm{k}_{1}}(z,\bm{x})\Phi^{\mu_{2}}_{\bm{k}_{2}}(z,\bm{x})\;,

(193)

where the coefficients
c_{\chi_{j}}(\mu)
have been defined at the beginning of section
3.2
in terms of the spectral density
\rho_{\chi_{j}}(\mu)
. In the case of free fields, we have:

|c_{\chi}(\mu)|^{2}=\frac{\hat{\delta}_{\mu_{\chi}}(\mu)}{H^{2}\mathcal{N}_{\mu}}\;.

(194)

On the other hand, one can see the product of two fields as a composite operator
\chi_{1}\chi_{2}(z,\bm{x})
with wavefunction:

\bra{\Omega}\chi_{1}\chi_{2}(z,x)\ket{\mu,\bm{k}}=c_{\chi_{1}\chi_{2}}(\mu)\Phi^{\mu}_{\bm{k}}(z,\bm{x})\;.

(195)

The modulus square of the coefficient
c_{\chi_{1}\chi_{2}}(\mu)
is given by:

|c_{\chi_{1}\chi_{2}}(\mu)|^{2}=\frac{\rho_{\chi_{1}\chi_{2}}(\mu)}{H^{2}\mathcal{N}_{\mu}}\;,

(196)

where the special density of a product of two fields is such that:

W_{\mu_{\chi_{1}}}(\sigma^{E})W_{\mu_{\chi_{2}}}(\sigma^{E})=\int\displaylimits^{\infty}_{-\infty}\mathrm{d}\mu\rho_{\chi_{1}\chi_{2}}(\mu)W_{\mu}(\sigma^{E})\;,

(197)

and the function
W_{\mu}(\sigma^{E})
is defined in (
232
). Inverting equation (
197
) gives an explicit expression for the spectral density
Bros:2009bz
;
Loparco:2023rug
:

\rho_{\chi_{1}\chi_{2}}(\mu)=\frac{H^{d-1}\mathcal{N}_{\mu}}{32\pi^{\frac{d}{2}+2}\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}\pm i\mu\right)}\prod_{\pm,\pm,\pm}\Gamma\left(\frac{\frac{d}{2}\pm i\mu\pm i\mu_{\chi_{1}}\pm i\mu_{\chi_{2}}}{2}\right)\;.

(198)

Equivalent expressions can be found in terms of a
{}_{7}F_{6}
hypergeometric function, see e.g.
Marolf:2010zp
;
DiPietro:2021sjt
;
Xianyu:2022jwk
.
As explained in
Loparco:2023rug
, other UIRs in the density (
198
) arise if there exists some integer
n
such that
\frac{d}{2}+2n<i\mu_{\chi_{1}}+i\mu_{\chi_{2}}<d
, and should be taken into account exactly like in the CFT example in section
3.4
by taking the residues of (
198
). We ignore this as we assumed
\chi_{1}
and
\chi_{2}
to be in the principal series.

Inserting the resolution of identity (
58
) into (
193
) and using the orthogonality relation among the KLF harmonic functions yields:

c_{\chi_{1}\chi_{2}}(\mu)\,\left(\begin{array}[]{crc}\mu_{1}&\mu_{2}&\mu\\
\bm{k}_{1}&\bm{k}_{2}&\bm{k}\end{array}\right)^{*}=\frac{H^{\frac{d+1}{2}}}{\pi^{3/2}}c_{\chi_{1}}(\mu_{1})c_{\chi_{2}}(\mu_{2})(2\pi)^{d}\delta^{(d)}(\bm{k}_{1}+\bm{k}_{2}-\bm{k})\mathcal{I}^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\mu}_{k_{1}k_{2}\;k}\;.

(199)

Plugging this into the orthogonality relation (
192
) gives:

\frac{\rho_{\chi_{1}\chi_{2}}(\mu)}{\mathcal{N}_{\mu}^{2}}\hat{\delta}_{\mu^{\prime}}(\mu)=\frac{H^{d-1}}{\pi^{3}}\frac{\hat{\delta}_{\mu_{\chi_{1}}}(\mu_{1})\hat{\delta}_{\mu_{\chi_{2}}}(\mu_{2})}{\delta(0)^{2}}\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{d}\bm{p}}{(2\pi)^{d}}\mathcal{I}^{\mu\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}_{kp|\bm{k}-\bm{p}|}\mathcal{I}^{\mu^{\prime}\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}_{k\,p|\bm{k}-\bm{p}|}\;.

(200)

We get rid of the delta functions on the right-hand side by evaluating it at
\mu_{j}=\mu_{\chi_{j}}
. After relabelling, this gives the following result for the loop momentum-integral (
187
):

\mathcal{Q}_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}(\mu,\mu^{\prime})=\frac{\pi^{3}}{H^{d-1}}\frac{\rho_{\mu\mu^{\prime}}(\mu_{1})}{\mathcal{N}_{\mu_{1}}}\frac{\hat{\delta}_{\mu_{1}}(\mu_{2})}{\mathcal{N}_{\mu_{2}}}\;,

(201)

where
\rho_{\mu\mu^{\prime}}
denotes the spectral density of the product of two fields with frequencies
\mu
and
\mu^{\prime}
.

Using (
201
), the amputated diagram (
186
) can therefore be rewritten as a double layer spectral integral:

\mathcal{G}^{A}_{{\sf{a}}{\sf{b}}}\left({}^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}_{k_{1}k_{2}}\right)=-\frac{{\sf{a}}{\sf{b}}g^{2}e^{\frac{i\pi({\sf{a}}+{\sf{b}})d}{2}}}{H^{2}}\frac{\hat{\delta}_{\mu_{2}}(\mu_{1})}{\mathcal{N}_{\mu_{1}}^{2}}\int\displaylimits^{\infty}_{-\infty}\mathrm{d}\mu\,\mathcal{N}_{\mu}\,\mathrm{d}\mu^{\prime}\,\mathcal{N}_{\mu^{\prime}}\,\rho_{\mu\mu^{\prime}}(\mu_{1})\mathcal{G}_{{\sf{a}}{\sf{b}}}^{\chi_{1}}(\mu)\mathcal{G}_{{\sf{a}}{\sf{b}}}^{\chi_{2}}(\mu^{\prime})\;.

(202)

Instead of evaluating this integral by direct computation, as shown in appendix
F.2
, one can use the property (
197
) to write (
202
) as a single spectral integral:

\mathcal{G}_{{\sf{a}}{\sf{b}}}^{A}\left({}^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}_{k_{1}k_{2}}\right)\equiv-\frac{{\sf{a}}{\sf{b}}g^{2}e^{\frac{i\pi({\sf{a}}+{\sf{b}})d}{2}}}{H^{2}}\frac{\hat{\delta}_{\mu_{2}}(\mu_{1})}{\mathcal{N}_{\mu_{1}}}\int\displaylimits^{\infty}_{-\infty}\mathrm{d}\mu\rho_{\chi_{1}\chi_{2}}(\mu)\mathcal{G}_{{\sf{a}}{\sf{b}}}^{\mu}(\mu_{1})\;.

(203)

Then, the full KLF two-point function (
185
) can be written as:

\mathcal{G}^{\mu_{\varphi},\textrm{1 loop}}_{{\sf{a}}_{1}{\sf{a}}_{2}}\left({}^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}_{\bm{k}_{1}\bm{k}_{2}}\right)=\int\displaylimits^{\infty}_{-\infty}\mathrm{d}\mu\rho_{\chi_{1}\chi_{2}}(\mu)\mathcal{G}^{\mu,\textrm{mix}}_{{\sf{a}}_{1}{\sf{a}}_{2}}\left({}^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}_{\bm{k}_{1}\bm{k}_{2}}\right)\;,

(204)

where
\mathcal{G}^{\mu,\textrm{mix}}_{{\sf{a}}_{1}{\sf{a}}_{2}}\left({}^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}_{\bm{k}_{1}\bm{k}_{2}}\right)
is:

\mathcal{G}^{\mu,\textrm{mix}}_{{\sf{a}}_{1}{\sf{a}}_{2}}\left({}^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}_{\bm{k}_{1}\bm{k}_{2}}\right)=-g^{2}\delta\left({}^{\mu_{1}\;\mu_{2}}_{\bm{k}_{1}-\bm{k}_{2}}\right)\sum_{{\sf{b}}_{1},{\sf{b}}_{2}}{\sf{b}}_{1}{\sf{b}}_{2}e^{\frac{i\pi({\sf{b}}_{1}+{\sf{b}}_{2})d}{2}}\frac{\mathcal{G}^{\mu_{\varphi}}_{{\sf{a}}_{1}{\sf{b}}_{1}}(\mu_{1})}{H^{2}}\frac{\mathcal{G}^{\mu}_{{\sf{b}}_{1}{\sf{b}}_{2}}(\mu_{1})}{H^{2}}\frac{\mathcal{G}^{\mu_{\varphi}}_{{\sf{b}}_{2}{\sf{a}}_{2}}(\mu_{1})}{H^{2}}\;.

(205)

One can check that this corresponds to the
\mathcal{O}(g^{2})
contribution to the
\varphi
two-point function in an alternative theory featuring a quadratic mixing
\mathcal{L}_{\textrm{mix}}=-g\varphi\chi
where the mass of
\chi
is given by
m_{\chi}=HM_{\mu}
. Diagrammatically, for fixed values of
{\sf{b}}_{1}
and
{\sf{b}}_{2}
, equation (
204
) becomes:

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{40.87874pt}{-10.00812pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope {}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\right)\;,

(206)

This is consistent with the standard spectral representation techniques in the evaluation of loop diagrams in dS
Marolf:2010nz
;
Higuchi:2010xt
;
Xianyu:2022jwk
;
Zhang:2025nzd
.

As noted in
Higuchi:2010xt
, one can always get rid of such a quadratic mixing by diagonalising the mass matrix. As we show in appendix
F
, the final sum over the indices
{\sf{b}}_{1},{\sf{b}}_{2}
can thus be simplified (see Eq. (
317
)), and one finds that the one-loop reduced propagator takes the form:

\mathcal{G}^{\mu_{\varphi},1\;\textrm{loop}}_{{\sf{a}}{\sf{b}}}\left(\mu_{1}\right)=\bar{g}^{2}\int\displaylimits^{\infty}_{-\infty}\mathrm{d}\mu\frac{\rho_{\chi_{1}\chi_{2}}(\mu)}{H^{d-1}}\frac{\mathcal{G}_{{\sf{a}}{\sf{b}}}^{\mu}(\mu_{1})}{(\mu_{\varphi}^{2}-\mu^{2})^{2}}\;.

(207)

The UV divergences of this expression are contained in the Gamma factors in the spectral density
\rho_{\chi_{1}\chi_{2}}(\mu)

Higuchi:2010xt
, and they can be renormalised by adding the proper set of local counterterms.

6
Conclusions & Future Directions

In this paper, we constructed a new frequency-momentum space for dS correlators by simultaneously diagonalising the Casimir and the spatial translation operators. Being spanned by the states
\ket{\mu,\bm{k}}
, the resulting Kontorovich-Lebedev-Fourier (KLF) space is analogous to the familiar Hilbert space for Lorentz invariant QFT in flat spacetime. After showing that square-integrable functions decompose into the principal series UIRs of
\mathrm{SO}(1,d+1)
, we found how the corresponding KLF expansion can be extended to a wide class of functions by including non-principal contributions. In the example of a bulk CFT, we showed how the KLF decomposition of the Wightman two-point function recovers the Källén-Lehmann representation.

At the level of perturbative computations, we reformulated the double-branch path integral formulation directly in KLF space and derived the appropriate set of Feynman diagrammatic rules. The parallel between our construction and the energy-momentum space in Minkowski makes manifest the similarities with flat space in the structure of perturbative computations. In particular, the rational form taken by the (anti-)time ordered propagators relates the propagation of a virtual internal particle to the non-analyticities of the two-point function. However, it also unveils an intrinsic difference between the two spacetimes: the non-conservation of the dS frequency at vertices implies vertex contributions made of generalised hypergeometric functions, and forces one to keep a spectral integration per internal line.
Unlike real-time computations where nested integrals are required at each interaction vertex, spectral integrations can be performed simply by closing the contours and collecting the residues of their meromorphic integrands, as we showed in the example of the four-point exchange diagram. At the loop level, the group theoretical nature of our new language allowed us to recast the momentum integral in the self-energy correction to the scalar propagator as fundamental relations among the Clebsch-Gordan coefficients of
\mathrm{SO}(1,d+1)
.

As our work introduces a novel way to think about cosmological correlators, it naturally opens several avenues of research.

•

It would be interesting to apply the methods we developed in this paper to more complicated diagrams.

•

For simplicity, we restricted our construction to scalar fields. The formulation of realistic QFTs in dS would require an extension of the formalism to spinning cosmological correlators.

•

In flat spacetime, momentum-space methods provide the clearest path to understand low-energy effective field theories from a Wilsonian perspective. It would be interesting to seek for a notion of renormalisation group flow in KLF space.

•

Here, we made a maximal use of
\mathrm{SO}(1,d+1)
invariance. However, the space of consistent QFTs in dS is also strongly constrained by unitarity and causality. Characterising these constraints in the KLF formulation would be an interesting starting point for a non-perturbative bootstrap in dS.

•

Many phenomenologically relevant inflationary models involving dS-breaking interactions can be deduced from dS invariant correlators, to which our formalism applies. Besides, it would be interesting to study scenarios where the background itself deviates from pure dS.
