The research of DW is funded by the European Union (ERC,
, 101118787). Views and opinions expressed are however those of the author(s) only and do not necessarily reflect those of the European Union or the European Research Council Executive Agency. Neither the European Union nor the granting authority can be held responsible for them.

Appendix A
Kontorovich-Lebedev Transform

In this appendix, we show how the Kontorovich-Lebedev transform can be derived using spectral theory.

Let us first recall the simpler situation of Sturm-Liouville theory. The latter applies to bounded linear operators
\mathcal{O}
acting on
L^{2}([a,b])
.
18
18

18

An operator
O
is bounded if there exists a constant
K
such that
\lVert\mathcal{O}f\rVert\leq K\lVert f\rVert
for all
f
.
When such an operator is self-adjoint, the Sturm-Liouville theorem states that its eigenvectors constitute an orthogonal basis of
L^{2}([a,b])
. Here or below in the unbounded case,
\mathcal{O}
being self-adjoint implies that the eigenvalues are real and that the eigenfunctions are orthogonal to one another, the point here is to build a
basis
made of such orthogonal functions.

The situation where
\mathcal{O}
is unbounded is more subtle. The Hilbert space
L^{2}
is not the proper mathematical structure to formulate that problem, but one should instead work with the
rigged Hilbert space

Gelfand-Vilenkin
;
Maurin
, see for instance
delaMadrid:2005qdg
for a pedagogical discussion. The latter is constituted of a Gelfand triple forming the following structure:

\Phi\subset L^{2}\subset\Phi^{\times}\cong\Phi^{\prime}\;,

(208)

where
\Phi
is the maximal dense subset of
L^{2}
which is closed under the action on
\mathcal{O}
, and
\Phi^{\times}
and
\Phi^{\prime}
are, respectively, the set of (anti)linear functionals acting
on
\Phi
. Alternatively, one can think of these two sets as the distributions defined on the space of test functions
\Phi
. The elements of
\Phi^{\times}(\Phi^{\prime})
are identified with a ket
\ket{f}
(a bra
\bra{f}
) such that their actions on functions are:

\mathcal{F}_{f}[\varphi]:=\braket{f|\varphi}=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}z}{z}f^{*}(z)\varphi(z)\quad\text{and}\quad\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{f}[\varphi]:=\braket{\varphi|f}=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}z}{z}f(z)\varphi^{*}(z)\;.

(209)

Therefore, these spaces can be identified with the set of functions for which the integral (
209
) can be defined:

\Phi^{\times}\cong\left\{f(z)\left|\int^{\infty}_{0}\frac{\mathrm{d}z}{z}f^{*}(z)\varphi(z)<\infty\;,\quad\forall\;\varphi\in\Phi\right.\right\}\;.

(210)

When
\mathcal{O}
is unbounded, its eigenfunctions are not all in
L^{2}
, nor all in
\Phi^{\times}
. However, the generalised Sturm-Liouville theorem of interest to us states that the eigenfunctions that belong to
\Phi^{\times}
form an orthogonal basis of
\Phi^{\times}
.

The relevant operator
\mathcal{O}
for the Kontorovitch-Lebedev transform is the modified Bessel differential operator defined as:

\mathcal{O}f(z)\equiv z\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}z}\left(z\frac{\mathrm{d}f}{\mathrm{d}z}\right)-z^{2}f(z)\;.

(211)

This operator is self-adjoint in the Hilbert space
L^{2}\left(\mathbb{R}_{+},\frac{\mathrm{d}z}{z}\right)
with the inner product

\braket{f|g}=\int\displaylimits^{\infty}_{0}\frac{\mathrm{d}z}{z}f^{*}(z)g(z)\;,

(212)

for functions that satisfy the appropriate boundary conditions
\left[z(g^{\prime}f^{*}-gf^{\prime*})\right]^{\infty}_{0}
. The test functions in that case can be thought of as the functions that decay faster than any polynomial at infinity, and behave around
0
like
z^{\alpha}
with
\mathrm{Re}(\alpha)>0
around zero. As for the eigenfunctions of
\mathcal{O}
with eigenvalue
\lambda
, they are given by the modified Bessel functions
K_{\sqrt{\lambda}}(z)
and
I_{\pm\sqrt{\lambda}}(z)
. However, the
I_{\pm\sqrt{\lambda}}
do not belong to
\Phi^{\times}
. For the
K
’s, given the behaviour
K_{\mu}(z)\underset{0}{\sim}2^{-\mu-1}\Gamma(-\mu)x^{\mu}+2^{\mu-1}\Gamma(\mu)x^{-\mu}
, only the
K_{i\mu}
with real
\mu
belong to
\Phi^{\times}
. As a result, the generalised Sturm-Liouville theorem thus tells us that the
K_{i\mu},\mu\in\mathbb{R}
, constitute the orthogonal basis of
\Phi^{\times}
we were after.

In the following, we go one step further and derive the completeness relation among these basis functions, and hence the associated spectral measure. For this, we define, for
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
, the Green function
G^{(\lambda)}(z,z^{\prime})

such that

\left(\mathcal{O}-\lambda\right)_{z}G^{(\lambda)}(z,z^{\prime})=z^{\prime}\;\delta(z-z^{\prime})\;

(213)

and with vanishing boundary conditions.
Following the standard method, one can find the explicit expression:

G^{(\lambda)}(z,z^{\prime})=-K_{\sqrt{\lambda}}(z)I_{\sqrt{\lambda}}(z^{\prime})\Theta(z-z^{\prime})-I_{\sqrt{\lambda}}(z)K_{\sqrt{\lambda}}(z^{\prime})\Theta(z^{\prime}-z)\;,

(214)

where we picked the positive square root for
I
in order to satisfy the boundary conditions at
z\to 0
. One can see that the location where the Green function fails to be analytic, along the branch cut on the negative real axis, coincides with the values we identified above according to the generalised Sturm-Liouville theorem. Now, using Cauchy theorem, one can write, for
\lambda_{0}\notin\mathbb{R}_{-}
:

G^{(\lambda_{0})}(z,z^{\prime})=\oint_{\mathcal{C}_{0}}\frac{\mathrm{d}\lambda}{2\pi i}\frac{G^{(\lambda)}(z,z^{\prime})}{\lambda-\lambda_{0}}\;,

(215)

where
\mathcal{C}_{0}
is a contour that does not cross
\mathbb{R}_{-}
, as shown in figure
6
.

\text{Re}(\lambda)
\text{Im}(\lambda)
\mathcal{C}
\lambda_{0}
\mathcal{C}_{0}

Figure 6
:
Integration contour for the dispersive integral (
215
).

Then, by blowing up the contour to
\mathcal{C}
like in the figure, and as the contribution from the arc at infinity vanishes, we are left with the following dispersive integral:

G^{(\lambda_{0})}(z,z^{\prime})=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{-}}\frac{\mathrm{d}\lambda}{2\pi i}\frac{\text{Disc}_{\lambda^{\prime}}(G^{(\lambda)}(z,z^{\prime}))}{\lambda-\lambda_{0}}\;.

(216)

Writing
\lambda=-\mu^{2}
and for
z>z^{\prime}
(
z<z^{\prime}
is similar), this discontinuity is given by:

\text{Disc}_{\lambda}(G^{(\lambda^{\prime})}(z,z^{\prime}))=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\left(G^{(-\mu^{2}+i\epsilon)}(z,z^{\prime})-G^{(-\mu^{2}-i\epsilon)}(z,z^{\prime})\right)

(217)

=K_{-i\mu}(z)I_{-i\mu}(z^{\prime})-K_{i\mu}(z)I_{i\mu}(z^{\prime})=\frac{2i}{\pi}\sinh(\pi\mu)K_{i\mu}(z)K_{i\mu}(z^{\prime})\;.

(218)

After the change of variable
\lambda=-\mu^{2}
, one finds that the Green function can be written as the spectral integral:

G^{(\lambda_{0})}(z,z^{\prime})=\frac{2}{\pi}\int^{\infty}_{0}\mathrm{d}\mu\frac{\mu\sinh(\pi\mu)}{\pi}\frac{K_{i\mu}(z)K_{i\mu}(z^{\prime})}{-\mu^{2}+\mu^{2}_{0}}\;.

(219)

Now, one can apply the operator
\mathcal{O}-\lambda_{0}
to the left in order to get rid of the denominator:

\left(\mathcal{O}_{z}-\lambda_{0}\right)G^{(\lambda_{0})}(z,z^{\prime})=\frac{2}{\pi}\int^{\infty}_{0}\mathrm{d}\mu\frac{\mu\sinh(\pi\mu)}{\pi}K_{i\mu}(z)K_{i\mu}(z^{\prime})=z^{\prime}\delta(z-z^{\prime})\;.

(220)

Because of the shadow symmetry of the integral, one can rewrite it as:

\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\mathrm{d}\mu\,\mathcal{N}_{\mu}K_{i\mu}(z)K_{i\mu}(z^{\prime})=z^{\prime}\delta(z-z^{\prime})\,\quad\textrm{with}\quad\mathcal{N}_{\mu}=\frac{\mu}{\pi}\sinh(\pi\mu)\,.

(221)

This is the completeness relation we were after. From it, one also deduces the coefficient entering into the orthogonality relation among these eigenfunctions:

\int^{\infty}_{0}\frac{\mathrm{d}z}{z}K_{i\mu}(z)K_{i\mu^{\prime}}(z)=\frac{\pi|\mu|}{\mathcal{N}_{\mu}}\delta(\mu^{2}-\mu^{\prime 2})\;,\quad\mu\in\mathbb{R}\,.

(222)

The two relations (
221
) and (
222
) thus enable us derive the Kontorovitch-Lebedev transform and its inverse:

f(z)=\int_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\mathrm{d}\mu\;\mathcal{N}_{\mu}\Phi^{\mu}(z)f^{\mu}\;,\quad f^{\mu}=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}z}{z}\Phi^{\mu}(z)f(z)

(223)

where the harmonic function is given by:

\Phi^{\mu}(z)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\pi}}K_{i\mu}(z)\;.

(224)

This transformation was first introduced in
Kontorovich1938
and later studied in
Lebedev1946
. The Parseval identity was proved in
Lebedev1949
, the KL transform for distributions was considered in
Yakubovich1994
;
Zemanian_1975
, and a table for KL transform can be found in
Erdelyi1954
.

Together with the Fourier transform, the relations (
221
) and (
222
) imply the following completeness and orthogonality relations among the harmonic functions (
42
) in the principal series:

\displaystyle\int_{\mathrm{KLF}}\left[\Phi^{\mu}_{\bm{k}}(z^{\prime},\bm{x}^{\prime})\right]^{*}\Phi^{\mu}_{\bm{k}}(z,\bm{x})

\displaystyle=(Hz)^{d+1}\delta(z-z^{\prime})\delta^{(d)}(\bm{x}-\bm{x}^{\prime})\,,

(225)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathrm{EAdS}}\left[\Phi^{\mu}_{\bm{k}}(z,\bm{x})\right]^{*}\Phi^{\mu^{\prime}}_{\bm{k}^{\prime}}(z,\bm{x})

\displaystyle=\frac{(2\pi)^{d}}{\mathcal{N}_{\mu}}\delta^{(d)}(\bm{k}-\bm{k}^{\prime})\frac{1}{2}(\delta_{\mu^{\prime}}(\mu)+\delta_{\mu^{\prime}}(-\mu))\;,

with the integration measures

\displaystyle\int_{\mathrm{KLF}}

\displaystyle\equiv\int\displaylimits_{-\infty}^{+\infty}\mathrm{d}\mu\,\mathcal{N}_{\mu}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{d}\bm{k}}{(2\pi)^{d}}\,,

(226)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathrm{EAdS}}

\displaystyle\equiv\int\displaylimits_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}z}{(Hz)^{d+1}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathrm{d}^{d}\bm{x}\,.

The KLF transform and its inverse thus read:

\displaystyle f(z,\bm{x})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathrm{KLF}}\Phi^{\mu}_{\bm{k}}(z,\bm{x})f^{\mu}_{\bm{k}}\,,

(227)

\displaystyle f^{\mu}_{\bm{k}}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathrm{EAdS}}\left[\Phi^{\mu}_{\bm{k}}(z,\bm{x})\right]^{*}f(z,\bm{x})\,.

Appendix B
From KLF to Position-Space Propagators

In section
4.2
, we showed that the four possible KLF propagators are given by Eqs. (
121
) and (
127
). Here, in order to make contact with results in the literature, we explicitly perform the KLF inverse transform and the Wick rotation in order to go back to configuration Lorentzian space.

For this, let us recall that KLF space is defined in Euclidean Anti de-Sitter space (EAdS). The latter is defined in embedding space as the set of points at fixed negative Lorentzian distance from the origin:

\eta_{AB}X^{E,A}X^{E,B}=-R_{\mathrm{EAdS}}^{2}\;,

(228)

where
R_{\mathrm{EAdS}}
is called the EAdS radius. Like dS, this can described in Poincaré coordinates, such that

X^{E,0}=R_{\mathrm{EAdS}}\,\frac{z^{2}+\bm{x}^{2}+1}{2z}\,,\quad X^{E,i}=-R_{\mathrm{EAdS}}\,\frac{x^{i}}{z}\,,\quad X^{E,d+1}=R_{\mathrm{EAdS}}\,\frac{z^{2}+\bm{x}^{2}-1}{2z}\,,

(229)

where
i=1,\ldots,d
and
z>0
, in which the EAdS metric reads

\mathrm{d}s^{2}=R_{\mathrm{EAdS}}^{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}z^{2}+\mathrm{d}\bm{x}^{2}}{z^{2}}\,.

(230)

When dealing with two-point correlation functions, it is natural to introduce the EAdS two-point invariant such that:

\sigma^{E}\equiv\frac{X_{1}^{E,A}X_{2}^{E,B}\eta_{AB}}{R_{\mathrm{EAdS}}^{2}}=-\frac{z_{1}^{2}+z_{2}^{2}+|\bm{x}_{12}|^{2}}{2z_{1}z_{2}}\in(-\infty,-1)\;.

(231)

In what follows, the main building block will be the function defined by the momentum integral of two harmonic functions (
42
):

\displaystyle W_{\mu}(\sigma^{E})

\displaystyle=H^{-2}\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{d}\bm{k}}{(2\pi)^{d}}\Phi^{\mu}_{\bm{k}}(X^{E}_{1})\Phi^{\mu}_{-\bm{k}}(X^{E}_{2})

(232)

\displaystyle=\frac{2H^{d-1}\pi^{\frac{d-3}{2}}(z_{1}z_{2})^{\frac{d}{2}}}{(2\pi)^{d}\Gamma\left(\frac{d-1}{2}\right)}\int\displaylimits_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}k\;k^{d-1}K_{i\mu}(kz_{1})K_{i\mu}(kz_{2})

\displaystyle\times\int\displaylimits^{1}_{-1}\mathrm{d}\chi(1-\chi^{2})^{\frac{d-3}{2}}e^{-ik|\bm{x}_{12}|\chi}

\displaystyle=\frac{2^{\frac{d}{2}}H^{d-1}\pi^{\frac{d}{2}-1}(z_{1}z_{2})^{\frac{d}{2}}}{(2\pi)^{d}|\bm{x}_{12}|^{\frac{d}{2}-1}}\int\displaylimits_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}k\;k^{\frac{d}{2}}K_{i\mu}(kz_{1})K_{i\mu}(kz_{2})J_{\frac{d}{2}-1}(k|\bm{x}_{12}|)

\displaystyle=\frac{H^{d-1}}{(4\pi)^{\frac{d+1}{2}}}\Gamma\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\frac{d}{2}\pm i\mu\\
\frac{d+1}{2}\end{array}\right)\;_{2}F_{1}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\frac{d}{2}+i\mu&\frac{d}{2}-i\mu\\
\frac{d+1}{2}&\end{array};\frac{1+\sigma^{E}}{2}\right)\;.

The integral over the angles and the norm are respectively given by Eq.
(3.387,2)
and Eq.
(6.578
,
10
) of
gradshteyn2007
. We also used Eq.
(14.3.15)
of
NIST:DLMF
to connect the associated Legendre to hypergeometric functions. This is closely related to what are called the harmonic function of EAdS in

Costa:2011dw
as:

\Omega_{\mu}(\sigma^{E})=H^{2}\mathcal{N}_{\mu}W_{\mu}(\sigma^{E})\;,

(233)

which are known to form a complete basis of square integrable bi-local functions in EAdS. This fact easily derives from the orthogonality and completeness of KLF modes, from which one indeed finds:

H^{2}\int\displaylimits^{\infty}_{-\infty}\mathrm{d}\mu\mathcal{N}_{\mu}W^{\mu}(\sigma^{E}_{12})=(Hz_{1})^{d+1}\delta(z_{1}-z_{2})\delta^{(d)}(\bm{x}_{1}-\bm{x}_{2})\;,

(234)

and

H^{2}\int_{\mathrm{EAdS}}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{d}\bm{x}\mathrm{d}z}{(Hz)^{d+1}}W_{\mu}(\sigma^{E}_{1x})W_{\mu^{\prime}}(\sigma^{E}_{x2})=\frac{\hat{\delta}_{\mu^{\prime}}(\mu)}{\mathcal{N}_{\mu}}W_{\mu}(\sigma^{E}_{12})\;.

(235)

The integral (
232
) is the EAdS counterpart of the addition theorem for spherical harmonics on the Euclidean sphere:

\sum_{\bm{m}}Y^{L}_{\bm{m}}(X_{1}^{S})\left[Y^{L}_{\bm{m}}(X_{2}^{S})\right]^{*}=\frac{-2L-d}{(4\pi)^{\frac{d-1}{2}}}\frac{\Gamma\left(-L,L+d\right)}{\Gamma\left(\frac{d+1}{2}\right)}\;_{2}F_{1}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}-L&L+d\\
\frac{d+1}{2}&\end{array};\frac{1-\sigma^{S}}{2}\right)\;,

(236)

where
Y^{L}_{\bm{m}}(X^{S})
are the
(d+1)
-dimensional spherical harmonics and
\sigma^{S}
is the two-point invariant on the sphere between the two points
X_{1}^{S}
and
X_{2}^{S}
. See e.g.
Marolf:2010zp
for more details about obtaining dS propagators from (
236
).

Let us now turn to the Euclidean position-space version of the four propagators. Using the general KLF decomposition of correlators (
95
), the links (
120
), (
126
) between general and reduced propagators, and the definition (
232
) of
W_{\mu}
, one has

\mathcal{C}_{{\sf{a}}{\sf{b}}}^{E,\varphi}(\sigma^{E})=\int\displaylimits^{\infty}_{-\infty}\mathrm{d}\mu\,\mathcal{N}_{\mu}\,W_{\mu}(\sigma^{E})\,\mathcal{G}^{\varphi}_{{\sf{a}}{\sf{b}}}(\mu)\,.

(237)

We begin with the simplest case of the
\pm\mp
two-point function. Using the simple form (
127
) of the KLF Wightman function straightforwardly gives

\mathcal{C}^{E}_{\pm\mp}(X_{1}^{E},X_{2}^{E})=W_{\mu_{\varphi}}(\sigma^{E})\;.

(238)

We now move on with the more subtle case of
\pm\pm
propagators. In section (
4.2
), we showed that these diagonal propagators in KLF are given by Eq. (
114
) in terms of the arbitrary functions
f_{\sf{a}}(\mu)
. In the following, we show that there are physical choices for these functions corresponding to the (anti-)time ordered boundary conditions. In Lorentzian time, the Fourier space time-ordered two-point function is:

\mathcal{C}_{{\sf{a}}{\sf{a}}}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2};k)=H^{d-1}u^{{\sf{a}}}_{k}(\tau_{1},\mu)u^{-{\sf{a}}}_{k}(\tau_{2},\mu)\Theta(\tau_{1}-\tau_{2})+(\tau_{1}\leftrightarrow\tau_{2})\;,

(239)

where the mode functions
u^{{\sf{a}}}_{k}(\tau,\mu)
are those defined in (
46
). After the Wick rotation, one finds:

\mathcal{C}_{{\sf{a}}{\sf{a}}}^{E}(z_{1},z_{2};k)=\frac{1}{H^{2}}\Phi^{\mu}_{\bm{k}}(z_{1},\bm{0})\Phi^{\mu}_{\bm{k}}(e^{-i{\sf{a}}\pi}z_{2},\bm{0})\Theta(z_{1}-z_{2})+(z_{1}\leftrightarrow z_{2})\;.

(240)

Principal series.

When the propagating field is in the principal series, one needs an
i\epsilon
prescription (
116
) that also introduces an arbitrary function
g(\mu)
. In order to see why, let us notice that the usual Feynman prescription breaks the shadow symmetry:

\displaystyle\int\displaylimits^{\infty}_{-\infty}\mathrm{d}\mu\mathcal{N}_{\mu}\frac{K_{i\mu}(z_{1})K_{i\mu}(z_{2})}{\mu^{2}-\mu_{\varphi}^{2}+ia\epsilon}

\displaystyle=\pi\Theta(z_{1}-z_{2})K_{i\mu_{\varphi}}(z_{1})I_{ia\mu_{\varphi}}(z_{2})

(241)

\displaystyle+\pi\Theta(z_{2}-z_{1})K_{i\mu_{\varphi}}(z_{2})I_{ia\mu_{\varphi}}(z_{1})\;.

In order to perform this integral, we split the function
K_{i\mu}(z)
into a sum of the function
I_{i\mu}(z)
and its shadow using

{\cal N}_{\mu}K_{i\mu}(z)=\frac{i\mu}{2}(I_{i\mu}(z)-I_{-i\mu}(z))\;.

(242)

When
z_{1}>z_{2}
, we have to split the function in
z_{2}
so that
K_{i\mu}(kz_{1})I_{\pm i\mu}(kz_{2})
decays in the lower (upper) half-plane. The case where
z_{1}<z_{2}
can be obtained by permuting the
z
variables. The shadow symmetry breaking comes from the function
I_{i\mu_{\varphi}}
, which is not symmetric under reflection of the frequency
\mu_{\varphi}
. Therefore, one needs to sum two opposite Feynman pole prescriptions in order to restore it. To this aim, we introduce:

\frac{1}{(\mu^{2}-\mu_{\varphi}^{2})^{g}_{i\epsilon}}=\frac{g(\mu_{\varphi})}{\mu^{2}-\mu_{\varphi}^{2}+i\epsilon}+\frac{g(-\mu_{\varphi})}{\mu^{2}-\mu_{\varphi}^{2}-i\epsilon}\;,

(243)

where
g(\mu)
is an arbitrary function such that:

g(\mu)+g(-\mu)=1\;.

(244)

We impose the last equality so that the small
\epsilon
limit gives back the propagator:

\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{(\mu^{2}-\mu_{\varphi}^{2})^{g}_{i{\sf{a}}\epsilon}}=\frac{1}{\mu^{2}-\mu_{\varphi}^{2}}\;.

(245)

Let us see how to determine the functions
f
and
g
with physical boundary conditions. For concreteness, we look at the
++
propagator, as the one
--
can be simply obtained by complex conjugation. Let us first perform the spectral integration, i.e.
\mathcal{C}^{E}_{++}(X^{E}_{1},X^{E}_{2})=\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{d}\bm{k}}{(2\pi)^{d}}e^{-i\bm{k}\cdot(\bm{x}_{1}-\bm{x}_{2})}\mathcal{C}_{++}^{E}(z_{1},z_{2};k)
, where

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{++}^{E}(z_{1},z_{2};k)=\frac{H^{d-1}}{\pi}e^{-\frac{i\pi(d-1)}{2}}(z_{1}z_{2})^{\frac{d}{2}}\int\displaylimits^{\infty}_{-\infty}\mathrm{d}\mu\mathcal{N}_{\mu}K_{i\mu}(kz_{1})K_{i\mu}(kz_{2})

(246)

\displaystyle\times\Bigg(\frac{1}{(\mu^{2}-\mu_{\varphi}^{2})^{g}_{i\epsilon}}+\hat{\delta}_{\mu_{\varphi}}(\mu)f_{+}(\mu)\Bigg)\;.

To evaluate this, we use (
241
) and find, for
z_{1}>z_{2}
(the other case is similar):

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{++}^{E}(z_{1},z_{2};k)=H^{d-1}e^{-i\frac{\pi(d-1)}{2}}(z_{1}z_{2})^{\frac{d}{2}}K_{i\mu_{\varphi}}(kz_{1})\Bigg(g(\mu_{\varphi})I_{i\mu_{\varphi}}(kz_{2})

(247)

\displaystyle+g(-\mu_{\varphi})I_{-i\mu_{\varphi}}(kz_{2})+\frac{1}{\pi}\mathcal{N}_{\mu_{\varphi}}f_{+}(\mu_{\varphi})K_{i\mu_{\varphi}}(kz_{2})\Bigg)\;.

Now, we have to find all the possible functions
f_{\sf{a}}
and
g
compatible with (
240
). To this aim, we use Eq. (
242
) and the analytic continuation formula for the Bessel functions:

K_{i\mu}\left(e^{-i\pi}z\right)=\frac{i\pi}{2\sinh(\pi\mu)}\left(e^{\pi\mu}I_{i\mu}(z)-e^{-\pi\mu}I_{-i\mu}(z)\right)\;.

(248)

This fixes one of the two functions in terms of the other via the following condition:

\displaystyle 2g(\mu)+\frac{i}{\pi}\mu f_{+}(\mu)

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{\pi\mu}}{\sinh(\pi\mu)}\,.

(249)

There is still a large freedom in the choice of the function
g
, and this can be used to set the functions
f_{\sf{a}}
to zero:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{crc}f_{\sf{a}}(\mu)&=&0\\
g(\mu)&=&\frac{e^{\pi\mu}}{2\sinh(\pi\mu)}\end{array}\right.\;.

(250)

This is equivalent to the prescription used in
Melville:2024ove
;
Werth:2024mjg
and yields the result (
118
) quoted in the main text. Since the contribution from the functions
f_{\sf{a}}(\mu)
are within the principal series, (
250
) amounts to absorbing it in the integral over the principal series, which is the simplest choice.

Other UIRs.

Let us now consider a propagating field that is not in the principal series, i.e. its frequency is purely imaginary and we write
\mu_{\varphi}=i\nu_{\varphi}
with real
\nu_{\varphi}
. In that case, the poles of the reduced two-point function (
114
) are on the imaginary axis. Hence, there is no need for an
i\epsilon
prescription, i.e. we can set
g=0
, and the boundary conditions are fully encoded in the functions
f_{a}(\mu)
. We look at the
++
propagator, the other case being similar. To evaluate (
246
), like above, we use the formula (
242
) and close the contour in the lower half-plane for
z_{1}>z_{2}
. As there is no
i\epsilon
prescription here, the contour integral picks a single pole here, yielding:

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{E}_{++}(z_{1},z_{2};k)=H^{d-1}e^{-\frac{i\pi(d-1)}{2}}(z_{1}z_{2})^{\frac{d}{2}}K_{\nu_{\varphi}}(kz_{1})\Bigg(I_{|\nu_{\varphi}|}(kz_{2})+\frac{\mathcal{N}_{i\nu_{\varphi}}f_{+}(i\nu_{\varphi})}{\pi}K_{\nu_{\varphi}}(kz_{2})\Bigg)\;.

(251)

Again using Eq. (
242
), the matching with the behaviour (
240
) now gives a unique solution for
f_{+}
, and similarly for
f_{-}
:

f_{\sf{a}}(i\nu_{\varphi})=-i{\sf{a}}\frac{e^{i{\sf{a}}\pi|\nu_{\varphi}|}}{\mathcal{N}_{i\nu_{\varphi}}}\,.

(252)

Physically, this contribution corresponds to the component of the propagator that cannot be absorbed in the principal series integral.

Momentum integration.

Then, one has to carry out the momentum integral in order to obtain the Euclidean position-space propagator:

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{E}_{{\sf{a}}{\sf{a}}}(X_{1}^{E},X_{2}^{E})=\frac{1}{H^{2}}\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{d}\bm{k}}{(2\pi)^{d}}\Bigg(\Phi^{\mu_{\varphi}}_{\bm{k}}(z_{1},\bm{x}_{1})\Phi^{\mu_{\varphi}}_{-\bm{k}}(e^{-i{\sf{a}}\pi}z_{2},\bm{x}_{2})\Theta(z_{1}-z_{2})

(253)

\displaystyle+\Phi^{\mu_{\varphi}}_{\bm{k}}(e^{-i{\sf{a}}\pi}z_{1},\bm{x}_{1})\Phi^{\mu_{\varphi}}_{-\bm{k}}(z_{2},\bm{x}_{2})\Theta(z_{2}-z_{1})\Bigg)\;.

Each integration can be carried out independently and are simply obtained by analytic continuation of the integral (
232
), yielding:

\mathcal{C}^{E}_{++}(X_{1}^{E},X_{2}^{E})=W_{\mu_{\varphi}}(-\sigma^{E}-i{\sf{a}}\epsilon)\;.

(254)

The ensemble of Euclidean two-point functions can thus be summarised as:

\bm{\mathcal{C}}^{E}(X_{1}^{E},X_{2}^{E})=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}W_{\mu_{\varphi}}(-\sigma^{E}-i\epsilon)&W_{\mu_{\varphi}}(\sigma^{E})\\
W_{\mu_{\varphi}}(\sigma^{E})&W_{\mu_{\varphi}}(-\sigma^{E}+i\epsilon)\end{array}\right)\;.

(255)

Back to Lorentzian time.

These propagators only make sense as Wick rotated versions of their Lorentzian dS counterparts. Let us explain how to carry the analytical continuation of the Euclidean two-point invariant (
231
) to Lorentzian signature. Since the building block
W_{\mu}(\sigma)
has a branch cut for
\sigma\in(1,\infty)
, we must keep track of the
i\epsilon
prescription and rotate to the tilted axis
\tau_{c}=\tau e^{i{\sf{a}}\epsilon}
by applying (
34
) to the two Euclidean times
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
. The four possible analytic continuations are given by:

\displaystyle\sigma_{{\sf{a}}{\sf{b}}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\left(-\tau_{1}e^{i{\sf{a}}\left(\frac{\pi}{2}-\epsilon\right)}\right)^{2}+\left(-\tau_{2}e^{i{\sf{b}}\left(\frac{\pi}{2}-\epsilon\right)}\right)^{2}+|\bm{x}_{12}|^{2}}{2\tau_{1}\tau_{2}e^{i({\sf{a}}+{\sf{b}})\left(\frac{\pi}{2}-\epsilon\right)}}

(256)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{{\sf{a}}+{\sf{b}}}\left(\sigma^{\mathrm{dS}}-i{\sf{a}}\epsilon\tau_{1}^{2}-i{\sf{b}}\epsilon\tau_{2}^{2}\right)+i({\sf{a}}+{\sf{b}})\sigma^{\mathrm{dS}}\epsilon\;.

On the one hand, the off-diagonal components give:

\sigma_{-{\sf{a}}{\sf{a}}}=\sigma^{\mathrm{dS}}-i{\sf{a}}\epsilon\;\textrm{sgn}(\tau_{1}-\tau_{2})\;.

(257)

On the other hand, the diagonal components are:

\sigma_{{\sf{a}}{\sf{a}}}=-\sigma^{\mathrm{dS}}+i{\sf{a}}(1+\sigma^{\mathrm{dS}})\epsilon\;.

(258)

From equation (
255
), it is clear that the regularisation will only be needed at time-like separations for
\sigma^{\mathrm{dS}}>1
. The final prescription is therefore:

\sigma_{{\sf{a}}{\sf{a}}}=-\sigma^{\mathrm{dS}}+i{\sf{a}}\epsilon\;.

(259)

Inserting this in the Euclidean propagators yields the following result for the Lorentzian free two-point functions in dS:

\bm{\mathcal{C}}(X_{1},X_{2})=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}W_{\mu_{\varphi}}(\sigma^{\textrm{dS}}-i\epsilon)&W_{\mu_{\varphi}}(\sigma^{\textrm{dS}}-i\epsilon\;\textrm{sgn}(\tau_{1}-\tau_{2}))\\
W_{\mu_{\varphi}}(\sigma^{\textrm{dS}}+i\epsilon\;\textrm{sgn}(\tau_{1}-\tau_{2}))&W_{\mu_{\varphi}}(\sigma^{\textrm{dS}}+i\epsilon)\end{array}\right)\;.

(260)

This matches the usual results (see e.g.
3.3
of
DiPietro:2021sjt
). In particular, all these propagators agree at space-like separations as required by micro-causality. The different time orderings come from the regularisation prescription of the branch cut at time-like separations.

Appendix C
Details of the KLF Decomposition of the CFT Two-Point Function

In section
3.4
, we took the example of the CFT two-point function in order to illustrate the role of the other UIRs in the KLF decomposition of non square-integrable functions. Here is a detailed version of the computations.

KLF transform.

Let us first perform the KLF transform (
76
) explicitly, where we recall the expression (
75
) of the Euclidean Wightman two-point function, and we write
X_{2}^{E}=(z,\bm{x})
:

\displaystyle\int_{\mathrm{EAdS}}\frac{\mathrm{d}z_{1}\mathrm{d}^{d}\bm{x}_{1}}{(Hz)^{d+1}}\left[\Phi^{\mu}_{\bm{k}}(z_{1},\bm{x}_{1})\right]^{*}\braket{\mathcal{O}(X_{1}^{E})\mathcal{O}(X_{2}^{E})}

(261)

\displaystyle=\int\frac{\mathrm{d}z^{\prime}\mathrm{d}^{d}\bm{x}^{\prime}}{(Hz^{\prime})^{d+1}}\frac{(zz^{\prime})^{\Delta}}{((z+z^{\prime})^{2}+|\bm{x}_{12}|^{2})^{\Delta}}\left[\Phi^{\mu}_{\bm{k}}(z^{\prime},\bm{x}^{\prime})\right]^{*}

\displaystyle=\frac{H^{-\frac{d+1}{2}}z^{\Delta}e^{i\bm{k}\cdot\bm{x}}}{\pi^{\frac{1}{2}}}\int\mathrm{d}z^{\prime}(z^{\prime})^{\Delta-\frac{d}{2}-1}K_{i\mu}(pz^{\prime})\int\mathrm{d}^{d}\bm{x}^{\prime}\frac{e^{i\bm{k}\cdot\bm{x}^{\prime}}}{{((z+z^{\prime})^{2}+|\bm{x}^{\prime}|^{2})^{\Delta}}}\;.

The spatial integral is:

\displaystyle\int\mathrm{d}^{d}\bm{x}^{\prime}\frac{e^{i\bm{k}\cdot\bm{x}^{\prime}}}{{((z+z^{\prime})^{2}+|\bm{x}^{\prime}|^{2})^{\Delta}}}

(262)

\displaystyle=\frac{2\pi^{\frac{d-1}{2}}}{\Gamma\left(\frac{d-1}{2}\right)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}rr^{d-1}}{((z+z^{\prime})^{2}+r^{2})^{\Delta}}\int^{1}_{-1}\mathrm{d}\chi(1-\chi^{2})^{\frac{d-3}{2}}e^{ikr\chi}

\displaystyle=\frac{(2\pi)^{\frac{d}{2}}}{p^{\frac{d}{2}-1}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}r\;r^{\frac{d}{2}}J_{\frac{d}{2}-1}(kr)}{((z+z^{\prime})^{2}+r^{2})^{\Delta}}

\displaystyle=\frac{(2\pi)^{\frac{d}{2}}}{2^{\Delta-1}p^{\frac{d}{2}-\Delta}\Gamma(\Delta)}(z+z^{\prime})^{\frac{d}{2}-\Delta}K_{\Delta-\frac{d}{2}}(k(z+z^{\prime}))\;.

For the last step, we used Eq.
(6.565
,
4
) of
gradshteyn2007
. This integral converges if
d>0
,
\Delta>\frac{d-1}{4}
and
z+z^{\prime}>0
. The second condition gives a candidate bound for the square-integrability of the two-point function and the last one enforces the need to define this transformation in the Euclidean manifold.

Now, let us perform the remaining time integral:

\displaystyle\frac{2^{\frac{d}{2}-\Delta+1}\pi^{\frac{d-1}{2}}H^{-\frac{d+1}{2}}z^{\Delta}e^{i\bm{k}\cdot\bm{x}}}{k^{\frac{d}{2}-\Delta}\Gamma(\Delta)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}z^{\prime}}{z^{\prime}}\left(\frac{z^{\prime}}{z+z^{\prime}}\right)^{\Delta-\frac{d}{2}}K_{\Delta-\frac{d}{2}}(k(z+z^{\prime}))K_{i\mu}(kz^{\prime})

(263)

\displaystyle=H^{-(d+1)}\frac{2^{-2\Delta+d+1}\pi^{\frac{d-1}{2}}\Gamma\left(\Delta-\frac{d}{2}\pm i\mu\right)}{\Gamma(\Delta)\Gamma\left(\Delta-\frac{d}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\right)}\mu\sinh(\pi\mu)\frac{\left[\Phi_{\bm{k}}^{\mu}(z,\bm{x})\right]^{*}}{\mathcal{N}_{\mu}}\;.

We made use of
(6.583)
from
gradshteyn2007
. This converges when
\Delta-\frac{d}{2}>\text{Re}(i\mu)=0
and
\text{arg}(z)\in]-\pi,\pi[
. The first condition gives a more stringent bound on the scaling dimension that the one we obtained from the spatial integral, and the second condition enforces the need of an
i\epsilon
prescription when going back to dS. As a result, the inverse formula is defined for the following regime:

\Delta>\frac{d}{2}\;,\quad\text{square integrable function}\;,

(264)

and the spectral density along the principal series is given by:

\rho^{\mathcal{P}}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mu)=\frac{\mathcal{N}_{\mu}}{H^{d-1}}c_{\Delta}\Gamma\left(\Delta-\frac{d}{2}\pm i\mu\right)\;,

(265)

where

c_{\Delta}=\frac{2^{-2\Delta+d+1}\pi^{\frac{d+1}{2}}}{\Gamma(\Delta)\Gamma\left(\Delta-\frac{d}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\right)}\;.

(266)

Evaluating the spectral integral.

Now, let us turn to the evaluation of the spectral integral (
79
). To this aim, it is convenient to split the Wightman function into two EAdS bulk-to-bulk propagators
Sleight:2021plv
;
DiPietro:2021sjt
:

W_{\mu}(\sigma^{E})=\frac{1}{2i\sinh(\pi\mu)}\left(\Pi^{\mathrm{EAdS}}_{-\mu}(\sigma^{E})-\Pi^{\mathrm{EAdS}}_{\mu}(\sigma^{E})\right)\;,

(267)

where
\Pi^{\mathrm{EAdS}}_{\mu}
is defined as:

\displaystyle\Pi^{\mathrm{EAdS}}_{\mu}(\sigma^{E})

\displaystyle\equiv H^{d-1}(z_{1}z_{2})^{\frac{d}{2}}\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{d}\bm{k}}{(2\pi)^{d}}e^{-i\bm{k}\cdot(\bm{x}_{1}-\bm{x}_{2})}I_{i\mu}(kz_{1})K_{i\mu}(kz_{2})

(268)

\displaystyle=\frac{H^{d-1}}{2\pi^{\frac{d}{2}}}\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}+i\mu\right)}{\Gamma(1+i\mu)}\frac{1}{(2|\sigma^{E}|-2)^{\frac{d}{2}+i\mu}}\;_{2}F_{1}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\frac{d}{2}+i\mu,&\frac{1}{2}+i\mu\\
1+2i\mu\end{array};\frac{2}{1+\sigma^{E}}\right)\;.

This decays exponentially in the
\mu
lower half-plane and it only has poles in the upper half-plane. It is equivalent to what is done in appendix
B
where we performed the spectral integral before the momentum one. After this step, the CFT two-point function becomes

\displaystyle\braket{\mathcal{O}(X_{1}^{E})\mathcal{O}(X_{2}^{E})}

\displaystyle=c_{\Delta}H^{-(d-1)}\int\mathrm{d}\mu\,\mathcal{N}_{\mu}\,\Gamma\left(\Delta-\frac{d}{2}\pm i\mu\right)W_{\mu}(\sigma^{E})

(269)

\displaystyle=\frac{ic_{\Delta}}{\pi H^{d-1}}\int\mathrm{d}\mu\;\mu\;\Gamma\left(\Delta-\frac{d}{2}\pm i\mu\right)\Pi^{\mathrm{EAdS}}_{\mu}(\sigma^{E})\;.

Closing the contour in the lower half-plane and collecting the residues attached to the poles of the Gamma function yields the following expression for the two-point function:

\displaystyle\braket{\mathcal{O}(X_{1}^{E})\mathcal{O}(X_{2}^{E})}=\frac{1}{2^{\Delta}(1-\sigma^{E})^{\Delta}}

(270)

\displaystyle=\frac{2c_{\Delta}}{H^{d-1}}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{n}}{n!}\left(\Delta-\frac{d}{2}+n\right)\Gamma\left(2\Delta-d+n\right)\Pi^{\mathrm{EAdS}}_{-i\left(\Delta-\frac{d}{2}+n\right)}(\sigma^{E})\;.

As explained in section
3.4
, this expansion is valid for any value of the dimension
\Delta
. However, for
\Delta<\frac{d}{2}
, the spectral integral (
269
) no longer coincides with the series of residues (
270
). In that case, the integral is given by a mixture of the poles
\mu_{\pm}^{n}
:

\displaystyle\frac{c_{\Delta}}{H^{d-1}}\int\mathrm{d}\mu\,\mathcal{N}_{\mu}\Gamma\left(\Delta-\frac{d}{2}\pm i\mu\right)W_{\mu}(\sigma^{E})

(271)

\displaystyle=\frac{2c_{\Delta}}{H^{d-1}}\sum_{n=0}^{\lfloor\frac{d}{2}-\Delta\rfloor}\frac{(-1)^{n}}{n!}(\Delta-\frac{d}{2}+n)\Gamma\left(2\Delta-d+n\right)\Pi^{\mathrm{EAdS}}_{+i\left(\Delta-\frac{d}{2}+n\right)}(\sigma^{E})

\displaystyle+\frac{2c_{\Delta}}{H^{d-1}}\sum_{n=\lceil\frac{d}{2}-\Delta\rceil}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{n}}{n!}(\Delta-\frac{d}{2}+n)\Gamma\left(2\Delta-d+n\right)\Pi^{\mathrm{EAdS}}_{-i\left(\Delta-\frac{d}{2}+n\right)}(\sigma^{E})\;.

Using the connection formula (
267
), one finds:

\displaystyle\braket{\mathcal{O}(X_{1}^{E})\mathcal{O}(X_{2}^{E})}=\frac{c_{\Delta}}{H^{d-1}}\int\mathrm{d}\mu\mathcal{N}_{\mu}\Gamma\left(\Delta-\frac{d}{2}\pm i\mu\right)W_{\mu}(\sigma^{E})

(272)

\displaystyle+\frac{4\pi c_{\Delta}}{H^{d-1}}\sum_{n=0}^{\lfloor\frac{d}{2}-\Delta\rfloor}\frac{(-1)^{n}}{n!}\mathcal{N}_{i\left(\Delta-\frac{d}{2}+n\right)}\Gamma\left(2\Delta-d+n\right)W_{i\left(\Delta-\frac{d}{2}+n\right)}(\sigma^{E})

\displaystyle=\int\mathrm{d}\mu\,\rho_{\mathcal{O}}^{\mathcal{P}}(\mu)W_{\mu}(\sigma^{E})+4i\pi\sum_{n=0}^{\lfloor\frac{d}{2}-\Delta\rfloor}\textrm{Res}\left(\rho_{\mathcal{O}}^{\mathcal{P}}(\mu)W_{\mu}(\sigma^{E}),i\left(\Delta-\frac{d}{2}+n\right)\right)\;,

which is the result (
81
).

Appendix D
Special Functions

Here we collect some definitions and mathematical identities involving special functions, in particular in view of the computation of the four-point diagram in subsection
5.2
.

\Gamma
-function.

The
\Gamma
-function, defined as

\Gamma(z)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}t\,e^{-t}t^{z-1}\quad\,,\quad\mathrm{Re}(z)>0\,,

(273)

can be analytically continued to
\mathbb{C}\setminus\{-p,p\in\mathbb{N}\}
using the functional relation

\Gamma(z+1)=z\,\Gamma(z)\;.

(274)

The Gamma function is meromorphic, with poles located at negative integers
-p\,,\,p\in\mathbb{N}
. The corresponding residue is

\mathrm{Res}\left(\Gamma(z)\,,\,z=-p\right)=\frac{(-1)^{p}}{p!}\,.

(275)

Other useful functional relations are the reflection formula and the duplication formula:

\Gamma(z)\Gamma(1-z)=\frac{\pi}{\sin(\pi z)}\,,

(276)

\Gamma(2z)=\frac{2^{2z-1}}{\sqrt{\pi}}\Gamma\left(z+\frac{1}{2}\right)\Gamma(z)\,,

(277)

To avoid clutter, we define the following notation:

\Gamma(a\pm b)\equiv\Gamma(a+b)\Gamma(a-b)\;.

(278)

Hypergeometric function.

The Gauss hypergeometric function is defined by the series

{}_{2}F_{1}\left(\begin{matrix}a\,,\,b\\
c\end{matrix}\,,z\right)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{\left(a\right)_{n}\left(b\right)_{n}}{\left(c\right)_{n}}\frac{z^{n}}{n!}\;,

(279)

where the Pochhammer symbol is

\left(a\right)_{n}\equiv\frac{\Gamma(a+n)}{\Gamma(a)}\,.

(280)

The series (
279
) is convergent for
|z|<1
.
z\mapsto\,_{2}F_{1}\left(a,b;c;z\right)
can be analytically continued in
\mathbb{C}\setminus\left[1;+\infty\right]
. Also, due to
\Gamma
-function factors,
a,b\mapsto\,_{2}F_{1}\left(a,b;c;z\right)
is analytic and
c\mapsto\,_{2}F_{1}\left(a,b;c;z\right)
is meromorphic with poles at
c=-p\,,\,p\in\mathbb{N}
.

Associated Legendre functions: definitions.

The associated Legendre function of the first kind is defined by

P_{i\mu-\frac{1}{2}}^{-\frac{d-3}{2}}(x)=\frac{1}{\Gamma\left(\frac{d-1}{2}\right)}\left(\frac{x-1}{x+1}\right)^{\frac{d-3}{4}}\,_{2}F_{1}\left(\begin{matrix}\frac{1}{2}+i\mu\,,\,\frac{1}{2}-i\mu\\
\frac{d-1}{2}\end{matrix}\,;\frac{1-x}{2}\right)\quad,\quad x>1\,.

(281)

It is related to the Gegenbauer function
C_{i\mu-\frac{d-2}{2}}^{\left(\frac{d-2}{2}\right)}
as:

P_{i\mu-\frac{1}{2}}^{-\frac{d-3}{2}}(x)=\frac{\Gamma(d-2)\Gamma\left(\frac{4-d}{2}+i\mu\right)}{2^{\frac{d-3}{2}}\Gamma\left(\frac{d-2}{2}+i\mu\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{d-1}{2}\right)}\left(x^{2}-1\right)^{\frac{d-3}{4}}C_{i\mu-\frac{d-2}{2}}^{\left(\frac{d-2}{2}\right)}(x)\,,\quad x>1.

(282)

The associated Legendre of function of the second kind is defined by

Q_{i\mu-\frac{1}{2}}^{\frac{d-3}{2}}(x)=\frac{e^{i\pi\frac{d+1}{2}}\sqrt{\pi}\left(x^{2}-1\right)^{\frac{d-3}{4}}}{x^{\frac{d-3}{2}}(2x)^{i\mu+\frac{1}{2}}}\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{d-2}{2}+i\mu\right)}{\Gamma\left(1+i\mu\right)}\,_{2}F_{1}\left(\begin{matrix}\frac{d}{4}+\frac{i\mu}{2}\,,\,\frac{d-2}{4}+\frac{i\mu}{2}\\
1+i\mu\end{matrix}\,,\frac{1}{x^{2}}\right)\,,x>1\,.

(283)

Asymptotic behaviour.

The associated Legendre functions
P_{i\mu-\frac{1}{2}}^{-\frac{d-3}{2}}
and
Q_{\pm i\mu-\frac{1}{2}}^{\frac{d-3}{2}}
have the following large-
\mu
asymptotic behaviour:

P_{i\mu-\frac{1}{2}}^{-\frac{d-3}{2}}(\cosh(\xi))\underset{\mu\rightarrow+\infty}{=}\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi\sinh(\xi)}}\frac{1}{\mu^{\frac{d}{2}-1}}\sin\left(\mu\,\xi+\frac{\pi}{4}\left(4-d\right)\right)\left(1+O\left(\frac{1}{\mu}\right)\right)\,.

(284)

Q_{\pm i\mu-\frac{1}{2}}^{\frac{d-3}{2}}(\cosh(\xi))\underset{\mu\rightarrow+\infty}{=}e^{i\frac{\pi}{2}(d+1)}\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{2\sinh(\xi)}}\left(\pm i\mu\right)^{\frac{d}{2}-2}e^{\mp i\mu\xi}\left(1+O\left(\frac{1}{\mu}\right)\right)\;,\quad\xi>0\,.

(285)

Connection formulas.

The connection formula between the two associated Legendre functions is given by

P_{i\mu-\frac{1}{2}}^{-\frac{d-3}{2}}(x)=\frac{ie^{-i\pi\left(\frac{d-3}{2}\right)}}{\sinh(\pi\mu)\Gamma\left(\frac{d-2}{2}\pm i\mu\right)}\left(Q_{i\mu-\frac{1}{2}}^{\frac{d-3}{2}}(x)-Q_{-i\mu-\frac{1}{2}}^{\frac{d-3}{2}}(x)\right)\,,\quad x>1\,.

(286)

From (
282
), this connection formula can be expressed in terms of the Gegenbauer function:

C_{i\mu-\frac{d-2}{2}}^{\left(\frac{d-2}{2}\right)}(x)=\frac{e^{-i\pi\left(\frac{d-1}{2}\right)}\sinh\left(\pi\mu+i\pi\frac{d}{2}\right)}{\sqrt{\pi}\,2^{\frac{d-3}{2}}\Gamma\left(\frac{d-2}{2}\right)\sinh(\pi\mu)}\left(x^{2}-1\right)^{\frac{3-d}{4}}\left(Q_{i\mu-\frac{1}{2}}^{\frac{d-3}{2}}(x)-Q_{-i\mu-\frac{1}{2}}^{\frac{d-3}{2}}(x)\right)\,,\quad x>1\,.

(287)

Analytic continuation formulas.

The analytic continuation formula for the associated Legendre function of the first kind at
z=e^{+i\pi}x\,,\,x>1
, is

P_{i\mu-\frac{1}{2}}^{-\frac{d-3}{2}}(e^{+i\pi}x)=\frac{e^{-i\pi\frac{d-3}{2}}}{\sinh(\pi\mu)\Gamma\left(\frac{d-2}{2}\pm i\mu\right)}\left(e^{\pi\mu}Q_{i\mu-\frac{1}{2}}^{\frac{d-3}{2}}(x)-e^{-\pi\mu}Q_{-i\mu-\frac{1}{2}}^{\frac{d-3}{2}}(x)\right)\,.

(288)

Appendix E
Three-Point Vertex Function

The three-point vertex function is defined as the triple-
K
integral (
139
). It can be evaluated by mean of the Appell
F_{4}
function
gradshteyn2007
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}^{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\mu_{3}}_{\bm{k}_{1}\bm{k}_{2}\bm{k}_{3}}=\frac{2^{\frac{d}{2}-4}}{k_{3}^{\frac{d}{2}}}\Bigg(A^{\mu_{1},\mu_{2}}_{\mu_{3}}\left(\frac{k_{1}}{k_{3}},\frac{k_{2}}{k_{3}}\right)+A^{\mu_{1},-\mu_{2}}_{\mu_{3}}\left(\frac{k_{1}}{k_{3}},\frac{k_{2}}{k_{3}}\right)+A^{-\mu_{1},\mu_{2}}_{\mu_{3}}\left(\frac{k_{1}}{k_{3}},\frac{k_{2}}{k_{3}}\right)

(289)

\displaystyle+A^{-\mu_{1},-\mu_{2}}_{\mu_{3}}\left(\frac{k_{1}}{k_{3}},\frac{k_{2}}{k_{3}}\right)\Bigg)\;,

where we defined
A^{\mu_{1},\mu_{2}}_{\mu_{3}}(u,v)
in terms of the regularised Appell
F_{4}
function:

\displaystyle A^{\mu_{1},\mu_{2}}_{\mu_{3}}(u,v)=-\frac{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}u^{i\mu_{1}}v^{i\mu_{2}}}{\mathcal{N}_{\mu_{1}}\mathcal{N}_{\mu_{2}}}\Gamma\left(\frac{\frac{d}{2}+i\mu_{1}+i\mu_{2}\pm i\mu_{3}}{2}\right)

(290)

\displaystyle\times\bm{F}_{4}\left(\begin{array}[]{crc}\frac{\frac{d}{2}+i\mu_{1}+i\mu_{2}+i\mu_{3}}{2}&,&\frac{\frac{d}{2}+i\mu_{1}+i\mu_{2}-i\mu_{3}}{2}\\
1+i\mu_{1}&,&1+i\mu_{2}\end{array};u^{2},v^{2}\right)\;.

The regularised Appell
F_{4}
function admits the following series expansion:

\bm{F}_{4}(\alpha,\beta;\gamma,\gamma^{\prime};u^{2},v^{2})=\frac{1}{\Gamma(\gamma)\Gamma(\gamma^{\prime})}\sum_{n,m=0}^{\infty}\frac{(\alpha)_{n+m}(\beta)_{m+n}}{(\gamma)_{n}(\gamma^{\prime})_{m}n!m!}u^{2n}v^{2m}\;,\quad\textrm{for}\quad|u|+|v|<1\;.

(291)

This is analytic in all the parameters. Therefore, the only singularities in (
290
) come from the
\Gamma
factors. The spurious poles coming from the
1/\mathcal{N}_{\mu}
are cancelled by some zeros of the
F_{4}
function.

The physical domain of the vertex function is
\bm{k}_{1}+\bm{k}_{2}+\bm{k}_{3}=0
, for which the triangle inequality imposes
k_{3}\leq k_{1}+k_{2}
. In Eq. (
289
), we thus need to evaluate
F_{4}
for
u\equiv k_{1}/k_{3}
and
v\equiv k_{2}/k_{3}
such that
u+v\geq 1
, i.e. by analytically continuing
F_{4}
outside the domain in (
291
).

Appendix F
Details of the Perturbative Computations

F.1
Four-Point Function

Here we give some details about the computation of the four-point diagram in subsection
5.2
.

Background residues.

To simplify the sum of background residues (
177
) and (
178
), we can use the connection formula (
286
) with
\mu=-i(n+1)\,,\,n\in\mathbb{N}
:

\displaystyle Q_{n+\frac{1}{2}}^{\frac{d-3}{2}}\left(x\right)-Q_{-n-\frac{3}{2}}^{\frac{d-3}{2}}\left(x\right)

\displaystyle=+e^{-i\pi\frac{d-3}{2}}\Gamma\left(\frac{d-4}{2}-n\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}+n\right)

(292)

\displaystyle\times\cos\left(\pi\left(n+\frac{1}{2}\right)\right)P_{n+\frac{1}{2}}^{-\frac{d-3}{2}}\left(x\right)\,.

When
d
is not an even integer, each term in the above right-hand side is finite, and since
\cos(\pi(n+\frac{1}{2}))=0
,

Q_{n+\frac{1}{2}}^{\frac{d-3}{2}}\left(x\right)-Q_{-n-\frac{3}{2}}^{\frac{d-3}{2}}\left(x\right)=0\quad,\quad d\neq 2k\,k\in\mathbb{N}\,.

(293)

Then, the sum of (
177
) and (
178
) vanishes and the background residues are simply given by (
179
).

When
d
is an even integer, the term
\Gamma\left(\frac{d-4}{2}-n\right)
in (
292
) becomes infinite for
n\geq K-1
, with

K\equiv\frac{d-2}{2}\,,\,K\in\mathbb{N}\,.

(294)

However, we still have
\cos(\pi(n+\frac{1}{2}))=0
. Then, let us see if the connection formula (
286
) can be analytically continued in
\mu=-i(n+1)\,,\,n\geq K-1
, when
d
is even. Let write (
286
) for
\mu=-i(n+1+\epsilon)
, with
\epsilon>0
:

\displaystyle Q_{n+\epsilon+\frac{1}{2}}^{\frac{d-3}{2}}\left(x\right)-Q_{-n-\epsilon-\frac{3}{2}}^{\frac{d-3}{2}}\left(x\right)

\displaystyle=e^{-i\pi\frac{d-3}{2}}\Gamma\left(\frac{d-4}{2}-n-\epsilon\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}+n+\epsilon\right)

(295)

\displaystyle\times\cos\left(\pi\left(n+\epsilon+\frac{1}{2}\right)\right)P_{n+\epsilon+\frac{1}{2}}^{-\frac{d-3}{2}}\left(x\right)\,.

Using the asymptotic expansions for small
\varepsilon>0
(in particular for the
\Gamma
-function from the residue expression (
275
)),

\cos\left(\pi\left(n+\epsilon+\frac{1}{2}\right)\right)\underset{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}{=}(-1)^{n+1}\pi\,\epsilon+O(\epsilon^{2})\,,

(296a)

\Gamma\left(\frac{d-4}{2}-n-\epsilon\right)=\Gamma\left(K-1-n-\epsilon\right)\underset{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}{=}\frac{(-1)^{n-K}}{\Gamma\left(n+2-K\right)}\frac{1}{\epsilon}+O(1)\,,

(296b)

and the continuity of
\mu\mapsto Q_{\mu}^{\nu}(x)
, the connection formula can be analytically continued by sending
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
:

\displaystyle Q_{n+\frac{1}{2}}^{\frac{d-3}{2}}\left(x\right)-Q_{-n-\frac{3}{2}}^{\frac{d-3}{2}}\left(x\right)

\displaystyle=-i\pi\,\frac{\Gamma\left(n+1+K\right)}{\Gamma\left(n+2-K\right)}\,P_{n+\frac{1}{2}}^{-\frac{d-3}{2}}\left(x\right)\;,\;n\geq K-1\,.

(297)

This last formula can be used to simplify the sum of the background residues to find (
180
).

Analytical Properties of KLF integrands

Analytic structure of
\mu\mapsto\frac{\mu}{\sinh(\pi\mu)}
.

The background terms (
177
), (
178
) in
\hat{F}_{++}
come from the function
\mu\mapsto\frac{\mu}{\sinh(\pi\mu)}
, which has poles at values
\mu=in\,,\,n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}
, with residues:

\mathrm{Res}\left(\mu\mapsto\frac{\mu}{\sinh(\pi\mu)}\,;\,\mu=in\,,\,n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}\right)=\frac{(-1)^{n}in}{\pi}\,.

(298)

Analytic structure of
\mu\mapsto Q_{\pm i\mu-\frac{1}{2}}^{\frac{d-3}{2}}(x)
.

The associated Legendre functions
Q_{\pm i\mu-\frac{1}{2}}^{\frac{d-3}{2}}
(
283
) that appear in the integrands of
\hat{F}_{++}^{0}
and
\hat{F}_{++}^{1}
have a different analytic structure in the complex
\mu
plane depending on
d
.

When
d
is any complex number that is not an even integer, the singularities only come from the
\Gamma(\frac{d-2}{2}+i\mu)
factor. Indeed, even if the Gauss hypergeometric function (
279
) is singular for
c=-p\,,\,p\in\mathbb{N}
, the dressed hypergeometric function

\,{}_{2}\mathcal{F}_{1}\left(\begin{matrix}a\,,\,b\\
c\end{matrix}\,,z\right)\equiv\frac{1}{\Gamma(c)}\,_{2}F_{1}\left(\begin{matrix}a\,,\,b\\
c\end{matrix}\,,z\right)\,

(299)

that enters the expression of
Q_{\pm i\mu-\frac{1}{2}}
is analytic in
c
.
Then, from the residues of the
\Gamma
-function, the residues of
\mu\mapsto Q_{\pm i\mu-\frac{1}{2}}^{\frac{d-3}{2}}
are

\displaystyle\mathrm{Res}\left(\mu\mapsto Q_{\pm i\mu-\frac{1}{2}}^{\frac{d-3}{2}}(x)\,;\,\mu=\pm i\left(n+\frac{d-2}{2}\right)\,,\,n\in\mathbb{N}\right)

(300)

\displaystyle=-i\frac{e^{i\pi\frac{d-3}{2}}\sqrt{\pi}\left(x^{2}-1\right)^{\frac{d-3}{4}}}{2^{-\frac{d-3}{2}}(2x)^{-n}}\frac{(-1)^{n}}{n!}\frac{1}{\Gamma\left(-n-\frac{d-4}{2}\right)}\,_{2}F_{1}\left(\begin{matrix}\frac{1-n}{2}\,,\,-\frac{n}{2}\\
-n-\frac{d-4}{2}\end{matrix}\,,\frac{1}{x^{2}}\right)\,,

When
d
is even,
\mu\mapsto Q_{\pm i\mu-\frac{1}{2}}^{\frac{d-3}{2}}(x)
is analytic in the whole complex plane. This is because each singularity coming from the
\Gamma(\frac{d-2}{2}+i\mu)
factor is cancelled at the same point by the zero of the dressed hypergeometric function (
299
) that enters (
283
). Let us see this explicitly for
\mu\mapsto Q_{i\mu-\frac{1}{2}}^{\frac{d-3}{2}}(x)
. First, it can be shown that for
m\in\mathbb{N}
,

\lim_{c\rightarrow-n}\,{}_{2}\mathcal{F}_{1}\left(\begin{matrix}a\,,\,b\\
c\end{matrix}\,,z\right)=\frac{\left(a\right)_{n+1}\left(b\right)_{n+1}}{\left(n+1\right)!}\,x^{n+1}\,_{2}F_{1}\left(\begin{matrix}a+n+1\,,\,b+n+1\\
n+2\end{matrix}\,,z\right)\,.

(301)

Then, because the poles of
\mu\mapsto\Gamma\left(K+i\mu\right)
are located at
\mu=i(n+K)
, we look at the following limit:

\displaystyle\frac{\Gamma\left(K+i\mu\right)}{\Gamma\left(1+i\mu\right)}

\displaystyle{}_{2}F_{1}\left(\begin{matrix}\frac{K+1+i\mu}{2}\,,\,\frac{K+i\mu}{2}\\
1+i\mu\end{matrix}\,,\frac{1}{x^{2}}\right)

(302)

\displaystyle\underset{\mu\rightarrow i(n+K)}{\sim}

\displaystyle\frac{\Gamma\left(K+i\mu\right)}{\Gamma\left(\frac{K+1+i\mu}{2}\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{K+i\mu}{2}\right)}\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{K+1+i\mu}{2}+n+K\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{K+i\mu}{2}+n+K\right)}{\left(n+K\right)!}

\displaystyle\times x^{-2(n+K)}\,_{2}F_{1}\left(\begin{matrix}\frac{K+1+i\mu}{2}+n+K\,,\,\frac{K+i\mu}{2}+n+K\\
n+K+1\end{matrix}\,,\frac{1}{x^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\underset{\mu\rightarrow i(n+K)}{\sim}

\displaystyle\frac{2^{-(n+1)}}{\sqrt{\pi}}\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{K+1+i\mu}{2}+n+K\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{K+i\mu}{2}+n+K\right)}{\left(n+K\right)!}

\displaystyle\times x^{-2(n+K)}\,_{2}F_{1}\left(\begin{matrix}\frac{K+1+i\mu}{2}+n+K\,,\,\frac{K+i\mu}{2}+n+K\\
n+K+1\end{matrix}\,,\frac{1}{x^{2}}\right)\,,

where we used the duplication formula (
277
) in the last line. The last term is finite in the limit
\mu\rightarrow i(n+K)
, therefore
\mu\mapsto Q_{i\mu-\frac{1}{2}}^{\frac{d-3}{2}}
is analytic when
d
is even (a similar derivation can be done for
\mu\mapsto Q_{-i\mu-\frac{d-2}{2}}^{\frac{1}{2}}
).

Analytic structure of
\mu\mapsto\frac{1}{\left(\mu^{2}-\mu_{\chi}^{2}\right)_{i\varepsilon}}
.

From the
i\epsilon
-prescription expression (
118
) and as shown in Fig.
5
, poles of
\mu\mapsto\frac{1}{\left(\mu^{2}-\mu_{\chi}^{2}\right)_{i\varepsilon}}
are located at
\mu=\pm\mu_{\chi}\pm i\epsilon
. Their residues are the following:

\displaystyle\mathrm{Res}\left(\mu\mapsto\frac{1}{\left(\mu^{2}-\mu_{\chi}^{2}\right)_{i\epsilon}}\,;\,\mu=\pm\mu_{\chi}-i\epsilon\right)=\frac{e^{\pm\pi\mu_{\chi}}}{4\mu_{\chi}\sinh(\pi\mu_{\chi})}\,,

(303)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Res}\left(\mu\mapsto\frac{1}{\left(\mu^{2}-\mu_{\chi}^{2}\right)_{i\epsilon}}\,;\,\mu=\pm\mu_{\chi}+i\epsilon\right)=-\frac{e^{\mp\pi\mu_{\chi}}}{4\mu_{\chi}\sinh(\pi\mu_{\chi})}\,.

F.2
Loop Diagram

In this section, we fill two gaps from section (
5.3
) about the one-loop correction to the scalar propagator.

Reduction of the number of spectral integrations.

First, let us show how the double spectral integral (
202
) can be turned into the single spectral integral (
203
). Let us consider the product of two Euclidean position-space propagators. Using (
237
), this can be written as

\mathcal{C}_{{\sf{a}}{\sf{b}}}^{E,\chi_{1}}(\sigma^{E})\,\mathcal{C}_{{\sf{a}}{\sf{b}}}^{E,\chi_{2}}(\sigma^{E})=\int\displaylimits^{\infty}_{-\infty}\mathrm{d}\mu\mathcal{N}_{\mu}\mathrm{d}\mu^{\prime}\mathcal{N}_{\mu^{\prime}}W_{\mu}(\sigma^{E})W_{\mu^{\prime}}(\sigma^{E})\mathcal{G}^{\chi_{1}}_{{\sf{a}}{\sf{b}}}(\mu)\mathcal{G}^{\chi_{1}}_{{\sf{a}}{\sf{b}}}(\mu^{\prime})\;.

(304)

Using the property of the spectral density (
197
), this reads

\mathcal{C}_{{\sf{a}}{\sf{b}}}^{E,\chi_{1}}(\sigma^{E})\,\mathcal{C}_{{\sf{a}}{\sf{b}}}^{E,\chi_{2}}(\sigma^{E})=\int\displaylimits^{\infty}_{-\infty}\mathrm{d}\nu W_{\nu}(\sigma^{E})\int\displaylimits^{\infty}_{-\infty}\mathrm{d}\mu\mathcal{N}_{\mu}\mathrm{d}\mu^{\prime}\mathcal{N}_{\mu^{\prime}}\rho_{\mu\mu^{\prime}}(\nu)\mathcal{G}^{\chi_{1}}_{{\sf{a}}{\sf{b}}}(\mu)\mathcal{G}^{\chi_{1}}_{{\sf{a}}{\sf{b}}}(\mu^{\prime})\;.

(305)

At the same time, the Euclidean position-space propagators are simply expressed in terms of analytic continuations of
W_{\mu}
, see Eq. (
255
). Considering the same continuations, the property (
197
) thus implies that

\mathcal{C}_{{\sf{a}}{\sf{b}}}^{E,\chi_{1}}(\sigma^{E})\,\mathcal{C}_{{\sf{a}}{\sf{b}}}^{E,\chi_{2}}(\sigma^{E})=\int\displaylimits^{\infty}_{-\infty}\mathrm{d}\mu\rho_{\chi_{1}\chi_{2}}(\mu)\mathcal{C}^{E,\mu}_{{\sf{a}}{\sf{b}}}(\sigma^{E})\,.

(306)

Using again (
237
), this gives

\mathcal{C}_{{\sf{a}}{\sf{b}}}^{E,\chi_{1}}(\sigma^{E})\,\mathcal{C}_{{\sf{a}}{\sf{b}}}^{E,\chi_{2}}(\sigma^{E})=\int\displaylimits^{\infty}_{-\infty}\mathrm{d}\nu\mathcal{N}_{\nu}W_{\nu}(\sigma^{E})\int\displaylimits^{\infty}_{-\infty}\mathrm{d}\mu\rho_{\chi_{1}\chi_{2}}(\mu)\mathcal{G}_{{\sf{a}}{\sf{b}}}^{\mu}(\nu)\;.

(307)

We now use that
\mathcal{N}_{\nu}W_{\nu}(\sigma^{E})
form a basis of square integrable bi-local functions in EAdS and compare the expressions (
305
) and (
307
) to deduce that

\int\displaylimits^{\infty}_{-\infty}\mathrm{d}\mu\mathcal{N}_{\mu}\mathrm{d}\mu^{\prime}\mathcal{N}_{\mu^{\prime}}\rho_{\mu\mu^{\prime}}(\nu)\mathcal{G}^{\chi_{1}}_{{\sf{a}}{\sf{b}}}(\mu)\mathcal{G}^{\chi_{1}}_{{\sf{a}}{\sf{b}}}(\mu^{\prime})=\mathcal{N}_{\nu}\int\displaylimits^{\infty}_{-\infty}\mathrm{d}\mu\rho_{\chi_{1}\chi_{2}}(\mu)\mathcal{G}_{{\sf{a}}{\sf{b}}}^{\mu}(\nu)\;,

(308)

which is the desired expression.

Summation of the four
{\sf{b}}_{1}{\sf{b}}_{2}
contributions.

Let us explain how to make the final sum over the internal vertices in expression (
205
). To this aim, we consider an alternative theory with a quadratic mixing between the external field
\varphi
and a massive field
\chi
of mass
m_{\chi}=HM_{\mu}
:

\mathcal{L}_{\textrm{mix}}=-g\varphi\chi\;.

(309)

According to the KLF space Feynman rules (
144
), this generates a vertex of the form:

\hbox to121.71pt{\vbox to21.87pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 60.85645pt\lower-7.77745pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

\lxSVG@closescope {}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}=-i{\sf{a}}ge^{\frac{i{\sf{a}}\pi d}{2}}\delta\left({}^{\mu_{1}\;\mu_{2}}_{\bm{k}_{1}-\bm{k}_{2}}\right)\;.

(310)

Therefore, at first order in
g
, one can write the following KLF space mixed two-point function:

\displaystyle\braket{\Omega|\varphi^{\mu_{1}}_{\bm{k}_{1}}\chi^{\mu_{2}}_{\bm{k}_{2}}|\Omega}_{{\sf{a}}_{1}{\sf{b}}_{2}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{{\sf{b}}_{1}=\pm}\hbox to127.4pt{\vbox to31.09pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 63.70146pt\lower-14.9939pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{23.62636pt}{5.81369pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope }}}

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0.1211,0.4648,0.707}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{11.09793pt}{-9.71646pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope {}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

(311)

\displaystyle=-ig\delta\left({}^{\mu_{1}\;\mu_{2}}_{\bm{k}_{1}-\bm{k}_{2}}\right)\sum_{{\sf{b}}_{1}=\pm}{\sf{b}}_{1}e^{\frac{i{\sf{b}}_{1}\pi d}{2}}\frac{\mathcal{G}^{\mu_{\varphi}}_{{\sf{a}}_{1}{\sf{b}}_{1}}(\mu_{1})}{H^{2}}\frac{\mathcal{G}^{\mu}_{{\sf{b}}_{1}{\sf{b}}_{2}}(\mu_{1})}{H^{2}}\;.

As noted in
Higuchi:2010xt
, the presence of the mixing (
309
) in the interacting sector is an artefact of the choice of field variables. Indeed, if one consider a free theory with fields
\Psi_{1}
and
\Psi_{2}
of masses
m_{1}
and
m_{2}
, it can be mapped to our
\varphi-\chi
lagrangian by an
\mathrm{SO}(2)
rotation in field space:

\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\varphi\\
\chi\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\cos(\omega)&-\sin(\omega)\\
\sin(\omega)&\cos(\omega)\end{array}\right)\cdot\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\Psi_{1}\\
\Psi_{2}\end{array}\right)\;.

(312)

The parameters in the initial Lagrangian are then related to
m_{1,2}
and
\omega
as:

\displaystyle m_{\varphi}^{2}

\displaystyle=m_{1}^{2}\cos^{2}(\omega)+m_{2}^{2}\sin^{2}(\omega)\;,

(313)

\displaystyle m_{\chi}^{2}

\displaystyle=m_{1}^{2}\sin^{2}(\omega)+m_{2}^{2}\cos^{2}(\omega)\;,

\displaystyle g

\displaystyle=(m_{1}^{2}-m_{2}^{2})\sin(\omega)\cos(\omega)\;.

Using the rotation (
312
), the mixed two-point function can be written as a combination of free propagators:

\braket{\Omega|\varphi^{\mu_{1}}_{\bm{k}_{1}}\chi^{\mu_{2}}_{\bm{k}_{2}}|\Omega}_{{\sf{a}}_{1}{\sf{b}}_{2}}=\frac{g}{m_{1}^{2}-m_{2}^{2}}\left(\braket{\Omega|\Psi^{\mu_{1}}_{1,\bm{k}_{1}}\Psi^{\mu_{2}}_{1,\bm{k}_{2}}|\Omega}_{{\sf{a}}_{1}{\sf{b}}_{2}}-\braket{\Omega|\Psi^{\mu_{1}}_{2,\bm{k}_{1}}\Psi^{\mu_{2}}_{2,\bm{k}_{2}}|\Omega}_{{\sf{a}}_{1}{\sf{b}}_{2}}\right)\;,

(314)

where we used the fact that the two-point functions between
\Psi_{1}
and
\Psi_{2}
vanishes. As the two terms in (
314
) are simply given by free KLF propagators, one obtains:

\braket{\Omega|\varphi^{\mu_{1}}_{\bm{k}_{1}}\chi^{\mu_{2}}_{\bm{k}_{2}}|\Omega}_{{\sf{a}}_{1}{\sf{b}}_{2}}=g\delta\left({}^{\mu_{1}\;\mu_{2}}_{\bm{k}_{1}-\bm{k}_{2}}\right)\frac{\mathcal{G}^{\Psi_{1}}_{{\sf{a}}_{1}{\sf{b}}_{2}}(\mu_{1})-\mathcal{G}^{\Psi_{2}}_{{\sf{a}}_{1}{\sf{b}}_{2}}(\mu_{1})}{H^{2}(m_{1}^{2}-m_{2}^{2})}\;.

(315)

Now, comparing Eqs (
311
) and (
315
) consistently at order
g
, one can identify that:

-i\sum_{{\sf{b}}_{1}=\pm}{\sf{b}}_{1}e^{\frac{i{\sf{b}}_{1}\pi d}{2}}\mathcal{G}^{\mu_{\varphi}}_{{\sf{a}}_{1}{\sf{b}}_{1}}(\mu_{1})\mathcal{G}^{\mu}_{{\sf{b}}_{1}{\sf{b}}_{2}}(\mu_{1})=-\frac{\mathcal{G}^{\mu}_{{\sf{a}}_{1}{\sf{b}}_{2}}(\mu_{1})}{\mu_{\varphi}^{2}-\mu^{2}}\;,

(316)

where we discarded the second term as it does not contribute for principal series fields
Higuchi:2010xt
. Using twice Eq. (
316
), the sum in (
205
) therefore simplifies to:

-g^{2}\sum_{{\sf{b}}_{1},{\sf{b}}_{2}}{\sf{b}}_{1}{\sf{b}}_{2}e^{\frac{i\pi({\sf{b}}_{1}+{\sf{b}}_{2})d}{2}}\frac{\mathcal{G}^{\mu_{\varphi}}_{{\sf{a}}_{1}{\sf{b}}_{1}}(\mu_{1})}{H^{2}}\frac{\mathcal{G}^{\mu}_{{\sf{b}}_{1}{\sf{b}}_{2}}(\mu_{1})}{H^{2}}\frac{\mathcal{G}^{\mu_{\varphi}}_{{\sf{b}}_{2}{\sf{a}}_{2}}(\mu_{1})}{H^{2}}=\frac{g^{2}}{H^{6}}\frac{\mathcal{G}^{\mu}_{{\sf{a}}_{1}{\sf{a}}_{2}}(\mu_{1})}{(\mu_{\varphi}^{2}-\mu^{2})^{2}}\;.

(317)
